arXiv:2412.01489v2 [math.PR] 22 Jun 2026 
 
 
 One- and two-particle spectral gap identities for the symmetric inclusion process and related models
 
 
 
 Seonwoo Kim
 
 Address:  Department of Mathematics, Yonsei University, Seoul, Republic of Korea
 
 Email address:  seonwookim@yonsei.ac.kr 
 
  and  
 Federico Sau
 
 Address:  Dipartimento di Matematica “F. Enriques”, Università degli Studi di Milano, Milano, Italy
 
 Email address:  federico.sau@unimi.it 
 

 Abstract. 
    
 The symmetric inclusion process (SIP) models particles diffusing on a graph with mutual attraction. We recently showed  [ KS24 ]  that, in the log-concave regime (where diffusivity dominates interaction), the spectral gap of the conservative SIP matches that of a single particle. In this paper, our main result demonstrates that this identity generally fails outside this regime, but always holds for the non-conservative SIP, regardless of the interaction strength.
When this one-particle spectral gap identity breaks down, we derive sharp bounds for the gap in terms of diffusivity, and reveal a two-particle spectral gap identity in the vanishing diffusivity limit. Our approach leverages the rigid eigenstructure of SIP, refined comparisons of Dirichlet forms for arbitrary diffusivity and particle numbers, and techniques from slow–fast system analysis. These findings extend to the dual interacting diffusion known as Brownian energy process, and shed some light on the spectral gap behavior for related Dirichlet-reversible systems on general, non-mean-field, geometries. 
  
 
 
 Key words and phrases:  Spectral gap; interacting particle systems; Dirichlet distribution; symmetric inclusion process; Brownian energy process
 
 2020 Mathematics Subject Classification Primary 60K35; secondary 60J27, 05C50.
 
 
 1.  Introduction 

 
 The  symmetric inclusion process  ( SIP {\rm SIP} ) is a system of interacting particles hopping on the sites  x , y , z , … ∈ V x,y,z,\ldots\in V  of a finite graph  G G . In its conservative version, each particle jumps from a site  x x  to another one  y y  at rate  c x ​ y ​ ( α y + η y ) c_{xy}\left(\alpha_{y}+\eta_{y}\right) , where: 
 
 •  
 
 c x ​ y = c y ​ x ≥ 0 c_{xy}=c_{yx}\geq 0  is a symmetric weight attached to the edge  x ​ y xy ; 
 
 •  
 
 α y > 0 \alpha_{y}>0  is a weight attached to the arrival site  y y ; 
 
 •  
 
 η y ∈ ℕ 0 \eta_{y}\in\mathbb{N}_{0}  denotes the number of particles sitting on  y y . 
 
 
 While the rate  c x ​ y ​ α y c_{xy}\,\alpha_{y}  accounts for the free motion (or diffusion) of each particle,  c x ​ y ​ η y c_{xy}\,\eta_{y}  introduces an interaction among them by favoring jumps on top of already occupied sites. Moreover, this dynamics is conservative (i.e., it preserves the total number of particles), reversible with respect to a discrete Dirichlet distribution, and irreducible as soon as the underlying graph is connected.
We refer to Section  2  below for the detailed definition of the process and its properties. 
 
 
 For now, let us just remark that  SIP {\rm SIP}  arises within different contexts (e.g., as a discrete model of heat conduction  [ GKR07 ] , and as a multi-type Moran model from population genetics with non-mean-field interactions), and comes with closely related models from statistical physics. For instance, by setting  α y = 1 \alpha_{y}=1  and replacing the plus sign with a minus sign in the rates, one obtains the renowned symmetric exclusion process  [ Spi70 ] . Furthermore, SIP admits a continuous-spin counterpart, referred to as the Brownian energy process  [ GKRV09 ]  (see also Section  8.1  below). This continuous-spin model, roughly speaking, describes the energies of an instance of Kac’s walk  [ Kac56 ] , in which velocities evolve as interacting diffusions, rather than being instantaneously updated at random “collision” times. 
 
 
 In all these models, a central question is that of determining precise convergence rates to equilibrium, and, in the context of reversible processes, one of the most investigated quantities for this purpose is the so-called  spectral gap . 
 
 
 1.1.  One-particle spectral gap identity 

 
 For  SIP {\rm SIP}  on a graph  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})  endowed with site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} , 
 

 
 (1.1) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) > 0 {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)>0 
 
 
 denotes the corresponding spectral gap, i.e., the smallest non-zero eigenvalue of (the negative of) the infinitesimal generator (see Section  2  for the precise definition). In a previous work  [ KS24 ] , we established the following bounds on  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  in terms of  gap RW ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha) , the spectral gap of the random walk ( RW {\rm RW} ) on  G G  which jumps from  x x  to  y y  with rate  c x ​ y ​ α y c_{xy}\,\alpha_{y} : for all graphs  G G  and site weights  α \alpha , 
 

 
 (1.2) 
 
 ( 1 ∧ α min ) ​ gap RW ​ ( G , α ) ≤ gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) ≤ gap RW ​ ( G , α ) ​ . \left(1\wedge\alpha_{\rm min}\right){\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)\leq{\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)\leq{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here and all throughout,  a ∧ b ≔ min ⁡ { a , b } a\wedge b\coloneqq\min\{a,b\}  and  α min ≔ min x ∈ V ⁡ α x \alpha_{\rm min}\coloneqq\min_{x\in V}\alpha_{x} . 
 
 
 Notably, as soon as  α min ≥ 1 \alpha_{\rm min}\geq 1 , the inequalities in ( 1.2 ) saturate to an identity: 
 

 
 (1.3) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) = gap RW ​ ( G , α ) ​ , α min ≥ 1 ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\alpha_{\rm min}\geq 1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This one-particle reduction corresponds to a  SIP {\rm SIP} -version of the celebrated Aldous’ spectral gap conjecture, originally formulated for interchange and symmetric exclusion processes in the early 90s, and settled two decades later in  [ CLR10 ] . In fact, the identity in ( 1.3 ) is extremely powerful, as it reduces, for every underlying graph  G G , the spectral gap of  SIP {\rm SIP}  — an arbitrarily large system, as it may consist of arbitrarily many particles — to that of  RW {\rm RW} , a much simpler Markov chain with finite state space  V V , for which several techniques to bound the spectral gap in terms of simple features of the underlying graph  G G  are known (see, e.g.,  [ SC97 ,  MT06 ,  LP17 ,  Her23 ] ). For completeness, let us emphasize that an identity like ( 1.3 ), while trivial for a system of independent particles, is not at all expected to hold for truly interacting systems. Indeed, apart from the processes treated in  [ CLR10 ] , only a handful of other models were discovered to satisfy, on any geometry, a spectral gap identity involving the corresponding random walk. These models are: 
 
 •  
 
 the symmetric exclusion process in contact with reservoirs in  [ Sal23 ,  Sal24 ] ; 
 
 •  
 
 the Binomial splitting process in  [ QS23 ,  BC24 ] . 
 
 
 The identity in ( 1.3 ) adds  SIP {\rm SIP}  to the short list above, provided that  α min ≥ 1 \alpha_{\rm min}\geq 1  (we shall also refer to this condition as “log-concave regime”, see Remark  2.1  below). 
 
 
 One of our main results ( Theorem  2.4 ) states that, without this condition on  α min \alpha_{\rm min} , this identity, in general, fails: for some graphs  G G  and site weights  α \alpha , 
 

 
 (1.4) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) < gap RW ​ ( G , α ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)<{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 In other words,
the factor  1 ∧ α min 1\wedge\alpha_{\rm min}  in ( 1.2 ) cannot be generally neglected when 
 

 
 (1.5) 
 
 α min ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) ​ . \alpha_{\rm min}\in(0,1)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This naturally leads us to further investigate this regime, with the twofold goal of: 
 
 (1)  
 
 deriving an alternative lower bound for  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha) , better capturing its dependence on  α min \alpha_{\rm min} ; 
 
 (2)  
 
 obtaining a two-particle reduction of  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha) , in the asymptotic regime of vanishing diffusivity  α → 0 \alpha\to 0 . 
 
 
 We discuss these two steps in Sections  1.2  and  1.3 , respectively. 
 
 
 
 1.2.  Sharp dependence on  α min \alpha_{\rm min} 

 
 In order to isolate the role of  α min \alpha_{\rm min} , let us introduce new site weights  α ^ = α α min \hat{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha_{\rm min}} .
With this notation, a simple scaling argument implies 
 

 
 (1.6) 
 
 gap RW ​ ( G , α ) = α min ​ gap RW ​ ( G , α ^ ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)=\alpha_{\rm min}\,{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Due to the particle interaction, the same argument does not directly apply to  SIP ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha) . Instead,
if combined with the inequalities in ( 1.2 ), the above identity yields 
 

 
 (1.7) 
 
 α min 2 ​ gap RW ​ ( G , α ^ ) ≤ gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) ≤ α min ​ gap RW ​ ( G , α ^ ) ​ , α min ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) ​ . \alpha_{\rm min}^{2}\,{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha})\leq{\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)\leq\alpha_{\rm min}\,{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\alpha_{\rm min}\in(0,1)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, if we encode the dependence on  ( G , α ^ ) (G,\hat{\alpha})  through  gap RW ​ ( G , α ^ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha}) , the lower bound in ( 1.7 ) above would predict  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  to depend on the square of
 α min \alpha_{\rm min} . 
 
 
 Our second main result ( Theorem  2.2 ) proves this guess to be wrong, showing that, in general,  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  depends linearly, rather than quadratically as in ( 1.7 ), on  α min ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha_{\rm min}\in(0,1) : for all graphs  G G  and site weights  α \alpha , 
 

 
 (1.8) 
 
 C ​ α min ≤ gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) ​ , α min ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) ​ , C\,\alpha_{\rm min}\leq{\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\alpha_{\rm min}\in(0,1)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  C > 0 C>0  is a constant depending only on  G G  and  α ^ = α α min \hat{\alpha}=\frac{\alpha}{\alpha_{\rm min}} .
Indeed, in Theorem  2.2 , since  α min ≤ 1 \alpha_{\rm min}\leq 1  and  α ^ ratio = α ratio \hat{\alpha}_{\rm ratio}=\alpha_{\rm ratio} , we have 
 

 
 (1.9) 
 
 α min ​ { 1 54 ​ α ^ ratio ( 6 ​ e ) 1 / α ^ ratio ​ c min | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) } ≤ gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) ​ , \alpha_{\rm min}\left\{\frac{1}{54}\,\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{\rm ratio}}{(6e)^{1/\hat{\alpha}_{\rm ratio}}}\,\frac{c_{\rm min}}{|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)}\right\}\leq{\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the constant inside the bracket in the left-hand side depends only on the graph structure and the constant  α ^ \hat{\alpha} .
Because of the second inequality in ( 1.7 ), the order-one dependence on  α min \alpha_{\rm min}  for  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  in ( 1.8 ) is sharp. As we will see, this sharpened result crucially requires to express the dependence on  G G  not through  gap RW ​ ( G , α ^ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha}) , but other features of the underlying geometry. 
 
 
 
 1.3.  Two-particle spectral gap identity 

 
 All considerations made so far leave the following question unanswered:
given that the spectral gap of  SIP {\rm SIP}  does not always coincide with that of a single particle (namely,  RW {\rm RW} ), does it instead coincide with the spectral gap of the  k k -particle  SIP {\rm SIP} , for some integer  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , independent of the underlying graph  G G  and site weights  α \alpha ? 
 
 
 Asking the same question for  SIP {\rm SIP} ’s “instantaneously thermalized”  variant also known as Beta-Binomial splitting process (see, e.g.,  [ PR26 ]  or Section  8.2 ) — Pietro Caputo conjectured (personal communication) this spectral gap reduction to hold true with  k = 2 k=2 . In words, the Beta-Binomial splitting dynamics consists of, first selecting an edge  x ​ y xy  with rate  c x ​ y c_{xy}  (regardless of the particle configuration), and then letting particles sitting on  x x  and  y y  redistribute themselves according to the  SIP {\rm SIP} -equilibrium, restricted to that edge. Besides being clearly related to  SIP {\rm SIP} ,
the Beta-Binomial splitting process is the particle analogue of a model which shows up in the literature under various names, e.g., the Kipnis-Marchioro-Presutti model  [ KMP82 ] , a random walk on the simplex  [ CLL20 ] , or (the energies of) the renowned Kac’s walk  [ Kac56 ,  CCL03 ] . Spectral gap estimates for this model are known only on two specific geometric settings: the complete graph  [ CCL03 ,  Cap08 ] , and the segment with  α min ≥ 1 \alpha_{\rm min}\geq 1   [ CLL20 ] . In both cases, a spectral gap identity with  k = 2 k=2  is indeed verified. 
 
 
 The aforementioned conjecture translates to the context of  SIP {\rm SIP}  as follows: for all graphs  G G  and site weights  α \alpha , 
 

 
 (1.10) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) = gap 2 ​ ( G , α ) ​ , {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)={\rm gap}_{2}(G,\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  gap k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha) ,  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , stands for the spectral gap of the  k k -particle  SIP {\rm SIP} . Clearly, we have  gap 1 ​ ( G , α ) = gap RW ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{1}(G,\alpha)={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)  and ( 1.10 ) equivalently reads as  gap k ​ ( G , α ) = gap 2 ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha)={\rm gap}_{2}(G,\alpha) , for all  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  (cf. ( 2.5 ) and ( 2.10 )). Moreover, in view of ( 1.3 ) and  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) ≤ gap 2 ​ ( G , α ) ≤ gap 1 ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)\leq{\rm gap}_{2}(G,\alpha)\leq{\rm gap}_{1}(G,\alpha)  in ( 2.10 ), the two-particle spectral gap identity in ( 1.10 ) remains to be verified only off the log-concave regime, i.e., when ( 1.5 ) holds. 
 
 
 We provide a partial answer in the affirmative to this conjecture, by determining spectral gap asymptotics as  α → 0 \alpha\to 0  along directional limits (i.e., for  ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0  and some  α ^ ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) V \hat{\alpha}\in(0,\infty)^{V} ,  α = ε ​ α ^ \alpha=\varepsilon\hat{\alpha} ; see ( 2.11 )). The precise form of the asymptotic validity of ( 1.10 ) is the content of  Theorem  2.3  below. We refer to Section  2.3  for more details, but let us briefly remark that our asymptotic analysis highlights the key role that the two-particle dynamics plays for both results in Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 . We believe this point of view to be fruitful also for other related interacting systems with Gamma-like reversible measures. For more details, see Section  8.2 . 
 
 
 
 1.4.  Non-conservative case 

 
 So far, we discussed only closed (or conservative) systems in which inclusion particles neither get created nor annihilated. Open (or non-conservative) systems allow for this possibility, usually modeled as systems in contact with reservoirs, see, e.g.,  [ Spo83 ,  DEHP93 ,  CGGR13 ] .
For  SIP {\rm SIP} , the standard choice consists of creating a particle in  x x  with rate  ω x ​ θ x ​ ( α x + η x ) \omega_{x}\,\theta_{x}\left(\alpha_{x}+\eta_{x}\right) , and
annihilating each particle therein (if any) with rate  ω x ​ ( 1 + θ x ) \omega_{x}\left(1+\theta_{x}\right) , where  ω x \omega_{x}  and  θ x \theta_{x}  form a set of non-negative site parameters added to the model. For precise definitions, see Section  6 . 
 
 
 In essence, the open  SIP {\rm SIP}  presents two main features: first, due to the lack of particle conservation, the configuration space now consists of a unique, countably infinite, irreducible component; second, the unique ergodic measure is reversible if the  θ \theta -parameters do not depend on  x x , and non-reversible if they do depend.
This drastic change from reversibility to non-reversibility goes together with the emergence of a number of remarkable phenomena (e.g., currents, long-range correlations), which distinguish equilibrium from non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, see, e.g.,  [ Spo91 ,  DMP91 ,  KL99 ,  Sch01 ] . 
 
 
 Nevertheless, as already observed in the physics literature around thirty years ago for a large class of exactly solvable models  [ ADHR94 ]  (see also  [ FGK20 ] ), the “spectrum” does not depend on the  θ \theta -parameters, and, thus, is the same for both equilibrium and non-equilibrium systems. The quotation marks above are required when dealing with infinite configuration spaces and corresponding generators, because the full spectrum,  a priori , may include also non-eigenvalues and may depend on the underlying functional space on which the matrix/operators act. That said, this  θ \theta -independence of the spectrum becomes rigorous for systems having a finite configuration space as, for instance, the symmetric exclusion process in contact with reservoirs (see Remark  6.6  below for more details). 
 
 
 In this article, we establish the following results for the non-conservative  SIP {\rm SIP} , for any underlying graph  G G  and site weights  α \alpha ,  ω \omega , and  θ \theta : 
 
 (1)  
 
 Eigenfunctions of the purely absorbing system (corresponding to  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0 ) “lift” to (generalized) eigenfunctions of  SIP {\rm SIP}  (with general  θ \theta ) associated to the same eigenvalues. 
 
 (2)  
 
 Remarkably, the spectral gap of the particle system with  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0  coincides with 
 

 
 (1.11) 
 
 gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) > 0 ​ , {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)>0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 the spectral gap of the one-particle system, namely, of the random walk evolving on  G G , and killed with rate  ω x \omega_{x}  when sitting on  x ∈ V x\in V  ( Theorem  6.1 ). 
 
 (3)  
 
 In the reversible case (i.e., when  θ ≡ const . \theta\equiv{\rm const.} ), the eigenfunctions obtained in  1  provide an orthonormal basis in a natural  L 2 L^{2} -space. 
 
 
 Let us emphasize that step  1  holds for general  θ \theta  (i.e., in both reversible and non-reversible settings), while step  2  is a statement concerning the case  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0  only; establishing the claim in  3  is where we need to restrict to the reversible  SIP {\rm SIP} . 
 
 
 Putting these steps together, our main result in this non-conservative setting ( Corollary  6.4 ) may be summarized as follows: for all graphs  G G  and site weights  α \alpha  and  ω \omega , 
 

 
 (1.12) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α , ω , ϱ ) = gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) ​ , whenever ​ θ ≡ ϱ > 0 ​ , {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\varrho)={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{whenever}\ \theta\equiv\varrho>0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  gap SIP ​ ( G , α , ω , ϱ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\varrho)  denotes the first gap in the spectrum of the reversible  SIP {\rm SIP}  with  θ ≡ ϱ > 0 \theta\equiv\varrho>0 , compatibly with the functional setting of step  3 . 
 
 
 When comparing the conservative and non-conservative settings, we remark a different behavior of  SIP {\rm SIP} ’s spectral gap: while a system of  two particles  sharply captures the spectral gap of the many-particle  closed   SIP {\rm SIP} , the spectral gap of the  open   SIP {\rm SIP}  is always governed (at least when  θ ≡ const . \theta\equiv{\rm const.} ) by  one random walk with killing  — with no constraint on the value of  α min > 0 \alpha_{\rm min}>0 . We believe this dichotomy to hold true not just for  SIP {\rm SIP} , but also for a larger class of interacting systems in statistical mechanics having Dirichlet-like steady states (see Section  8.2  for further details). 
 
 
 
 1.5.  Organization of the paper 

 
 The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Sections  2 – 5  concern the closed  SIP {\rm SIP} . More specifically, Section  2  contains the model definition, the main results (Theorems  2.2 – 2.4 ), and an outline of their proofs. In Sections  3 – 4 , we present the proof of Theorem  2.2 ; in Section  5 , we prove the remaining two theorems from Section  2 . Sections  6 – 7  focus on  SIP {\rm SIP}  in contact with reservoirs, the first one of these sections detailing the model and main results, the second one presenting the proofs of the main results therein.
Finally, in Section  8 , we briefly discuss some extensions of our results to discrete and continuous-spin models related to  SIP {\rm SIP} . In particular, in view of the isospectrality showed in  [ KS24 ]  between  SIP {\rm SIP}  and Brownian energy process, we derive spectral gap estimates for the latter. We conclude the paper with two appendices, in which we provide the full proofs of two technical ingredients employed in Section  5 .
For the reader’s convenience, we add, at the end of the paper, a notation guide collecting the main recurrent symbols. 
 
 
 
 
 2.  Conservative SIP. Setting and main results 

 
 Unless stated otherwise, all throughout the article, our underlying geometry consists of a weighted finite graph  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V}) , with symmetric conductances  c x ​ y = c y ​ x ≥ 0 c_{xy}=c_{yx}\geq 0  (conventionally,  c x ​ x = 0 c_{xx}=0 ). We always assume the undirected graph  G G  to be connected, i.e., for all  x , y ∈ V x,y\in V , there exists a sequence  x 0 = x , x 1 , … , x ℓ − 1 , x ℓ = y x_{0}=x,x_{1},\ldots,x_{\ell-1},x_{\ell}=y  in  V V  such that  ∏ j = 1 ℓ c x j − 1 ​ x j > 0 \prod_{j=1}^{\ell}c_{x_{j-1}x_{j}}>0 . The length  ℓ ∈ ℕ \ell\in\mathbb{N}  of the shortest sequence connecting two distinct sites  x , y ∈ V x,y\in V  corresponds to their graph distance,  dist G ​ ( x , y ) {\rm dist}_{G}(x,y) . As usual, we define the diameter,  diam ⁡ ( G ) ∈ { 1 , … , | V | } {\rm diam}(G)\in\{1,\ldots,|V|\} , as the largest of all pairwise distances. 
 
 
 2.1.  Model 

 
 For a graph  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V}) , positive site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} , and  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} ,  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha)  denotes the Markov chain evolving on the configuration space 
 

 
 (2.1) 
 
 Ξ k ≔ { η ∈ ℕ 0 V : | η | = k } ​ , with ​ | η | ≔ ∑ x ∈ V η x ​ , \Xi_{k}\coloneqq\left\{\eta\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{V}:|\eta|=k\right\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{with}\ |\eta|\coloneqq\sum_{x\in V}\eta_{x}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 with infinitesimal generator given, for all  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} , as 
 

 
 (2.2) 
 
 L G , α , k ​ f ​ ( η ) = ∑ x , y ∈ V c x ​ y ​ η x ​ ( α y + η y ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( η − δ x + δ y ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) ​ , η ∈ Ξ k ​ . L_{G,\alpha,k}f(\eta)=\sum_{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\left(\alpha_{y}+\eta_{y}\right)\left(f(\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})-f(\eta)\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Xi_{k}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 In this formula,  η − δ x + δ y ∈ Ξ k \eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y}\in\Xi_{k}  denotes the configuration obtained from  η \eta  by removing a particle from  x x  (if any) and placing it on  y y .
Since all particle configurations in  Ξ k \Xi_{k}  are accessible (as the graph  G G  is connected and the weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V}  are all positive),  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha)  is irreducible. As a simple detailed balance computation shows,  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha)  admits a unique reversible measure  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k} , given, for all  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k} , by 
 

 
 (2.3) 
 
 μ α , k ​ ( η ) = 1 Z α , k ​ ∏ x ∈ V Γ ⁡ ( α x + η x ) Γ ⁡ ( α x ) ​ η x ! ​ , with ​ Z α , k ≔ Γ ⁡ ( | α | + k ) Γ ⁡ ( | α | ) ​ k ! ​ . \mu_{\alpha,k}(\eta)=\frac{1}{Z_{\alpha,k}}\prod_{x\in V}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+\eta_{x})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x})\,\eta_{x}!}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{with}\ Z_{\alpha,k}\coloneqq\frac{\Gamma(|\alpha|+k)}{\Gamma(|\alpha|)\,k!}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  Γ \Gamma  denotes the usual gamma function satisfying, for all  a > 0 a>0 ,  Γ ⁡ ( a + 1 ) = a ​ Γ ​ ( a ) \Gamma(a+1)=a\,\Gamma(a) . 
 
 
 Remark 2.1   (Log-concavity) . 
 
 The measure  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  in ( 2.3 ) is known as Dirichlet-Multinomial distribution of parameters  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N}  and  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} , and is the discrete analogue of the Dirichlet distribution on the simplex  Σ ⊂ ℝ V \varSigma\subset\mathbb{R}^{V}  of probability measures, see ( 8.1 ) and ( 8.3 ) below. The Dirichlet distribution is well known to be log-concave (in the sense that its probability density is of the form  exp ⁡ ( − U ) \exp(-U) , for some convex  U : ℝ V → ℝ U:\mathbb{R}^{V}\to\mathbb{R} ) if and only if  α min ≥ 1 \alpha_{\rm min}\geq 1 . Hence,  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  is the discrete counterpart of a log-concave measure if and only if  α min ≥ 1 \alpha_{\rm min}\geq 1 .
More directly,  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  is a “discrete log-concave measure” also in the following sense:  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  is the canonical measure (i.e., conditional on  | η | = k |\eta|=k ) of the product measure  ν α , ϱ \nu_{\alpha,\varrho}  in ( 6.3 ) below, whose Negative-Binomial marginal measures on  ℕ 0 \mathbb{N}_{0}  are log-concave (i.e.,  ν α , ϱ ​ ( η x = ℓ ) 2 ≥ ν α , ϱ ​ ( η x = ℓ − 1 ) ​ ν α , ϱ ​ ( η x = ℓ + 1 ) \nu_{\alpha,\varrho}(\eta_{x}=\ell)^{2}\geq\nu_{\alpha,\varrho}(\eta_{x}=\ell-1)\,\nu_{\alpha,\varrho}(\eta_{x}=\ell+1) , for all  ℓ ≥ 1 \ell\geq 1  and  x ∈ V x\in V ) if and only if  α min ≥ 1 \alpha_{\rm min}\geq 1 . 
 
 
 
 
 2.2.  A non-asymptotic lower bound for the spectral gap 

 
 Because of irreducibility and reversibility, all  | Ξ k | |\Xi_{k}|  eigenvalues
of the (negative) generator  − L G , α , k -L_{G,\alpha,k}  in ( 2.2 ) are real and non-negative, the smallest one being equal to zero, whereas the second one — referred to as spectral gap of  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha)  and shortened as  gap k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha)  — being strictly positive. Confronting with the notation from Section  1 , we have 
 

 
 (2.4) 
 
 gap RW ​ ( G , α ) = gap 1 ​ ( G , α ) ​ , {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)={\rm gap}_{1}(G,\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 because  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha)  with  k = 1 k=1  corresponds to a single, thus, non-interacting particle, abbreviated as  RW ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm RW}(G,\alpha) . All cases  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  describe a truly interacting system; hence, we define 
 

 
 (2.5) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) = inf k ≥ 2 gap k ​ ( G , α ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)=\inf_{k\geq 2}{\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 We are now ready to state the precise statement of the result in ( 1.8 ), concerned with the sharp dependence of  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  on  α min = min x ∈ V ⁡ α x \alpha_{\rm min}=\min_{x\in V}\alpha_{x} , with a special focus on the regime  α min ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha_{\rm min}\in(0,1) . 
 
 
 Theorem 2.2 . 
 
 For all graphs  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})  and site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} , we have 
 

 
 (2.6) 
 
 α min ​ { 1 54 ​ α ratio ( 6 ​ e ) α min / α ratio ​ c min | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) } ≤ gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) ​ , \alpha_{\rm min}\left\{\frac{1}{{54}}\,\frac{\alpha_{\rm ratio}}{{{(6e)}}^{\alpha_{\rm min}/\alpha_{\rm ratio}}}\,\frac{c_{\rm min}}{|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)}\right\}\leq{\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where 
 

 
 (2.7) 
 
 α ratio ≔ α min α max = min x ∈ V ⁡ α x max y ∈ V ⁡ α y ≤ 1 ​ , c min ≔ min x , y ∈ V c x ​ y > 0 ⁡ c x ​ y > 0 ​ . \alpha_{\rm ratio}\coloneqq\frac{\alpha_{\rm min}}{\alpha_{\rm max}}=\frac{\min_{x\in V}\alpha_{x}}{\max_{y\in V}\alpha_{y}}\leq 1\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad c_{\rm min}\coloneqq\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in V\\
c_{xy}>0\end{subarray}}c_{xy}>0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 As already discussed in Section  1.2 , this lower bound captures the correct dependence of
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  on  α min \alpha_{\rm min}  for every fixed  G G , provided that
 α ratio \alpha_{\rm ratio}  remains bounded away from zero. Moreover, the estimate is non-asymptotic, with a rather explicit constant depending only on simple geometric parameters of the graph, such as its size and diameter. We do not claim, however, that this geometric dependence is sharp.
This is fundamentally due to the fact that the  L 2 L^{2}  inequalities (Lemmas  4.2 – 4.6 ) used in the proof of Theorem  2.2 
are designed to maintain the sharp linear dependence on  α min \alpha_{\rm min} , at the expense of suboptimality caused by exploiting the geometric structure. 
 
 
 To illustrate this point, take  α ≡ ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha\equiv\varepsilon\in(0,1) , so that
 α min = ε \alpha_{\rm min}=\varepsilon  and  α ratio = 1 \alpha_{\rm ratio}=1 . For  d d -dimensional boxes or tori of side length  N N , Theorem  2.2  gives 
 

 
 (2.8) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , ε ​ 𝟏 ) ≥ C ​ ε ​ N − ( 2 ​ d + 1 ) ​ , {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon{\bf 1})\geq C\,\varepsilon\,N^{-(2d+1)}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 for some  C = C ⁡ ( d , c min ) > 0 C=C(d,c_{\rm min})>0 . This is not expected to be the correct dependence on  N N . Indeed, in view of Theorem  2.3  and of the proof of Theorem  2.4 , the relevant small- ε \varepsilon  scale should rather be 
 

 
 (2.9) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , ε ​ 𝟏 ) ≍ ε / s d ​ ( N ) ​ , s d ​ ( N ) ≔ { N 2 if ​ d = 1 N 2 ​ log ⁡ N if ​ d = 2 N d if ​ d ≥ 3 ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon{\bf 1})\asymp\varepsilon/s_{d}(N)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad s_{d}(N)\coloneqq\begin{cases}N^{2}&\text{if}\ d=1\\
N^{2}\log N&\text{if}\ d=2\\
N^{d}&\text{if}\ d\geq 3\;\text{.}\end{cases} 
 
 
 Thus, while Theorem  2.2  is sharp in its dependence on
 α min \alpha_{\rm min} , its dependence on the geometry is generally far from optimal. 
 
 
 Rather than optimizing these geometric constants on specific families of graphs, we keep the underlying geometry arbitrary and pass to the regime of vanishing weights  α → 0 \alpha\to 0 . In this setting, we identify the relevant asymptotic object not in terms of the spectral gap of  RW ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm RW}(G,\alpha) , but of the “second simplest system” on the same geometry:  SIP 2 ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{2}(G,\alpha) , namely the symmetric inclusion process with just two particles. 
 
 
 
 2.3.  Spectral gap’s asymptotics 

 
 As already proved in  [ KS24 ]  (see also Section  3.1  below), we have, for all graphs  G G  and site weights  α \alpha , 
 

 
 (2.10) 
 
 gap k + 1 ​ ( G , α ) ≤ gap k ​ ( G , α ) ​ , k ≥ 1 ​ . {{\rm gap}_{k+1}(G,\alpha)\leq{\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 1\;\text{.}} 
 
 
 The next result shows that, along directional limits  α → 0 \alpha\to 0 , i.e., 
 

 
 (2.11) 
 
 α = ε ​ α ^ ​ , with ​ ε → 0 ​ and ​ α ^ = ( α ^ x ) x ∈ V ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) V ​ fixed ​ , \alpha=\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{with}\ \varepsilon\to 0\ \text{and}\ \hat{\alpha}=(\hat{\alpha}_{x})_{x\in V}\in(0,\infty)^{V}\ \text{fixed}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 equality is achieved in ( 2.10 ) for  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 .
This result is formulated along fixed positive directions; see Remark
 5.15  below for the relation with general limits  α → 0 \alpha\to 0 . 
 
 
 Theorem 2.3 . 
 
 For all graphs  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})  and site weights  α ^ = ( α ^ x ) x ∈ V \hat{\alpha}=(\hat{\alpha}_{x})_{x\in V} , we have 
 

 
 (2.12) 
 
 lim ε → 0 gap k ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) gap 2 ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) = 1 ​ , k ≥ 2 ​ . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{{\rm gap}_{k}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})}{{\rm gap}_{2}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})}=1\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad{k\geq 2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 In view of the definition of  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  in ( 2.5 ) and of the inequalities in ( 2.10 ),
the above limit is an asymptotic version of the two-particle spectral gap identity in ( 1.10 ).
Let us stress that this result holds true for any graph  G G , positive site weights  α ^ \hat{\alpha} , and integer  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  (trivially, for  k = 2 k=2 ). However,
the case  k = 1 k=1  is excluded for a good reason, as shown in the following result. 
 
 
 Theorem 2.4 . 
 
 For some graphs  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})  and site weights  α ^ = ( α ^ x ) x ∈ V \hat{\alpha}=(\hat{\alpha}_{x})_{x\in V} , we have 
 

 
 (2.13) 
 
 lim ε → 0 gap 2 ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) gap 1 ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) < 1 ​ . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{{\rm gap}_{2}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})}{{\rm gap}_{1}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})}<1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 This inequality provides, if combined with ( 2.10 ) and the definitions in ( 2.4 )–( 2.5 ), an instance of ( 1.4 ), namely, of the failure of the one-particle spectral gap identity in ( 1.3 ).
The graphs  G G  and site weights  α ^ \hat{\alpha}  which we adopt to prove this result are far from being intricate. In fact, we take  G G  to be the standard  d d -dimensional discrete torus, with  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2  and sufficiently large size, and  α ^ ≡ 1 \hat{\alpha}\equiv 1 . These are just some of the possible examples that one could exhibit for proving ( 2.13 ). Indeed, as it will become apparent from the proof, essentially any other sparse geometry for which the random walk’s relaxation time (i.e.,  gap RW ​ ( G , α ) − 1 {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)^{-1} ) is much smaller than the expected meeting time of two independent random walks initialized at equilibrium, will equally work.
We refer to the subsequent section for further explanations. 
 
 
 
 2.4.  Proofs outline 

 
 Recall that an inclusion particle jumps from  x x  to a nearest neighbor  y y  at rate proportional to  α y + η y \alpha_{y}+\eta_{y} . On the one hand, when non-zero, the term  η y ≥ 1 \eta_{y}\geq 1  stands for the interaction between particles on adjacent sites, which mutually attract each other, tending to stick together. On the other hand,  α y > 0 \alpha_{y}>0  represents an independent mechanism of particle diffusion. In this sense, the regime  α min ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha_{\rm min}\in(0,1)  depicts the situation in which, at least in some portions of the graph, particle stickiness dominates over diffusion. 
 
 
 This regime becomes particularly significant when considering the limit  α → 0 \alpha\to 0 . In this setting,  SIP ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  exhibits a metastable behavior, as thoroughly studied in the last decade  [ GRV13 ,  BDG17 ,  KS21 ,  Kim21 ,  Kim25 ] . These works describe the following qualitative picture, when  α = ε ​ α ^ \alpha=\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}  as in ( 2.11 ). Roughly speaking, as long as particles are far from each other (i.e., at graph distance  ≥ 2 \geq 2 ), they perform jumps on the timescale  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} . As soon as they are at distance one, they stick together in a much shorter time, roughly of order one. These two mechanisms bring particles to meet and pile up together in large stacks (or, condensates) in a time of order  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} . Concurrently, some particles sitting on a stack would still attempt to jump to empty nearest neighboring vertices at rate  ≍ ε \asymp\varepsilon . While some of the attempts fail, that is, the “courageous” particle is instantaneously sucked back into a neighboring stack, some of these will succeed to attract sufficiently many particles, managing to move the whole stack. Due to the rates’ symmetry encoded in the condition  c x ​ y = c y ​ x c_{xy}=c_{yx} , it is then part of the results in  [ GRV13 ,  BDG17 ]  to show that relevant jumps of entire stacks occur at times of order  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} , and that, on this timescale, stacks are effectively approximated by independent random walks on  G G , each evolving at rate proportional to  ε \varepsilon , and capable of coalescing (possibly in a very complicated way if three or more stacks are involved) when getting at distance one from each other. Hence, the metastable relevant part of the dynamics takes place, according to this qualitative picture, on the timescale  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} . 
 
 
 Although this metastable picture provides a first correct intuition that  gap SIP ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) ≍ ε {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})\asymp\varepsilon  as  ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 , our quantitative analysis must take into account also features not captured by usual metastability limit theorems.
For instance, while metastability describes macroscopic features of the system when initialized from macroscopically relevant configurations, global functional inequalities as those determining spectral gaps require bounds which must be uniform over the initial conditions.
Moreover, we seek for lower bounds for  gap SIP ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  that are independent of the system size.
However, a crucial ingredient in the metastability picture of  SIP ⁡ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  is that  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , the number of particles in the system is not too large; more precisely, 
 

 
 (2.14) 
 
 log ⁡ k ≪ ε − 1 ​ , as ​ ε ≪ 1 ​ . \log k\ll\varepsilon^{-1}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{as}\ \varepsilon\ll 1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This condition, always assumed in previous works (see, e.g.,  [ GRV13 ,  BDG17 ,  Kim21 ,  Kim25 ] ) is not just technical, but strictly required for the condensation mechanism to take place: ( 2.14 ) is a sufficient and necessary condition for the steady state of the system to charge configurations consisting of a single stack of particles only. 
 
 
 2.4.1.  Outline of the proof of Theorem  2.2 

 
 Our proof for determining the order-one dependence on  ε ​ α ^ min \varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{\rm min}  of  gap SIP ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  combines three main ingredients: 
 
 •  
 
 the nested eigenstructure of  SIP {\rm SIP}  (valid for any underlying graph  G G ) as already exploited in  [ KS24 ] ; 
 
 •  
 
 the full knowledge of the eigendecomposition in mean-field geometries and, more specifically, the fact that eigenvalues grow quadratically (neglecting multiplicities) with the particle total number; 
 
 •  
 
 comparison inequalities of Dirichlet forms associated to the particle system on a graph  G G , against that on the complete graph. 
 
 
 All three steps are presented and combined to yield the proof of Theorem  2.2  in Section  3 , but the actual proof of the comparison inequality, longer and more technical, is presented in Section  4 . 
 
 
 In the metastable regime ( 2.14 ), our comparisons build on the classical distinguishing paths method as applied, e.g., in the seminal work  [ DSC93 ]  to the symmetric exclusion process. Most of the care in our context lies in devising paths in which the total “cost” becomes not larger than  ε − 1 ​ k 2 \varepsilon^{-1}\,k^{2} , up to constants depending only on  G G  and  α ^ \hat{\alpha} . Here, the cost of each move heavily depends on whether relocating a single particle augments, keeps constant, or lowers the total number of stacks, and whether stacks do have or not neighboring stacks. Hence, following the metastable behavior of the system offers us a guideline to construct efficient distinguishing paths, e.g., taking care of never creating more than one extra occupied site along the path. We refer to Sections  4.2  and  4.3  for the details. 
 
 
 When ( 2.14 ) does not hold, as already mentioned, the metastability picture breaks down, and so does this approach via distinguishing paths. In order to overcome this, we replace paths by a more sophisticated two-dimensional  surface , which is obtained by collecting the trace of all relevant paths. In presence of stacks consisting of a huge number of particles, this two-dimensional surface allows us to rigorously implement the idea of moving only a smaller portion of the stack at the time according to some suitably chosen probability, and leaving the rest untouched. While this strategy of moving only some (and not all) particles of a stack produces an extra factor  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} , which we could not afford in the regime ( 2.14 ), this is now not problematic, as we can control this factor with a suitable function of the number of particles of the stack. For more details, see Section  4.4 . 
 
 
 As we just sketched, these estimates involving Dirichlet forms yield comparison constants which unavoidably degenerate like  1 / k 2 1/k^{2}  as the total number of particles  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N}  grows. Here is where we crucially exploit the first two ingredients of the proof, in particular, the quadratic growth of eigenvalues for the mean-field system, which removes this degeneracy. 
 
 
 
 2.4.2.  Outline of the proof of Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 

 
 In order to establish the strict inequality between  gap SIP ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  and  gap RW ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  as in ( 1.4 ), comparison arguments as those we just described turn out to be too loose (and, actually, too elaborated) to capture the precise pre-factors. Instead, we follow a more basic approach, namely, turning the aforementioned metastability picture into some quantitative spectral information. 
 
 
 More specifically, for a fixed graph  G G  and a fixed number of particles  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} , we employ and sharpen classical limit theorems for slow–fast systems from  [ Kur73 ]  (see also  [ GRV13 ]  for another application in the context of  SIP {\rm SIP} ). These theorems rigorously describe the metastable dynamics for the slow macroscopic variables of  SIP ⁡ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  in the limit  ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 . The limit of the slow variables after a thermalization of the fast ones identifies the low-lying spectrum. This allows us to capture the behavior of  gap SIP ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}) , at least for  ε \varepsilon  small enough. These intuitive ideas are stated and proved rigorously in Section  5.1 . 
 
 
 We are then left with identifying the spectral gap of the limiting metastable dynamics. In contrast to  SIP {\rm SIP} , which is irreducible, this metastable chain may consist of both recurrent and transient states, as we detail in Section  5.2 . Recurrent regions correspond to those configurations in which all particles sit together, and the resulting single stack moves like  RW ⁡ ( G , α ^ ) {\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha}) . Transient states are those configurations in which there are stacks at graph distance at least two from each other. Hence, the spectral gap of the metastable process is just the smallest among the random walk’s gap and the eigenvalues associated to any part of the (sub-stochastic) transient dynamics. 
 
 
 Most of our work is devoted to analyzing the hierarchy of transient states. Indeed, even the decomposition into irreducible components and the jump rates of the transient dynamics are, in general, highly complicated and non-trivially dependent on the underlying geometry and the number of particles. Nevertheless, by exploiting a form of consistency for the metastable limiting dynamics, we are able to deduce that the lowest-lying eigenvalue of the transient dynamics is attained by the system with just  two  particles, as adding more particles — thus, potentially, stacks — does not cause any slowdown in the system. More in detail, in Section  5.3 , we verify that one only needs to focus on the number of stacks (and not on the precise allocation of particles in each stack): for each  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , the smallest eigenvalue of all transient sub-systems with  m m  piles comes exactly from the sub-system with  m m  isolated particles. As a next step, in Section  5.4 , we prove that the smallest eigenvalue of all sub-systems with  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2  isolated particles is attained at  m = 2 m=2 , i.e., the sub-system with two isolated particles. Collecting these observations, we prove Theorem  2.3  in Section  5.5 .
Theorem  2.4  is also proved in this section: by exploiting this rigorous metastable description of eigenvalues’ asymptotics, our proof boils down to estimates of relaxation and mean meeting times of independent particles on a well studied geometry, corresponding, respectively, to (the inverse of) the spectral gap of the recurrent and transient dynamics of  SIP {\rm SIP} ’s metastable process. 
 
 
 
 
 
 3.  Proof of Theorem  2.2 

 
 We follow the outline in Section  2.4.1 . 
 
 
 3.1.  General eigenstructure of SIP 

 
 We start by recalling from  [ KS24 ]  some general facts on the eigenstructure of  SIP {\rm SIP}  functional to our analysis.
First of all,  SIP {\rm SIP}  is consistent, in the sense that the system with  k − 1 k-1  particles is recovered (in the sense of finite-dimensional distributions) from the system with  k k  particles, provided that one particle is removed  uniformly at random . In formula, this means that 
 

 
 (3.1) 
 
 L G , α , k ​ 𝔞 k = 𝔞 k ​ L G , α , k − 1 L_{G,\alpha,k}\,\mathfrak{a}_{k}=\mathfrak{a}_{k}\,L_{G,\alpha,k-1} 
 
 
 holds true for all graphs  G G , site weights  α \alpha , and  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , where  𝔞 k : ℝ Ξ k − 1 → ℝ Ξ k \mathfrak{a}_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k-1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  is the  annihilation operator  defined as 
 

 
 (3.2) 
 
 𝔞 k ​ g ​ ( η ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ V η x ​ g ​ ( η − δ x ) ​ , g ∈ ℝ Ξ k − 1 ​ , ​ η ∈ Ξ k ​ . \mathfrak{a}_{k}g(\eta)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in V}\eta_{x}\,g(\eta-\delta_{x})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad g\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k-1}}\;\text{,}\;\;\eta\in\Xi_{k}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 As proved in  [ KS24 , Appendix A] ,  𝔞 k \mathfrak{a}_{k}  is one-to-one. This readily implies that the (real) spectrum of  L G , α , k − 1 L_{G,\alpha,k-1}  is contained (with multiplicities) in that of  L G , α , k L_{G,\alpha,k}  (and, in particular, that ( 2.10 ) holds). More precisely, if  ( λ , f ) (\lambda,f)  is an eigenvalue-eigenfunction pair for  − L G , α , k − 1 -L_{G,\alpha,k-1} , then  ( λ , 𝔞 k ​ f ) (\lambda,\mathfrak{a}_{k}f)  is one for  − L G , α , k -L_{G,\alpha,k} . As a consequence of this fact and reversibility, the rest of the spectrum of  − L G , α , k -L_{G,\alpha,k}  that does not come from  − L G , α , k − 1 -L_{G,\alpha,k-1}  must be found in the orthogonal complement of those eigenfunctions “lifted” from those of  − L G , α , k − 1 -L_{G,\alpha,k-1} .
This observation leads us to introduce the  creation operator   𝔞 α , k − 1 † : ℝ Ξ k → ℝ Ξ k − 1 \mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}:\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k-1}} : 
 

 
 (3.3) 
 
 𝔞 α , k − 1 † ​ f ​ ( ξ ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ V ( ξ x + α x ) ​ f ​ ( ξ + δ x ) ​ , f ∈ ℝ Ξ k ​ , ​ ξ ∈ Ξ k − 1 ​ . \mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}f(\xi)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in V}(\xi_{x}+\alpha_{x})\,f(\xi+\delta_{x})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\;\text{,}\;\;\xi\in\Xi_{k-1}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 As demonstrated in  [ KS24 , Proposition 3.1] , the two operators  𝔞 k \mathfrak{a}_{k}  and  𝔞 α , k − 1 † \mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}  are adjoint one to each other in the following sense: 
 

 
 (3.4) 
 
 ⟨ 𝔞 k ​ g | f ⟩ α , k = k | α | + k − 1 ​ ⟨ g | 𝔞 α , k − 1 † ​ f ⟩ α , k − 1 ​ , f ∈ ℝ Ξ k ​ , ​ g ∈ ℝ Ξ k − 1 ​ , \langle\mathfrak{a}_{k}g\,|\,f\rangle_{\alpha,k}=\frac{k}{|\alpha|+k-1}\,\langle g\,|\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}f\rangle_{\alpha,k-1}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\;\text{,}\;\;g\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k-1}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ⟨ ⋅ | ⋅ ⟩ α , k \langle\,\cdot\,\,|\,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{\alpha,k}  denotes the inner product in  Ξ k \Xi_{k}  with respect to  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  given in ( 2.3 ). In turn,  𝔞 α , k − 1 † \mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}  is surjective and the following orthogonal decomposition holds: 
 

 
 (3.5) 
 
 L 2 ( μ α , k ) = Im 𝔞 k ⊕ ⟂ Ker 𝔞 α , k − 1 † . L^{2}(\mu_{\alpha,k})={\rm Im}\,\mathfrak{a}_{k}\oplus_{\perp}{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Next, let us recall a well-known variational characterization of  gap k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha) : for each  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , 
 

 
 (3.6) 
 
 gap k ​ ( G , α ) = inf f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f ≠ const. ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) Var α , k ​ ( f ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha)=\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\\
f\neq\text{const.}\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)}{{\rm Var}_{\alpha,k}(f)}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) = ⟨ f | − L G , α , k ​ f ⟩ α , k \mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)=\left\langle f\,\middle|\,-L_{G,\alpha,k}f\right\rangle_{\alpha,k}  denotes the Dirichlet form evaluated at  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} , namely, 
 

 
 (3.7) 
 
 ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ η ∈ Ξ k ∑ x , y ∈ V μ α , k ​ ( η ) ​ c x ​ y ​ η x ​ ( α y + η y ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( η − δ x + δ y ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) 2 ​ , \mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\sum_{x,y\in V}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\eta)\,c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\left(\alpha_{y}+\eta_{y}\right)\left(f(\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})-f(\eta)\right)^{2}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 whereas  Var α , k ​ ( f ) {\rm Var}_{\alpha,k}(f)  stands for the variance of  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  with respect to  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k} . If  f ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † f\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger} , then  ⟨ f ⟩ α , k = ⟨ f |  1 ⟩ α , k = 0 \langle f\rangle_{\alpha,k}=\langle f\,|\,1\rangle_{\alpha,k}=0  (remark that  1 ∈ Im ​ 𝔞 k 1\in{\rm Im}\,\mathfrak{a}_{k}  and apply ( 3.4 )) and, thus, 
 

 
 (3.8) 
 
 Var α , k ​ ( f ) = ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 ≔ ∑ η ∈ Ξ k μ α , k ​ ( η ) ​ f ​ ( η ) 2 ​ . {\rm Var}_{\alpha,k}(f)=\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\eta)\,f(\eta)^{2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Taking advantage of the lifting property of  𝔞 k \mathfrak{a}_{k} , the self-adjointness of  L G , α , k L_{G,\alpha,k}  on  L 2 ​ ( μ α , k ) L^{2}(\mu_{\alpha,k}) , and the orthogonal decomposition in ( 3.5 ), ( 3.6 ) simplifies as follows: 
 

 
 (3.9) 
 
 gap k ​ ( G , α ) = gap k − 1 ​ ( G , α ) ∧ ( inf f ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † f ≠ 0 ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha)={\rm gap}_{k-1}(G,\alpha)\wedge\bigg(\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}\\
f\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2}}\bigg)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, by an iterative argument on  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , we may focus on comparing  ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) \mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)  and  ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2} , only for functions  f ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † f\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger} . 
 
 
 
 3.2.  Dirichlet forms and comparisons 

 
 The Dirichlet form  ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) \mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)  depends on the underlying graph  G G , whereas the reversible measure  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k} , and, thus, the orthogonal decomposition in ( 3.5 ) and  ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2} , do not. This simple observation motivates us to compare  ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) \mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)  with its complete graph analogue, i.e.,  ℰ K , α , k ​ ( f ) \mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha,k}(f) , where  K = K V K=K_{V}  denotes the complete graph on the sites of  V V  with unitary conductances  c x ​ y ≡ 1 c_{xy}\equiv 1 . 
 
 
 It turns out that, in this complete graph case, we can obtain the full eigendecomposition of  SIP {\rm SIP} . Remark that, although the spectrum and a set of eigenfunctions of  SIP k ​ ( K , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(K,\alpha)  are known  [ Shi77 ]  (see also  [ Cor23 , Theorem 1.4]  or  [ WZ19 ]  and references therein), the next proposition, together with the identity in ( 3.9 ), provides a simple and complete description of eigenvalues and eigenspaces when  G = K G=K , which we shall exploit later. 
 
 
 Proposition 3.1 . 
 
 For every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  and  f ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † f\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger} , we have 
 

 
 (3.10) 
 
 ℰ K , α , k ​ ( f ) = k ⁡ ( | α | + k − 1 ) ​ ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 ​ . \mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha,k}(f)=k\left(|\alpha|+k-1\right)\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 The result is well known for  k = 1 k=1 , the random walk case. Indeed,  Ker ​ 𝔞 α , 0 † {\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,0}^{\dagger}  coincides with the subspace of functions  f ∈ ℝ Ξ 1 f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{1}}  having mean zero with respect to  μ α , 1 \mu_{\alpha,1} , which equals  μ α , 1 ​ ( δ x ) = α x | α | \mu_{\alpha,1}(\delta_{x})=\frac{\alpha_{x}}{|\alpha|} ,  x ∈ V x\in V . 
 
 
 Now, fix  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  and  f ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † f\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger} . We have 
 

 
 (3.11) 
 
 ℰ K , α , k ​ ( f ) = k ​ ∑ ξ ∈ Ξ k − 1 μ α , k − 1 ​ ( ξ ) ​ ℰ K , α + ξ , 1 ​ ( f ξ ) ​ , \mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha,k}(f)=k\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_{{k-1}}}\mu_{\alpha,k-1}(\xi)\,\mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha+\xi,1}(f_{\xi})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 with  f ξ ∈ ℝ Ξ 1 f_{\xi}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{1}}  being defined as  f ξ ​ ( δ x ) ≔ f ⁡ ( ξ + δ x ) f_{\xi}(\delta_{x})\coloneqq f(\xi+\delta_{x}) . Remark that ( 3.11 ) corresponds to  [ KS24 , Eq. (3.10)] , which is deduced from the following identity  [ KS24 , Eq. (3.9)] : for all  x ∈ V x\in V  and  ξ ∈ Ξ k − 1 \xi\in\Xi_{k-1} , 
 

 
 (3.12) 
 
 μ α , k ​ ( ξ + δ x ) ​ ( ξ x + 1 ) = Z α , k − 1 Z α , k ​ μ α , k − 1 ​ ( ξ ) ​ ( α x + ξ x ) = k ​ μ α , k − 1 ​ ( ξ ) ​ α x + ξ x | α | + k − 1 ​ , \mu_{\alpha,k}(\xi+\delta_{x})\left(\xi_{x}+1\right)=\frac{Z_{\alpha,k-1}}{Z_{\alpha,k}}\,\mu_{\alpha,k-1}(\xi)\left(\alpha_{x}+\xi_{x}\right)=k\,\mu_{\alpha,k-1}(\xi)\,\frac{\alpha_{x}+\xi_{x}}{|\alpha|+k-1}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the second step used (cf. ( 2.3 )) 
 

 
 (3.13) 
 
 Z α , k − 1 Z α , k = k | α | + k − 1 ​ . \frac{Z_{\alpha,k-1}}{Z_{\alpha,k}}=\frac{k}{|\alpha|+k-1}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Analogously to ( 3.11 ), we get 
 

 
 (3.14) 
 
 ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 = ∑ ξ ∈ Ξ k − 1 μ α , k − 1 ​ ( ξ ) ​ ‖ f ξ ‖ α + ξ , 1 2 ​ . \left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2}=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_{k-1}}\mu_{\alpha,k-1}(\xi)\left\lVert f_{\xi}\right\rVert_{\alpha+\xi,1}^{2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By ( 3.11 ) and the identity in ( 3.10 ) for  k = 1 k=1  (note that  f ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † f\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}  ensures that  f ξ ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , 0 † f_{\xi}\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,0}^{\dagger} ) with  α + ξ \alpha+\xi  in place of  α \alpha  therein (note that  | α + ξ | = | α | + k − 1 |\alpha+\xi|=|\alpha|+k-1 ), we obtain 
 

 
 (3.15) 
 
 ℰ K , α , k ​ ( f ) = k ⁡ ( | α | + k − 1 ) ​ ∑ ξ ∈ Ξ k − 1 μ α , k − 1 ​ ( ξ ) ​ ‖ f ξ ‖ α + ξ , 1 2 = k ⁡ ( | α | + k − 1 ) ​ ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 ​ , \mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha,k}(f)=k\left(|\alpha|+k-1\right)\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_{k-1}}\mu_{\alpha,k-1}(\xi)\left\lVert f_{\xi}\right\rVert_{\alpha+\xi,1}^{2}=k\left(|\alpha|+k-1\right)\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the last step used ( 3.14 ). This proves the desired result for  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 In view of ( 3.9 ) and Proposition  3.1 , it remains to compare the two Dirichlet forms  ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) \mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)  and  ℰ K , α , k ​ ( f ) \mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha,k}(f) , for any  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} . This is the content of the following theorem, and certainly represents the hardest step of the proof of Theorem  2.2 . 
 
 
 Theorem 3.2 . 
 
 For all  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  and  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} , we have 
 

 
 (3.16) 
 
 ℰ K , α , k ​ ( f ) ≤ 54 ​ k ​ ( α max + k − 1 ) ​ | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​ ( 6 ​ e ) α max α min ​ α ratio ​ c min ​ ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) ​ . \mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha,k}(f)\leq\frac{{54}\,k\left(\alpha_{\rm max}+k-1\right)|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)\,{(6e)}^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}{\alpha_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}\,c_{\rm min}}\,\mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 We postpone the proof of this estimate to Section  4  and, in Remark  4.7 , we discuss possible improvements. 
 
 
 Remark 3.3 . 
 
 While all previous steps relied on the fact that we chose the test function  f f  in  Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † ⊂ ℝ Ξ k {\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}\subset\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} , the last theorem is true for all  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} . 
 
 
 
 
 3.3.  Proof of Theorem  2.2 

 
 Assuming the validity of Theorem  3.2 ,
we now conclude the proof of Theorem  2.2 . 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  2.2 . 
 
 By ( 2.5 ), ( 3.9 ) and an iterative argument, it suffices to prove 
 

 
 (3.17) 
 
 inf f ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † f ≠ 0 ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 ≥ α min ​ 1 21 ​ α ratio ​ c min 6 α max ​ | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​ , for all ​ k ≥ 2 ​ . \inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}f\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}\\
f\neq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)}{\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2}}\geq\alpha_{\rm min}\,\frac{1}{21}\frac{\alpha_{\rm ratio}\,c_{\rm min}}{6^{\alpha_{\rm max}}\,|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{{for all}}\ k\geq 2\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By Theorem  3.2  and Proposition  3.1 , we have, for all  f ∈ Ker ​ 𝔞 α , k − 1 † f\in{\rm Ker}\,\mathfrak{a}_{\alpha,k-1}^{\dagger}  and  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , 
 

 
 (3.18) 
 
 ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f) 
 ≥ α min ​ α ratio ​ c min 54 ​ k ​ ( α max + k − 1 ) ​ | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​ ( 6 ​ e ) α max ​ ℰ K , α , k ​ ( f ) \displaystyle\geq\frac{\alpha_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}\,c_{\rm min}}{{54}\,k\left(\alpha_{\rm max}+k-1\right)|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)\,{(6e)}^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}\,\mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha,k}(f) 
 
 
 (3.19) 
 
 
 = α min ​ c min ​ α ratio ​ ( | α | + k − 1 ) 54 ​ ( α max + k − 1 ) ​ | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​ ( 6 ​ e ) α max ​ ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 \displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{\rm min}\,c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}\left(|\alpha|+k-1\right)}{{54}\left(\alpha_{\rm max}+k-1\right)|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)\,{(6e)}^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2} 
 
 
 (3.20) 
 
 
 ≥ 1 54 ​ α min ​ c min ​ α ratio | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​ ( 6 ​ e ) α max ​ ‖ f ‖ α , k 2 ​ . \displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{{54}}\frac{\alpha_{\rm min}\,c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}}{|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)\,{(6e)}^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}\left\lVert f\right\rVert_{\alpha,k}^{2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This concludes the proof of the theorem.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 4.  Proof of Theorem  3.2 

 
 Since  G , α , k G,\alpha,k  and  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  are fixed all throughout this section, we abbreviate 
 

 
 (4.1) 
 
 ℰ = ℰ G , α , k ​ ( f ) and ℰ K = ℰ K , α , k ​ ( f ) ​ . \mathcal{E}=\mathcal{E}_{G,\alpha,k}(f)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{E}_{K}=\mathcal{E}_{K,\alpha,k}(f)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Let us assume that an ordering is given on  V V , so that 
 

 
 (4.2) 
 
 ℰ K = ∑ x < y ∑ η ∈ Ξ k μ α , k ​ ( η ) ​ η x ​ ( α y + η y ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( η − δ x + δ y ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) 2 ≕ ∑ x < y ℰ K x , y ​ , \mathcal{E}_{K}=\sum_{x<y}\sum_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\eta)\,\eta_{x}\left(\alpha_{y}+\eta_{y}\right)\left(f(\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})-f(\eta)\right)^{2}\eqqcolon\sum_{x<y}\mathcal{E}_{K}^{x,y}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where, for any pair  x < y x<y ,  ℰ K x , y \mathcal{E}_{K}^{x,y}  is defined as the summation in  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k}  above.
The following lemma gives an upper bound for the last term  ℰ K x , y \mathcal{E}_{K}^{x,y} . 
 
 
 Lemma 4.1 . 
 
 For every pair  x < y x<y , 
 

 
 (4.3) 
 
 ℰ K x , y ≤ 108 ​ k ​ ( α max + k − 1 ) ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​ ( 6 ​ e ) α max α min ​ α ratio ​ c min ​ ℰ ​ . \mathcal{E}_{K}^{x,y}\leq\frac{{108\,k\left(\alpha_{\rm max}+k-1\right){\rm diam}(G)\,(6e)^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}}{\alpha_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}\,c_{\rm min}}\,\mathcal{E}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 We first prove Theorem  3.2  provided that Lemma  4.1  holds true. 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  3.2 . 
 
 Recall the notation from ( 4.1 ).
By ( 4.2 ) and ( 4.3 ), we obtain 
 

 
 (4.4) 
 
 ℰ K \displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{K} 
 ≤ ( | V | 2 ) ​ 108 ​ k ​ ( α max + k − 1 ) ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​ ( 6 ​ e ) α max α min ​ c min ​ α ratio ​ ℰ \displaystyle\leq{|V|\choose 2}\,\frac{108\,k\left(\alpha_{\rm max}+k-1\right){\rm diam}(G)\,(6e)^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}{\alpha_{\rm min}\,c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}}\,\mathcal{E} 
 
 
 (4.5) 
 
 
 ≤ 54 ​ k ​ ( α max + k − 1 ) ​ | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​ ( 6 ​ e ) α max α min ​ c min ​ α ratio ​ ℰ ​ . \displaystyle\leq\frac{54\,k\left(\alpha_{\rm max}+k-1\right)|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)\,(6e)^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}{\alpha_{\rm min}\,c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}}\,\mathcal{E}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This is exactly the claim in Theorem  3.2 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 In the remainder of this section, we fix  x < y x<y  and prove Lemma  4.1 . 
 
 
 For  A ⊆ V A\subseteq V  and  ℓ ∈ ℕ 0 \ell\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , define 
 

 
 (4.6) 
 
 Ξ ℓ A ≔ { σ ∈ Ξ ℓ : ∑ z ∈ A σ z = ℓ } ​ . \Xi_{\ell}^{A}\coloneqq\left\{\sigma\in\Xi_{\ell}:\sum_{{z}\in A}\sigma_{z}=\ell\right\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Note that  Ξ k V = Ξ k \Xi_{k}^{V}=\Xi_{k} . Moreover, for  σ ∈ Ξ ℓ A \sigma\in\Xi_{\ell}^{A} ,  y 1 , … , y s ∈ V y_{1},\ldots,y_{s}\in V , and  m 1 , … , m s ∈ ℕ 0 m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , define 
 

 
 (4.7) 
 
 σ m 1 , … , m s y 1 ⋯ y s ≔ σ + ∑ i = 1 s m i δ y i ∈ Ξ ℓ + m 1 + ⋯ + m s , \sigma_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}}^{y_{1}\cdots y_{s}}\coloneqq\sigma+\sum_{i=1}^{s}m_{i}\,\delta_{y_{i}}\in\Xi_{\ell+m_{1}+\cdots+m_{s}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the right-hand side should be understood as a summation of
functions. Thus, the number of particles in the new configuration  σ m 1 , … , m s y 1 ⋯ y s \sigma_{m_{1},\ldots,m_{s}}^{y_{1}\cdots y_{s}} 
equals  ℓ + m 1 + ⋯ + m s \ell+m_{1}+\cdots+m_{s} . 
 
 
 According to ( 4.7 ), we may decompose the Dirichlet summation  ℰ K x , y \mathcal{E}_{K}^{x,y}  in ( 4.2 ) as 
 

 
 (4.8) 
 
 ∑ ℓ = 1 k ∑ m = 1 ℓ ∑ σ ∈ Ξ k − ℓ V ∖ { x , y } μ α , k ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y ) − f ⁡ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y ) ) 2 ​ , \sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\sigma\in\Xi_{k-\ell}^{V\setminus\{x,y\}}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy})\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)(f(\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy})-f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}))^{2}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ℓ ∈ ⟦ 1 , k ⟧ ≔ [ 1 , k ] ∩ ℤ \ell\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket\coloneqq[1,k]\cap\mathbb{Z}  denotes the number of particles in  { x , y } \{x,y\} , and  m ∈ ⟦ 1 , ℓ ⟧ m\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket  denotes the number of particles at  x x .
For each  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k}  and  x , y ∈ V x,y\in V , define 
 

 
 (4.9) 
 
 𝔠 K ​ ( η , x , y ) ≔ μ α , k ​ ( η ) ​ η x ​ ( α y + η y ) ​ , ∇ K 2 f ​ ( η , x , y ) ≔ 𝔠 K ​ ( η , x , y ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( η − δ x + δ y ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) 2 ​ . {\mathfrak{c}_{K}(\eta;x,y)}\coloneqq\mu_{\alpha,k}(\eta)\,\eta_{x}\left(\alpha_{y}+\eta_{y}\right)\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\eta;x,y)\coloneqq\mathfrak{c}_{K}(\eta;x,y)\,(f(\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})-f(\eta))^{2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 According to this notation, 
 

 
 (4.10) 
 
 ℰ K x , y = ∑ ℓ = 1 k ∑ m = 1 ℓ ∑ σ ∈ Ξ k − ℓ V ∖ { x , y } ∇ K 2 ​ f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ​ . \mathcal{E}_{K}^{x,y}=\sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\sigma\in\Xi_{k-\ell}^{V\setminus\{x,y\}}}\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, the two summations in  ℓ ∈ ⟦ 1 , k ⟧ \ell\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket  and  m ∈ ⟦ 1 , ℓ ⟧ m\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket  count all possible types of jumps from  x x  to  y y , and the summation in  σ ∈ Ξ k − ℓ V ∖ { x , y } \sigma\in\Xi_{k-\ell}^{V\setminus\{x,y\}}  counts the possible particle configuration on the remaining vertices in  V ∖ { x , y } V\setminus\{x,y\} . 
 
 
 Fix a shortest sequence 
 

 
 (4.11) 
 
 x = x 0 , x 1 , … , x t = y x=x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}=y 
 
 
 in  G G  such that  c x s − 1 ​ x s > 0 c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}}>0  for all  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t\rrbracket , where  t ∈ ⟦ 1 , diam ( G ) ⟧ t\in\llbracket 1,{\rm diam}(G)\rrbracket .
For  η , ζ ∈ Ξ k \eta,\zeta\in\Xi_{k} , write 
 

 
 (4.12) 
 
 𝔠 ⁡ ( η , ζ ) ≔ μ α , k ​ ( η ) ​ r α , k ​ ( η , ζ ) and ∇ 2 f ​ ( η , ζ ) ≔ 𝔠 ⁡ ( η , ζ ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( ζ ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) 2 ​ , \mathfrak{c}(\eta,\zeta)\coloneqq\mu_{\alpha,k}(\eta)\,r_{\alpha,k}(\eta,\zeta)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nabla^{2}f(\eta,\zeta)\coloneqq\mathfrak{c}(\eta,\zeta)\left(f(\zeta)-f(\eta)\right)^{2}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  r α , k ​ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) r_{\alpha,k}(\cdot,\cdot)  is the transition rate function of  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha) .
Note that by the reversibility of  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k} ,  ∇ 2 f ​ ( η , ζ ) = ∇ 2 f ​ ( ζ , η ) \nabla^{2}f(\eta,\zeta)=\nabla^{2}f(\zeta,\eta) .
Then, we have 
 

 
 (4.13) 
 
 ℰ = ∑ η ∈ Ξ k ∑ z < w c z ​ w > 0 ∇ 2 f ​ ( η , η − δ z + δ w ) ​ . \mathcal{E}=\sum_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z<w\\
c_{zw}>0\end{subarray}}\nabla^{2}f(\eta,\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 A sequence of configurations  η 0 , η 1 , … , η M \eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{M}  in  Ξ k \Xi_{k}  is a  path  if the transition rates  r α , k ​ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) r_{\alpha,k}(\cdot,\cdot)  are positive along the sequence, i.e.,  r α , k ​ ( η s − 1 , η s ) > 0 r_{\alpha,k}(\eta_{s-1},\eta_{s})>0  for all  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , M ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,M\rrbracket . 
 
 
 The idea to prove Lemma  4.1  is as follows. First, we decompose  ℰ K x , y \mathcal{E}_{K}^{x,y}  according to ( 4.10 ). Then, for each triple  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma) , we upper bound  ∇ K 2 ​ f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}  with a certain collection of terms  ∇ 2 f ​ ( η , η − δ z + δ w ) \nabla^{2}f(\eta,\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w})  that appear in the right-hand side of ( 4.13 ). Finally, we count the number of overlaps for each term  ∇ 2 f ​ ( η , η − δ z + δ w ) \nabla^{2}f(\eta,\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w})  for all such triple  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma) , which gives an upper bound of  ℰ K x . y \mathcal{E}_{K}^{x.y}  in terms of  ℰ \mathcal{E} . 
 
 
 The procedure of upper bounding each  ∇ K 2 ​ f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}  depends on the detailed distribution of particles along the sequence ( 4.11 ). In the following four subsections, Sections  4.1 ,  4.2 ,  4.3  and  4.4 , we demonstrate each simple cases, and finally in Section  4.5  we deal with the general case and conclude the proof of Lemma  4.1 . 
 
 
 4.1.  Connected case 

 
 First, suppose that (cf. ( 4.11 )) 
 

 
 (4.14) 
 
 c x ​ y > 0 ​ , which implies that ​ t = 1 ​ . c_{xy}>0\;\text{,}\;\;\quad{\text{which implies that}}\ t=1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Define 
 

 
 (4.15) 
 
 Ω x , y ℓ , m , σ ≔ { { σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y } } ​ , {\Omega_{x,y}^{\ell,m,\sigma}}\coloneqq{\,\Big\{}\{\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy},\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}\}{\Big\}\,}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which is the singleton set that contains the edge  { σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y } \{\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy},\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}\} . When there is no room for confusion, we simply write  Ω = Ω x , y ℓ , m , σ \Omega=\Omega_{x,y}^{\ell,m,\sigma} . 
 
 
 Lemma 4.2 . 
 
 Suppose that ( 4.14 ) holds. Then, we have 
 

 
 (4.16) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ≤ m ⁡ ( α y + ℓ − m ) c min ​ α min ​ ∑ { ζ , ζ ′ } ∈ Ω ∇ 2 f ​ ( ζ , ζ ′ ) ​ . {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}\leq{\frac{m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm min}}}\sum_{\{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\}{\in}\Omega}\nabla^{2}f(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It is clear that  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy},\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}  is itself a path, and 
 

 
 (4.17) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) = 1 c x ​ y ​ ∇ 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y ) = 1 c x ​ y ​ ∑ { ζ , ζ ′ } ∈ Ω ∇ 2 f ​ ( ζ , ζ ′ ) ​ . {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}=\frac{1}{c_{xy}}\,\nabla^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy},\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy})=\frac{1}{c_{xy}}\,\sum_{\{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\}\in\Omega}\nabla^{2}f(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, the lemma easily follows since  c x ​ y ≥ c min c_{xy}\geq c_{\rm min}  and  m ⁡ ( α y + ℓ − m ) ≥ α y ≥ α min m\,(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m)\geq\alpha_{y}\geq\alpha_{\rm min} .
∎

 (4.18)

 Additionally, in this subsection, assume that all sites along the sequence ( 4.11 ) are occupied by particles of  σ \sigma , i.e., 
 

 
 (4.19)

 Let  Ω = Ω x , y ℓ , m , σ \Omega={\Omega_{x,y}^{\ell,m,\sigma}}  be the collection of all edges  { σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x s − 1 ​ y , σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x s ​ y } \{\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{s}y}\}  for all  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t\rrbracket , i.e., 
 

 
 (4.21)

 With this notation, we prove in this subsection the following lemma. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.3 . 
 
 Assume that the conditions in ( 4.18 ) and ( 4.19 ) hold. Then, we have 
 

 
 (4.22) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ≤ t ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) ​ ( 1 + α max ) c min ​ α min ​ ∑ { ζ , ζ ′ } ∈ Ω ∇ 2 f ​ ( ζ , ζ ′ ) ​ . {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}\leq{\frac{t\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)(1+\alpha_{\rm max})}{c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm min}}}\sum_{\{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\}{\in}\Omega}\nabla^{2}f(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By Cauchy–Schwarz inequality, for any path  η = η 0 , η 1 , … , η M = ζ \eta=\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{M}=\zeta , we have (cf. ( 4.12 )) 
 

 
 (4.23) 
 
 ( f ⁡ ( ζ ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) 2 ≤ ( ∑ i = 1 M ∇ 2 f ​ ( η i − 1 , η i ) ) ​ ( ∑ i = 1 M 1 𝔠 ⁡ ( η i − 1 , η i ) ) ​ . (f(\zeta)-f(\eta))^{2}\leq\left(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\nabla^{2}f(\eta_{i-1},\eta_{i})\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{M}\frac{1}{\mathfrak{c}(\eta_{i-1},\eta_{i})}\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, applying ( 4.23 ) along the path ( 4.20 ), we get (cf. ( 4.9 )) 
 
 
 
 (4.24) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ≤ ( ∑ s = 1 t ∇ 2 f ​ ( CLOSE CLOSE \displaystyle{\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}\leq\,\Bigg(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\nabla^{2}f( 
 OPEN OPEN σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x s − 1 ​ y , σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x s ​ y ) ) \displaystyle\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{s}y})\Bigg)\, 
 
 
 
 
 × ( ∑ s = 1 t μ α , k ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) 𝔠 ⁡ ( σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x s − 1 ​ y , σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x s ​ y ) ) ​ . \displaystyle\times\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\frac{\mu_{\alpha,k}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy})\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{\mathfrak{c}(\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{s}y})}\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 For  x ∈ V x\in V  and  m ∈ ℕ 0 m\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , define 
 

 
 (4.25) 
 
 π x ​ ( m ) ≔ Γ ⁡ ( α x + m ) Γ ⁡ ( α x ) ​ m ! ​ , such that ​ μ α , k ​ ( η ) = 1 Z α , k ​ ∏ x ∈ V π x ​ ( η x ) ​ . \pi_{x}(m)\coloneqq\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+m)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x})\,m!}\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{such that}\ \mu_{\alpha,k}(\eta)=\frac{1}{Z_{\alpha,k}}\prod_{x\in V}\pi_{x}(\eta_{x})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  Γ ⁡ ( ⋅ ) \Gamma(\cdot)  denotes the usual gamma function. We now collect some basic properties. 
 
 
 Lemma 4.4 . 
 
 We have, for each  m ∈ ℕ 0 m\in\mathbb{N}_{0} , 
 

 
 (4.26) 
 
 α x ≤ π x ​ ( m ) ​ ( α x + m ) = π x ​ ( m + 1 ) ​ ( m + 1 ) ≤ α x ​ e α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m 1 j ​ . \alpha_{x}\leq\pi_{x}(m)\left(\alpha_{x}+m\right)=\pi_{x}(m+1)\left(m+1\right)\leq\alpha_{x}\,e^{\alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{1}{j}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Moreover, for  m ≥ m ′ ≥ 1 m\geq m^{\prime}\geq 1 , 
 

 
 (4.27) 
 
 1 ≤ π x ​ ( m ) ​ m π x ​ ( m ′ ) ​ m ′ ≤ e α x ​ ∑ j = m ′ m − 1 1 j ​ . 1\leq\frac{\pi_{x}(m)\,m}{\pi_{x}(m^{\prime})\,m^{\prime}}\leq e^{\alpha_{x}\sum_{j=m^{\prime}}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Since  Γ ⁡ ( a + 1 ) = a ​ Γ ​ ( a ) \Gamma(a+1)=a\,\Gamma(a)  for all  a > 0 a>0 , 
 

 
 (4.28) 
 
 π x ​ ( m ) ​ ( α x + m ) = Γ ⁡ ( α x + m + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( α x ) ​ m ! = π x ​ ( m + 1 ) ​ ( m + 1 ) ​ . \pi_{x}(m)\left(\alpha_{x}+m\right)=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+m+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x})\,m!}=\pi_{x}(m+1)\left(m+1\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Clearly, since  α x > 0 \alpha_{x}>0 , 
 

 
 (4.29) 
 
 Γ ⁡ ( α x + m + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( α x ) ​ m ! = α x ​ ∏ j = 1 m j + α x j ≥ α x ​ . \frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+m+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x})\,m!}=\alpha_{x}\,\prod_{j=1}^{m}\frac{j+\alpha_{x}}{j}\geq\alpha_{x}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 On the other hand, since  1 + a ≤ e a 1+a\leq e^{a}  for any  a ∈ ℝ a\in\mathbb{R} , 
 

 
 (4.30) 
 
 Γ ⁡ ( α x + m + 1 ) Γ ⁡ ( α x ) ​ m ! = α x ​ ∏ j = 1 m ( 1 + α x j ) ≤ α x ​ exp ⁡ ( ∑ j = 1 m α x j ) ​ . \frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+m+1)}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x})\,m!}=\alpha_{x}\,\prod_{j=1}^{m}\left(1+\frac{\alpha_{x}}{j}\right)\leq\alpha_{x}\,\exp\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}\frac{\alpha_{x}}{j}\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Combining the three displayed identities proves ( 4.26 ). In addition, for  m ≥ m ′ ≥ 1 m\geq m^{\prime}\geq 1 , 
 

 
 (4.31) 
 
 π x ​ ( m ) ​ m π x ​ ( m ′ ) ​ m ′ = Γ ⁡ ( α x + m ) / ( m − 1 ) ! Γ ⁡ ( α x + m ′ ) / ( m ′ − 1 ) ! = ∏ j = m ′ m − 1 j + α x j ​ , \frac{\pi_{x}(m)\,m}{\pi_{x}(m^{\prime})\,m^{\prime}}=\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+m)/(m-1)!}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+m^{\prime})/(m^{\prime}-1)!}=\prod_{j=m^{\prime}}^{m-1}\frac{j+\alpha_{x}}{j}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which lies inside the interval  [ 1 , exp ⁡ ( ∑ j = m ′ m − 1 α x j ) ] [1,\exp\,(\sum_{j=m^{\prime}}^{m-1}\frac{\alpha_{x}}{j})]  due to the same reasoning as above. This proves ( 4.27 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 Returning to the proof of Lemma  4.3 , let us look at the term inside the second parenthesis in ( 4.24 ). The term for  s = 1 s=1  equals 
 

 
 (4.32) 
 
 μ α , k ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) 𝔠 ⁡ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x 1 ​ y ) = m ⁡ ( α y + ℓ − m ) r α , k ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x 1 ​ y ) = α y + ℓ − m ( α x 1 + σ x 1 ) ​ c x ​ x 1 ​ . \frac{\mu_{\alpha,k}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy})\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{\mathfrak{c}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy},\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{1}y})}=\frac{m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{r_{\alpha,k}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy},\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{1}y})}=\frac{\alpha_{y}+\ell-m}{\left(\alpha_{x_{1}}+\sigma_{x_{1}}\right)c_{xx_{1}}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Similarly, by ( 4.25 ), the term for  s = t s=t  becomes 
 

 
 (4.33) 
 
 π x ​ ( m ) ​ π x t − 1 ​ ( σ x t − 1 ) ​ m π x ​ ( m − 1 ) ​ π x t − 1 ​ ( σ x t − 1 + 1 ) ​ ( σ x t − 1 + 1 ) ​ c x t − 1 ​ y ​ , \frac{\pi_{x}(m)\,\pi_{x_{t-1}}(\sigma_{x_{t-1}})\,m}{\pi_{x}(m-1)\,\pi_{x_{t-1}}(\sigma_{x_{t-1}}+1)\,(\sigma_{x_{t-1}}+1)\,c_{x_{t-1}y}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and the term for  s ∈ ⟦ 2 , t − 1 ⟧ s\in\llbracket 2,t-1\rrbracket  becomes 
 

 
 (4.34) 
 
 π x ​ ( m ) ​ π x s − 1 ​ ( σ x s − 1 ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) π x ​ ( m − 1 ) ​ π x s − 1 ​ ( σ x s − 1 + 1 ) ​ ( σ x s − 1 + 1 ) ​ ( α x s + σ x s ) ​ c x s − 1 ​ x s ​ . \frac{\pi_{x}(m)\,\pi_{x_{s-1}}(\sigma_{x_{s-1}})\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{\pi_{x}(m-1)\,\pi_{x_{s-1}}(\sigma_{x_{s-1}}+1)\,(\sigma_{x_{s-1}}+1)\left(\alpha_{x_{s}}+\sigma_{x_{s}}\right)c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Combining the last three displayed identities,

the summation inside the second parenthesis in ( 4.24 ) equals 
 
 
 
 (4.35) 
 
 
 α y + ℓ − m ( α x 1 + σ x 1 ) ​ c x ​ x 1 + π x ​ ( m ) ​ π x t − 1 ​ ( σ x t − 1 ) ​ m π x ​ ( m − 1 ) ​ π x t − 1 ​ ( σ x t − 1 + 1 ) ​ ( σ x t − 1 + 1 ) ​ c x t − 1 ​ y \displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{y}+\ell-m}{\left(\alpha_{x_{1}}+\sigma_{x_{1}}\right)c_{xx_{1}}}+\frac{\pi_{x}(m)\,\pi_{x_{t-1}}(\sigma_{x_{t-1}})\,m}{\pi_{x}(m-1)\,\pi_{x_{t-1}}(\sigma_{x_{t-1}}+1)\,(\sigma_{x_{t-1}}+1)\,c_{x_{t-1}y}} 
 
 
 
 
 + ∑ s = 2 t − 1 π x ​ ( m ) ​ π x s − 1 ​ ( σ x s − 1 ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) π x ​ ( m − 1 ) ​ π x s − 1 ​ ( σ x s − 1 + 1 ) ​ ( σ x s − 1 + 1 ) ​ ( α x s + σ x s ) ​ c x s − 1 ​ x s . \displaystyle+\sum_{s=2}^{t-1}\frac{\pi_{x}(m)\,\pi_{x_{s-1}}(\sigma_{x_{s-1}})\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{\pi_{x}(m-1)\,\pi_{x_{s-1}}(\sigma_{x_{s-1}}+1)\,(\sigma_{x_{s-1}}+1)\left(\alpha_{x_{s}}+\sigma_{x_{s}}\right)c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 The first term in ( 4.35 ) is easily bounded by (cf. ( 2.7 ))

 where we used that  σ x 1 ≥ 1 \sigma_{x_{1}}\geq 1 . By ( 4.25 ), the second term in ( 4.35 ) reads as 
 

 
 (4.37)

 where we used  σ x t − 1 ≥ 1 \sigma_{x_{t-1}}\geq 1  and  α y + ℓ − m ≥ α min \alpha_{y}+\ell-m\geq\alpha_{\rm min}  at the first inequality and  α x + m − 1 ≤ m ⁡ ( 1 + α x ) \alpha_{x}+m-1\leq m(1+\alpha_{x})  at the second inequality.  where we used that  σ x s − 1 ≥ 1 \sigma_{x_{s-1}}\geq 1  and  α x s + σ x s ≥ α min {\alpha_{x_{s}}+\sigma_{x_{s}}\geq\alpha_{\rm min}}  for all  s ∈ ⟦ 2 , t − 1 ⟧ s\in\llbracket 2,t-1\rrbracket . Therefore, inserting these three bounds and ( 4.35 ) into ( 4.24 ), we conclude that 
 

 
 (4.39) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ≤ t ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) ​ ( 1 + α max ) c min ​ α min ​ ( ∑ s = 1 t ∇ 2 f ​ ( σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x s − 1 ​ y , σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x s ​ y ) ) ​ . {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}\leq{\frac{t\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)(1+\alpha_{\rm max})}{c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm min}}}\left(\sum_{s=1}^{t}\nabla^{2}f(\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{s-1}y},\sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{s}y})\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This proves Lemma  4.3 .

 4.3.  Decomposition of gradients: empty sites & few particles 

 
 Next, assume ( 4.18 ) and that sites  x 1 , … , x t − 1 x_{1},\dots,x_{t-1}  are empty with respect to  σ \sigma , i.e., 
 

 
 (4.40)

 In this case, we divide the analysis into two parts. In Section  4.3 , assume that 
 

 
 (4.41) 
 
 α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j ≤ 1 ​ . \alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}\leq 1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 We construct a path from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}  to  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy} 
as follows (see also Figure  4.2 ): 
 
 
 
 (F)  
 
 Move  m m  particles from  x s − 1 x_{s-1}  to  x s x_{s}  consecutively, for each  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t − 1 ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t-1\rrbracket . This subpath starts from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy} , visits  σ m − i , i , ℓ − m x s − 1 ​ x s ​ y \sigma_{m-i,i,\ell-m}^{x_{s-1}x_{s}y}  for each  i ∈ ⟦ 0 , m ⟧ i\in\llbracket 0,m\rrbracket  and  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t − 1 ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t-1\rrbracket , and finally arrives at  σ m , ℓ − m x t − 1 ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{x_{t-1}y} . 
 
 (S)  
 
 Move a particle from  x t − 1 x_{t-1}  to  y y . The resulting configuration
is  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x t − 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{x_{t-1}y} . If  m = 1 m=1 , the path is complete. 
 
 (B)  
 
 If  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , move the remaining  m − 1 m-1  particles from  x s x_{s}  to  x s − 1 x_{s-1} 
consecutively, for each  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t − 1 ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t-1\rrbracket  (backwards). This subpath starts from  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x t − 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{x_{t-1}y} , visits  σ m − i , i − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x s − 1 ​ x s ​ y \sigma_{m-i,i-1,\ell-m+1}^{x_{s-1}x_{s}y}  for each  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , m ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket  and  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t − 1 ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t-1\rrbracket , thereby arrives at  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}  as desired.
