Recall ( 4.9 ). In this subsection, we prove the following lemma: 
 
 
 Lemma 4.5 . 
 
 Assume that ( 4.18 ) holds, and suppose that  ℓ ∈ ⟦ 1 , k ⟧ \ell\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket ,  m ∈ ⟦ 1 , ℓ ⟧ m\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket  and  σ ∈ Ξ k − ℓ V ∖ { x , y } \sigma\in\Xi_{k-\ell}^{V\setminus\{x,y\}}  satisfy ( 4.40 ) and ( 4.41 ). Then, 
 

 
 (4.42) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ≤ 6 ​ t ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) c min ​ α min ​ α ratio ​ ( ∑ { ζ , ζ ′ } ∈ Ω ∇ 2 f ​ ( ζ , ζ ′ ) ) ​ . {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}\leq\frac{{6t\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}}{c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}}\left(\sum_{\{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\}{\in}\Omega}\nabla^{2}f(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Applying ( 4.23 ) along the path constructed above, we have the following upper bound for  ∇ K 2 ​ f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)} : 
 

 
 (4.43) 
 
 ( ∑ { η , ζ } ∈ Ω ∇ 2 f ​ ( η , ζ ) ) ​ ( ∑ { η , ζ } ∈ Ω μ α , k ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) 𝔠 ⁡ ( η , ζ ) ) ​ . \left(\sum_{\{\eta,\zeta\}{\in}\Omega}\nabla^{2}f(\eta,\zeta)\right)\left(\sum_{\{\eta,\zeta\}{\in}\Omega}\frac{\mu_{\alpha,k}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy})\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{\mathfrak{c}(\eta,\zeta)}\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here, we expand the term in the second parenthesis as done exactly in ( 4.35 ). Namely,
referring to the definition of  Ω \Omega  and ( 4.25 ), the term inside the second parenthesis in ( 4.43 ) expands as 
 
 
 
 (4.44) 
 
 
 ∑ s = 1 t − 1 ∑ i = 0 m − 1 π x ​ ( m ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) π x s − 1 ​ ( m − i ) ​ π x s ​ ( i ) ​ ( m − i ) ​ ( α x s + i ) ​ c x s − 1 ​ x s + π x ​ ( m ) π x t − 1 ​ ( m ) ​ c x t − 1 ​ y \displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\frac{\pi_{x}(m)\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{\pi_{x_{s-1}}(m-i)\,\pi_{x_{s}}(i)\,(m-i)\left(\alpha_{x_{s}}+i\right)c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}}}+\frac{\pi_{x}(m)}{\pi_{x_{t-1}}(m)\,c_{x_{t-1}y}} 
 
 
 
 
 + ∑ s = 1 t − 1 ∑ i = 2 m π x ​ ( m ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) π x s − 1 ​ ( m − i ) ​ π x s ​ ( i − 1 ) ​ ( i − 1 ) ​ ( α x s − 1 + m − i ) ​ c x s ​ x s − 1 . \displaystyle+\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\sum_{i=2}^{m}\frac{\pi_{x}(m)\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{\pi_{x_{s-1}}(m-i)\,\pi_{x_{s}}(i-1)\left(i-1\right)(\alpha_{x_{s-1}}+m-i)\,c_{x_{s}x_{s-1}}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here, the first double summation indicates the forward jumps in  (F) , the second single term indicates the jump in  (S) , and the last double summation indicates the backward jumps in  (B) .
First, consider the first (double) summation in ( 4.44 ).

According to ( 4.26 ) in Lemma  4.4 , 
 

 
 (4.45) 
 
 π x ​ ( m ) ​ m ≤ α x ​ e α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j ​ , π x s − 1 ​ ( m − i ) ​ ( m − i ) ≥ α x s − 1 ​ , π x s ​ ( i ) ​ ( α x s + i ) ≥ α x s ​ . \pi_{x}(m)\,m\leq\alpha_{x}\,e^{\alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}}\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\pi_{x_{s-1}}(m-i)\,(m-i)\geq\alpha_{x_{s-1}}\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\pi_{x_{s}}(i)\,(\alpha_{x_{s}}+i)\geq\alpha_{x_{s}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  c x s − 1 ​ x s ≥ c min c_{x_{s-1}x_{s}}\geq c_{\rm min}  from ( 2.7 ), we may bound the first double summation in ( 4.44 ) from above by 
 

 
 (4.46) 
 
 1 c min ​ ∑ s = 1 t − 1 ∑ i = 0 m − 1 α x ​ e α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) α x s − 1 ​ α x s ≤ e ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) ​ ( t − 1 ) c min ​ α min ​ α ratio ​ , \frac{1}{c_{\rm min}}\sum_{s=1}^{t-1}\sum_{i=0}^{m-1}\frac{\alpha_{x}\,e^{\alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}}\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}{\alpha_{x_{s-1}}\,\alpha_{x_{s}}}\leq\frac{e\,{m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)(t-1)}}{c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where we used that  α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j ≤ 1 \alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}\leq 1  from ( 4.41 ),

and  α x s − 1 ≥ α min \alpha_{x_{s-1}}\geq\alpha_{\rm min} .
Next, the second term in ( 4.44 ) can be similarly bounded as 
 

 
 (4.47)

 Similarly, the third (double) summation in ( 4.44 ) is bounded by 
 

 
 (4.48)

 Collecting ( 4.44 ), ( 4.46 ), ( 4.47 ) and ( 4.48 ),
and applying that  e ≤ 3 e\leq 3 ,

 (4.49) 
 
 9 ​ t ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) c min ​ α min ​ α ratio ​ . \frac{{9t\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}}{c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This concludes the proof of Lemma  4.5 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.4.  Decomposition of gradients: empty sites & many particles 

 
 In this subsection, we assume that ( 4.18 ), ( 4.40 ) hold and that (cf. ( 4.41 )) 
 

 
 (4.50) 
 
 α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j > 1 ​ . \alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}>1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 In this case, constructing a single one-dimensional path is insufficient to bound the term  ∇ K 2 ​ f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}  properly. The main reason is that in the inequality in ( 4.46 ), we are not able to bound the term  e α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j e^{\alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}}  with a constant in this new regime ( 4.50 ). Having in mind that this term essentially comes from the mechanism that all  m m  particles at  x x  move together, we now avoid this obstacle by sending only a limited amount of particles along the path. This replacement costs us an additional  α x − 1 \alpha_{x}^{-1}  term in the upper bound, which is now manageable by the new condition  α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j > 1 \alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}>1 ; we refer to ( 4.73 ) for the exact place where this point is effectively exploited. 
 
 
 Define a new integer  m ~ \widetilde{m}  as

 Figure 4.3.  Two-dimensional system of paths from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}  (top left)
to  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}  (top right) explained in Section  4.4 . Double arrows indicate
series of consecutive paths. Moreover, the blue (resp. red) numbers indicate the corresponding value of the flow  φ \varphi  along the path in the vertical (resp. horizontal) direction, as defined in ( 4.56 ) and ( 4.57 ). The values are chosen so as to ensure that  φ \varphi  is a unit flow from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}  to  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy} . 
 
 
 In Figure  4.3 , the downward vertical arrows starting from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}  in the top left corner denote the  m ~ \widetilde{m}  consecutive particle jumps from  x x  to  x 1 x_{1} . Then, for  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , m ~ ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,\widetilde{m}\rrbracket , the  i i -th bold arrow on the left, from  σ m − i , i , ℓ − m x ​ x 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-i,i,\ell-m}^{xx_{1}y}  to  σ m − i , i , ℓ − m x ​ x t − 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-i,i,\ell-m}^{xx_{t-1}y} , denote the movements of  i i  particles from  x s − 1 x_{s-1}  to  x s x_{s}  consecutively for each  s ∈ ⟦ 2 , t − 1 ⟧ s\in\llbracket 2,t-1\rrbracket , as in mechanism  (F)  in Section  4.3 . Next, the middle horizontal arrows denote the single particle jump from  x t − 1 x_{t-1}  to  y y , corresponding to mechanism  (S)  in Section  4.3 . The bold arrows in the right part denote the movements of  i − 1 i-1  particles from  x t − 1 x_{t-1}  back to  x 1 x_{1}  for each  i ∈ ⟦ 2 , m ~ ⟧ i\in\llbracket 2,\widetilde{m}\rrbracket  (backwards), as in mechanism  (B)  in Section  4.3 . Finally, the upward vertical arrows below  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}  in the top right corner denote the  m ~ − 1 \widetilde{m}-1  consecutive particle jumps from  x 1 x_{1}  to  x x .
The values attached to the arrows, roughly speaking, play the role of weights (encoded as the value of the flow, see below) for each move. 
 
 
 As before, let  Ω = Ω x , y ℓ , m , σ \Omega=\Omega_{x,y}^{\ell,m,\sigma}  be the collection of all edges that appear in Figure  4.3 . The objective of this subsection is to prove the following lemma: 
 
 
 Lemma 4.6 . 
 
 Assume that ( 4.18 ) holds, and suppose that  ℓ ∈ ⟦ 1 , k ⟧ \ell\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket ,  m ∈ ⟦ 1 , ℓ ⟧ m\in\llbracket 1,\ell\rrbracket 
and  σ ∈ Ξ k − ℓ V ∖ { x , y } \sigma\in\Xi_{k-\ell}^{V\setminus\{x,y\}}  satisfy ( 4.40 ) and ( 4.50 ).
Then, we have 
 

 
 (4.52) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ≤ 4 ​ t ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) ​  6 α max α min ​ α ratio ​ c min ​ ( ∑ { ζ , ζ ′ } ∈ Ω ∇ 2 f ​ ( ζ , ζ ′ ) ) ​ . {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}\leq\frac{{4t\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)}\,6^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}{\alpha_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}\,c_{\rm min}}\left(\sum_{\{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\}{\in}\Omega}\nabla^{2}f(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 To derive an estimate similar to ( 4.43 ) that works also in this two-dimensional setting, we make use of some potential-theoretic arguments. We present the statements as concisely as possible and refer the readers to, e.g.,  [ BH15 , Chapter 7]  or  [ Kim25 , Appendix A]  for more details about the objects used in this subsection. 
 
 
 Only in this subsection, we restrict the original system  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha)  to the subset of the configurations that appear in Figure  4.3 , which we denote by  Ξ ^ \hat{\Xi} . Moreover, let  𝒟 Ω ​ ( g ) \mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(g)  denote the (normalized) Dirichlet form in  Ξ ^ k {\hat{\Xi}_{k}} 
given by (cf. ( 4.12 )) 
 

 
 (4.53) 
 
 𝒟 Ω ​ ( g ) = 1 2 ​ ∑ η , ζ ∈ Ξ ^ k μ α , k ​ ( η ) ​ r α , k ​ ( η , ζ ) ​ ( g ⁡ ( ζ ) − g ⁡ ( η ) ) 2 = ∑ { η , ζ } ∈ Ω ∇ 2 g ​ ( η , ζ ) ​ . \mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(g)=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\eta,\zeta\in{\hat{\Xi}_{k}}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\eta)\,r_{\alpha,k}(\eta,\zeta)\left(g(\zeta)-g(\eta)\right)^{2}=\sum_{\{\eta,\zeta\}{\in}\Omega}\nabla^{2}g(\eta,\zeta)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 For non-empty disjoint subsets  A , B A,B  of  Ω \Omega , introduce (e.g.,  [ BH15 , Theorem 7.33]  or  [ Kim25 , Proposition A.1] ) 
 

 
 (4.54) 
 
 cap ⁡ ( A , B ) ≔ inf { 𝒟 Ω ​ ( g ) : g ∈ ℝ Ξ ^ k ​ such that ​ g = 1 ​ on ​ A ​ and ​ g = 0 ​ on ​ B } ​ , {\rm cap}(A,B)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(g):g\in\mathbb{R}^{{\hat{\Xi}_{k}}}\ \text{such that}\ g=1\ \text{on}\ A\ \text{and}\ g=0\ \text{on}\ B\right\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 the capacity between  A A  and  B B . In particular, for  A = { σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y } A=\{\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}\}  and  B = { σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y } B=\{\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}\} , we have, after shifting and renormalizing, 
 

 
 (4.55) 
 
 cap ⁡ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y ) − f ⁡ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y ) ) 2 ≤ 𝒟 Ω ​ ( f ) ​ , {\rm cap}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy},\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy})\,(f(\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy})-f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}))^{2}\leq\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(f)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  is the function which we fixed at the beginning of Section  4 . 
 
 
 Proof of Lemma  4.6 . 
 
 Let us describe the flow  φ \varphi  on  Ξ ^ k {\hat{\Xi}_{k}}  represented in Figure  4.3 , where we recall that “flow on  Ω \Omega ” refers to any antisymmetric real-valued function on  Ξ ^ k × Ξ ^ k {\hat{\Xi}_{k}}\times{\hat{\Xi}_{k}} . Due to this antisymmetry, we shall omit to specify the value of  φ \varphi  when exchanging the arguments. Hence, vertically, define 
 
 
 
 (4.56) 
 
 φ ⁡ ( σ m − i , i , ℓ − m x ​ x 1 ​ y , σ m − i − 1 , i + 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x 1 ​ y ) \displaystyle\varphi(\sigma_{m-i,i,\ell-m}^{xx_{1}y},\sigma_{m-i-1,i+1,\ell-m}^{xx_{1}y}) 
 ≔ ∑ j = i + 1 m ~ 1 j ∑ j = 1 m ~ 1 j ​ , \displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{j=i+1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j}}{\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 for i ∈ ⟦ 0 , m ~ − 1 ⟧ , \displaystyle\text{for}\ i\in\llbracket 0,\widetilde{m}-1\rrbracket\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 
 φ ⁡ ( σ m − i − 1 , i , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ x 1 ​ y , σ m − i , i − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ x 1 ​ y ) \displaystyle\varphi(\sigma_{m-i-1,i,\ell-m+1}^{x{x_{1}}y},\sigma_{m-i,i-1,\ell-m+1}^{x{x_{1}}y}) 
 ≔ ∑ j = i + 1 m ~ 1 j ∑ j = 1 m ~ 1 j ​ , \displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\sum_{j=i+1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j}}{\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 for i ∈ ⟦ 1 , m ~ − 1 ⟧ . \displaystyle\text{for}\ i\in\llbracket 1,\widetilde{m}-1\rrbracket\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Horizontally, define  φ \varphi  as a constant flow at each  i i -th vertical level from  σ m − i , i , ℓ − m x ​ x 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-i,i,\ell-m}^{xx_{1}y}  to  σ m − i , i − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ x 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-i,i-1,\ell-m+1}^{xx_{1}y}  with value 
 

 
 (4.57) 
 
 1 i ∑ j = 1 m ~ 1 j , for each i ∈ ⟦ 1 , m ~ ⟧ . \frac{\frac{1}{i}}{\sum_{j=1}^{\widetilde{m}}\frac{1}{j}}\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{for each}\ i\in\llbracket 1,\widetilde{m}\rrbracket\;\text{.} 
 
 
 The function  φ \varphi  is a unit flow from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}  to  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy} . Indeed, letting  ( div ​ φ ) ​ ( η ) ({\rm div}\,\varphi)(\eta)  denote the net divergence at  η \eta , i.e.,  ( div ​ φ ) ​ ( η ) ≔ ∑ ζ ∈ Ω φ ⁡ ( η , ζ ) ({\rm div}\,\varphi)(\eta)\coloneqq\sum_{\zeta\in\Omega}\varphi(\eta,\zeta) , we have 
 

 
 (4.58) 
 
 ( div ​ φ ) ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y ) = 1 ​ , ( div ​ φ ) ​ ( σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y ) = − 1 and div ​ φ = 0 otherwise ​ . ({\rm div}\,\varphi)(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy})=1\;\text{,}\;\;\quad({\rm div}\,\varphi)(\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy})=-1\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm div}\,\varphi=0\quad\text{otherwise}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, the standard Thomson principle (e.g.,  [ BH15 , Theorem 7.37]  or  [ Kim25 , Proposition A.2] ) indicates that 
 

 
 (4.59) 
 
 cap ⁡ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y ) ≥ 1 ‖ φ ‖ 2 ​ , {\rm cap}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy},\sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy})\geq\frac{1}{\|\varphi\|^{2}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ‖ φ ‖ 2 \|\varphi\|^{2}  is the (square) flow norm given by 
 

 
 (4.60) 
 
 ‖ φ ‖ 2 ≔ ∑ { η , ζ } ∈ Ω φ ​ ( η , ζ ) 2 𝔠 ⁡ ( η , ζ ) ​ . \|\varphi\|^{2}\coloneqq\sum_{{\{\eta,\zeta\}}\in\Omega}\frac{\varphi(\eta,\zeta)^{2}}{\mathfrak{c}(\eta,\zeta)}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, by ( 4.9 ), ( 4.55 ) and ( 4.59 ), we obtain 
 

 
 (4.61) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ≤ 𝒟 Ω ​ ( f ) ​ μ α , k ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y ) ​ m ​ ( α y + ℓ − m ) ​ ‖ φ ‖ 2 ​ . {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}\leq\mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(f)\,\mu_{\alpha,k}(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy})\,m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)\|\varphi\|^{2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Next, we upper bound the right-hand side of ( 4.61 ), except  𝒟 Ω ​ ( f ) \mathcal{D}_{\Omega}(f) . For this purpose, we decompose  φ = φ 1 + φ 2 \varphi=\varphi_{1}+\varphi_{2} , where  φ 1 \varphi_{1}  (resp.  φ 2 \varphi_{2} ) indicates the vertical (resp. horizontal) part of  φ \varphi . First, the (square) flow norm of the vertical part  φ 1 \varphi_{1}  reads as 
 

 
 (4.62)

 The constant  4 ​ t 4t  appears because the term in ( 4.73 ) should be counted twice to deal with the first and third lines in ( 4.69 ), and the term in ( 4.74 ) corresponds to the second line in ( 4.69 ), thus  2 × 2 ​ ( t − 2 ) + 2 = 4 ​ t − 6 2\times 2(t-2)+2=4t-6 .
Since  φ 1 \varphi_{1}  and  φ 2 \varphi_{2}  have disjoint supports, we have  ‖ φ ‖ 2 = ‖ φ 1 ‖ 2 + ‖ φ 2 ‖ 2 \|\varphi\|^{2}=\|\varphi_{1}\|^{2}+\|\varphi_{2}\|^{2} . Therefore, by ( 4.68 ) and ( 4.75 ), since  α ratio ≤ 1 \alpha_{\rm ratio}\leq 1 , we conclude that

 Collecting ( 4.53 ), ( 4.61 ) and ( 4.76 ), we arrive at conclusion of Lemma  4.6 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 4.5.  General case and proof of Theorem  3.2 

 
 Now, we handle the general case without the restrictions imposed in the previous four subsections.
The idea is to send a particle from  x x  to  y y  along the sequence  x = x 0 , x 1 , … , x t = y x=x_{0},x_{1},\ldots,x_{t}=y 
by obeying the following two criteria: 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 If consecutive sites are occupied by particles in  σ \sigma , send a single particle along these occupied sites as explained in Section  4.2 . 
 
 •  
 
 If consecutive sites are empty for the configuration  σ \sigma , follow the
mechanism explained in Sections  4.3  or  4.4  depending
on the size of the stack of particles just before these empty sites. 
 
 
 
 
 To state this procedure in a rigorous manner, we decompose  ⟦ 0 , t ⟧ {\llbracket 0,t\rrbracket}  as 
 

 
 (4.77) 
 
 ⟦ 0 , b 1 − 1 ⟧ ∪ ⟦ b 1 , a 2 − 1 ⟧ ∪ ⋯ ∪ ⟦ b r − 1 , a r − 1 ⟧ ∪ ⟦ a r , t ⟧ ≕ O 1 ∪ E 1 ∪ ⋯ ∪ E r − 1 ∪ O r , \llbracket{0},b_{1}-1\rrbracket\cup\llbracket b_{1},a_{2}-1\rrbracket\cup\cdots\cup{\llbracket b_{r-1},a_{r}-1\rrbracket\cup\llbracket a_{r},t\rrbracket}\eqqcolon O_{1}\cup E_{1}\cup\cdots\cup{E_{r-1}\cup O_{r}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  1 ≤ b 1 < a 2 < b 2 < ⋯ < b r − 1 < a r ≤ t 1\leq b_{1}<a_{2}<b_{2}<\cdots<b_{r-1}<a_{r}\leq t  and 
 

 
 (4.78) 
 
 { σ x s ≥ 1 if s ∈ O j for some j ∈ ⟦ 1 , r ⟧ , except for s = t σ x s = 0 if s ∈ E j for some j ∈ ⟦ 1 , r − 1 ⟧ . \begin{cases}\sigma_{x_{s}}\geq 1&\text{if}\ s\in O_{j}\ \text{for some}\ j\in\llbracket 1,r\rrbracket{{,\ \text{except for}\ s=t}}\\
\sigma_{x_{s}}=0&\text{if}\ s\in E_{j}\ \text{for some}\ j\in\llbracket 1,{r-1}\rrbracket\;\text{.}\end{cases} 
 
 
 Here, letter  O O  (resp.  E E ) stands for  occupied  (resp.  empty ).
Also, let  a 1 ≔ 0 a_{1}\coloneqq 0  and  b r ≔ t + 1 {b_{r}}\coloneqq t+1 . Now, the path from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy} 
to  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}  is constructed as follows. Refer to Figure  4.4  for a visual presentation of this recursive procedure. 
 
 
 
 [I]  
 
 Along  x a j , … , x b j − 1 x_{a_{j}},\ldots,x_{b_{j}-1}  for  j ∈ ⟦ 1 , r ⟧ j\in\llbracket 1,r\rrbracket :
send a single particle from  x a j x_{a_{j}}  to  x b j − 1 x_{b_{j}-1}  consecutively, as explained in Section  4.2 .
This would be a path from  σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x a j ​ y \sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{a_{j}}y}  to  σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x b j − 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{b_{j}-1}y} , where the first configuration for  j = 1 j=1  is  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}  and the second configuration for  j = r j=r  is  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy} . Notice that this step is omitted if  b j = a j + 1 b_{j}=a_{j}+1 . 
 
 [II]  
 
 Along  x b j − 1 , … , x a j + 1 x_{b_{j}-1},\ldots,x_{a_{j+1}}  for  j ∈ ⟦ 1 , r − 1 ⟧ j\in\llbracket 1,{r-1}\rrbracket 
if 
 

 
 
 α x b j − 1 ​ ∑ j = 1 σ x b j − 1 1 j ≤ 1 : \alpha_{x_{b_{j}-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\sigma_{x_{b_{j}-1}}}\frac{1}{j}\leq 1\;: 
 
 
 we proceed as in Section  4.3 ; move all  σ x b j − 1 + 1 \sigma_{x_{b_{j}-1}}+1  particles from  x b j − 1 x_{b_{j}-1} 
to  x a j + 1 − 1 x_{a_{j+1}-1}  consecutively, move a single particle from  x a j + 1 − 1 x_{a_{j+1}-1} 
to  x a j + 1 x_{a_{j+1}} , and then move back the remaining  σ x b j − 1 \sigma_{x_{b_{j}-1}} 
particles at  x a j + 1 − 1 x_{a_{j+1}-1}  to  x b j − 1 x_{b_{j}-1}  backwards.
This is a path from  σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x b j − 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{b_{j}-1}y}  to  σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x a j + 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{a_{j+1}}y} . 
 
 [III]  
 
 Along  x b j − 1 , … , x a j + 1 x_{b_{j}-1},\ldots,x_{a_{j+1}}  for  j ∈ ⟦ 1 , r − 1 ⟧ j\in\llbracket 1,{r-1}\rrbracket 
if 
 

 
 
 α x b j − 1 ​ ∑ j = 1 σ x b j − 1 1 j > 1 : \alpha_{x_{b_{j}-1}}\sum_{j=1}^{\sigma_{x_{b_{j}-1}}}\frac{1}{j}>1\;: 
 
 
 we proceed as in Section
 4.4 ; move each  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , ⌊ ( σ x b j − 1 + 1 ) / 2 ⌋ ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,\lfloor(\sigma_{x_{b_{j}-1}}+1)/2\rfloor\rrbracket  particles
from  x b j − 1 x_{b_{j}-1}  to  x a j + 1 − 1 x_{a_{j+1}-1}  consecutively, move a single
particle from  x a j + 1 − 1 x_{a_{j+1}-1}  to  x a j + 1 x_{a_{j+1}} , and then move back
the remaining  i − 1 i-1  particles at  x a j + 1 − 1 x_{a_{j+1}-1}  to  x b j − 1 x_{b_{j}-1}  backwards.
As in  [II] , this gives a path from  σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x b j − 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{b_{j}-1}y}  to  σ m − 1 , 1 , ℓ − m x ​ x a j + 1 ​ y \sigma_{m-1,1,\ell-m}^{xx_{a_{j+1}}y} . 
 
 
 
 Sec.  4.2 Sec.  4.3 Sec.  4.2 Sec.  4.4 ⋯ ⋯ \boldsymbol{\cdots\cdots} x x y y O 1 O_{1} E 1 E_{1} O 2 O_{2} E 2 E_{2} ⋯ ⋯ \cdots\cdots 
 Figure 4.4.   Path from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}  to  σ m − 1 , ℓ − m + 1 x ​ y \sigma_{m-1,\ell-m+1}^{xy}  in the general case explained in Section  4.5 . The figure explains how the red particle moves from  x 0 = x x_{0}=x  to  x t = y x_{t}=y  along the path. First, in  O 1 O_{1}  where the sites are occupied, the red particle simply jumps consecutively to the right as explained in Section  4.2 . Next, in  E 1 E_{1}  where the sites are empty and the initial stack is small, we follow the mechanism presented in Section  4.3 .
In  O 2 O_{2}  where the sites are again occupied, we proceed as in Section  4.2 . In  E 2 E_{2}  where the sites are empty and the initial stack is big, we follow the two-dimensional collection of paths explained in Section  4.4 . We iterate these procedures until the red particle arrives at  y y . 
 
 
 Again, denote by  Ω x , y ℓ , m , σ {\Omega_{x,y}^{\ell,m,\sigma}}  the collection of all edges that appear along the paths. 
 
 
 Proof of ( 4.3 ). 
 
 First, we bound  ∇ K 2 ​ f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}  from above by intermediate quadratic forms, on which we can apply the special results in Sections  4.1 – 4.4 .

 Thus, to conclude the proof of ( 4.3 ), according to ( 4.13 ) and ( 4.90 ), we only need to count the number of  overlaps , i.e., the number of triples  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma)  in ( 4.90 ) that produce the same Dirichlet summand  ∇ 2 f ​ ( η , η − δ z + δ w ) \nabla^{2}f(\eta,\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w})  in ( 4.13 ). 
 
 
 To this end, we fix  x < y x<y , the sequence  x = x 0 , x 1 , … , x t = y x=x_{0},x_{1},\dots,x_{t}=y  with  t ∈ ⟦ 1 , diam ( G ) ⟧ t\in\llbracket 1,{\rm diam}(G)\rrbracket , and a triple  ( η , z , w ) (\eta,z,w)  in ( 4.13 ), and then count the number of triples  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma)  that produce the term  ∇ 2 f ​ ( η , η − δ z + δ w ) \nabla^{2}f(\eta,\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w})  in ( 4.90 ) by distinguishing three cases. 
 
 
 (Case 1)  There exists no  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t\rrbracket  for which  { x s − 1 , x s } = { z , w } \{x_{s-1},x_{s}\}=\{z,w\} : 
 
 
 Clearly, in this case the overlap is  zero . 
 
 
 (Case 2)  There exists  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t\rrbracket  for which  ( x s − 1 , x s ) = ( z , w ) (x_{s-1},x_{s})=(z,w) : 
 
 
 By the minimal property of the path length  t t , there is no other  s ′ ∈ ⟦ 1 , t ⟧ s^{\prime}\in\llbracket 1,t\rrbracket  such that  { x s ′ − 1 , x s ′ } = { z , w } \{x_{s^{\prime}-1},x_{s^{\prime}}\}=\{z,w\} , thus we may only focus on the particle jump  z → w z\to w . First, we observe that if  t = 1 t=1 , then the overlap is at most  one . Thus, we only focus on the case  t ≥ 2 t\geq 2 . We further divide into three subcases according to the type —  [I] ,  [II]  and  [III]  — of the particle jump  z → w z\to w . 
 
 
 
 •  
 
 (Case 2.I)   η → η − δ z + δ w \eta\to\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w}  belongs to type  [I] : 
 
 
 In this case, recalling that the overall mechanism is to move a single particle from  x x  to  y y  along the path, we obtain 
 

 
 (4.91) 
 
 η = σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y + ( δ z − δ x ) ​ . \eta=\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}+(\delta_{z}-\delta_{x})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This observation characterizes  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma)  uniquely from the configuration  η \eta . Hence, in this subcase, there is exactly  one  overlap. 
 
 •  
 
 (Case 2.II)   η → η − δ z + δ w \eta\to\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w}  belongs to type  [II] : 
 
 
 Recall that the mechanism in Section  4.3  is further divided into types  (F) ,  (S)  and  (B) , where type  (B)  is impossible here since the particle jumps forward. Moreover, this  [II] -mechanism is happening in  x s ′ , … , x s − 1 , x s , … , x s ′′ x_{s^{\prime}},\dots,x_{s-1},x_{s},\dots,x_{s^{\prime\prime}}  with  s ′ ≤ s − 1 < s ≤ s ′′ s^{\prime}\leq s-1<s\leq s^{\prime\prime}  such that 1 1 
                       1 
                      
                      
                      
                    If  x s ′ = x x_{s^{\prime}}=x  (resp.  x s ′′ = y x_{s^{\prime\prime}}=y ), then condition  σ x s ′ ≥ 1 \sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}}}\geq 1  (resp.  σ x s ′′ ≥ 1 \sigma_{x_{s^{\prime\prime}}}\geq 1 ) should be removed. 
 

 
 (4.92) 
 
 σ x s ′ ≥ 1 ​ , σ x s ′ + 1 = ⋯ = σ x s ′′ − 1 = 0 and σ x s ′′ ≥ 1 ​ . \sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}}}\geq 1\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}+1}}=\cdots=\sigma_{x_{s^{\prime\prime}-1}}=0\quad\text{and}\quad\sigma_{x_{s^{\prime\prime}}}\geq 1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 First, suppose that  x s ′ ≠ x x_{s^{\prime}}\neq x . If the jump  η → η − δ z + δ w \eta\to\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w}  is of type  (F) , then 
 

 
 (4.93) 
 
 
 η + ( η w ​ δ z − η w ​ δ w ) \displaystyle\eta+(\eta_{w}\delta_{z}-\eta_{w}\delta_{w}) 
 
 
 (4.94) 
 
 
 = σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y + ( δ x s ′ − δ x ) + ( ( σ x s ′ + 1 ) ​ δ z − ( σ x s ′ + 1 ) ​ δ x s ′ ) ​ . \displaystyle\qquad=\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}+(\delta_{x_{s^{\prime}}}-\delta_{x})+((\sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}}}+1)\delta_{z}-(\sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}}}+1)\delta_{x_{s^{\prime}}})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Note that the jump  η → η − δ z + δ w \eta\to\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w}  happens within the  η z + η w \eta_{z}+\eta_{w}  jumps of the same number of particles from  z z  to  w w . Thus, the left-hand side configuration  η + ( η w ​ δ z − η w ​ δ w ) \eta+(\eta_{w}\delta_{z}-\eta_{w}\delta_{w})  detects the moment right before when the particle jumps  z → w z\to w  are about to happen. Then, the right-hand side configuration is the corresponding configuration represented in terms of  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma)  due to the following logic. First, from  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy} , a particle would have traveled from  x x  to  x s ′ x_{s^{\prime}} , giving the contribution  δ x s ′ − δ x \delta_{x_{s^{\prime}}}-\delta_{x} . Then, the whole  σ x s ′ + 1 \sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}}}+1  particles at  x s ′ x_{s^{\prime}}  would have traveled all together from  x s ′ x_{s^{\prime}}  to  z z , which gives the contribution  ( σ x s ′ + 1 ) ​ ( δ z − δ x s ′ ) (\sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}}}+1)(\delta_{z}-\delta_{x_{s^{\prime}}}) . This instance is exactly right before when the jumps  z → w z\to w  are initiated, giving the equality in the display. Thus,  σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y \sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}  has a unique representation in terms of  x , y , η , z , w x,y,\eta,z,w , thus the triple  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma)  is determined uniquely. More precisely,  m m  is exactly the number of particles at  x x ,  ℓ \ell  is the number of particles at both  x x  and  y y , and  σ \sigma  is the remaining configuration on  V ∖ { x , y } V\setminus\{x,y\} .
On the other hand, if  η → η − δ z + δ w \eta\to\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w}  is of type  (S) , then  s = s ′′ s=s^{\prime\prime} , and we have 
 

 
 (4.95) 
 
 η = σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y + ( δ x s ′ − δ x ) + ( ( σ x s ′ + 1 ) ​ δ z − ( σ x s ′ + 1 ) ​ δ x s ′ ) ​ . \eta=\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}+(\delta_{x_{s^{\prime}}}-\delta_{x})+((\sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}}}+1)\delta_{z}-(\sigma_{x_{s^{\prime}}}+1)\delta_{x_{s^{\prime}}})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here, both sides detect the instance right before when the single jump  z → w z\to w  occurs, which is clearly  η \eta  in the left-hand side and also the configuration in the right-hand side by the same logic as explained above. Hence, the triple  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma)  is again determined uniquely in this case. Thus, we have two possibilities of triples  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma) .
Now, suppose that  x s ′ = x x_{s^{\prime}}=x . Then, we may similarly detect two possibilities: 
 

 
 (4.96) 
 
 η + ( η w ​ δ z − η w ​ δ w ) = σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y + ( m ​ δ z − m ​ δ x ) \eta+(\eta_{w}\delta_{z}-\eta_{w}\delta_{w})=\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}+(m\delta_{z}-m\delta_{x}) 
 
 
 for type  (F) , and 
 

 
 (4.97) 
 
 η = σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y + ( m ​ δ z − m ​ δ x ) \eta=\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy}+(m\delta_{z}-m\delta_{x}) 
 
 
 for type  (S) . They both yield a unique triple  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma) . Therefore, we conclude that  (Case 2.II)  gives rise to at most  two  overlaps. 
 
 •  
 
 (Case 2.III)   η → η − δ z + δ w \eta\to\eta-\delta_{z}+\delta_{w}  belongs to type  [III] : 
 
 
 As done in  (Case 2.II) , we fix  x s ′ , … , x s − 1 , x s , … , x s ′′ x_{s^{\prime}},\dots,x_{s-1},x_{s},\dots,x_{s^{\prime\prime}}  on which this  [III] -mechanism takes place. A forward jump in type  [III]  is of one of the following three types: initial distribution of  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , m ~ ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,\widetilde{m}\rrbracket  particles  x s ′ → x s ′ + 1 x_{s^{\prime}}\to x_{s^{\prime}+1}  (downward arrows in Figure  4.3 ), movement of  i i  particles in the bulk (double horizontal arrows in the left half of Figure  4.3 ), or the single particle jump  x s ′′ − 1 → x s ′′ x_{s^{\prime\prime}-1}\to x_{s^{\prime\prime}}  (single horizontal arrows in the middle part of Figure  4.3 ). Each type determines a triple  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma)  uniquely, as thoroughly explained in  (Case 2.II) . Thus, we omit the details here and conclude that no more than  three  overlaps are possible in  (Case 2.III) . 
 
 
 Collecting these three subcases,  (Case 2)  admits at most  six  overlaps. 
 
 
 (Case 3)  There exists  s ∈ ⟦ 1 , t ⟧ s\in\llbracket 1,t\rrbracket  for which  ( x s − 1 , x s ) = ( w , z ) (x_{s-1},x_{s})=(w,z) : 
 
 
 The analysis in this third case is almost identical to the one done in  (Case 2) . Thus, we choose not to repeat the tedious computations and record here that, also in this case, at most  six  overlaps are possible in  (Case 3) . 
 
 
 Finally, given  x < y x<y  and  ( η , z , w ) (\eta,z,w) , collecting  (Case 1) ,  (Case 2)  and  (Case 3)  ensure that there are at most  six  possible overlaps of triples  ( ℓ , m , σ ) (\ell,m,\sigma) . Therefore, by ( 4.13 ), we deduce 
 

 
 (4.98) 
 
 ∑ ℓ = 1 k ∑ m = 1 ℓ ∑ σ ∈ Ξ k − ℓ V ∖ { x , y } ∑ { ζ , ζ ′ } ∈ Ω x , y ℓ , m , σ ∇ 2 f ​ ( ζ , ζ ′ ) ≤ 6 ​ ℰ ​ . \sum_{\ell=1}^{k}\sum_{m=1}^{\ell}\sum_{\sigma\in\Xi_{k-\ell}^{V\setminus\{x,y\}}}\sum_{\{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\}{\in}{\Omega_{x,y}^{\ell,m,\sigma}}}\nabla^{2}f(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})\leq 6\,\mathcal{E}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Combining this with ( 4.90 ), the proof of ( 4.3 ) is now completed.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 4.7 . 
 
 The full combination of the three types  [I] ,  [II]  and  [III]  is needed to obtain the correct dependence on  α min \alpha_{\rm min}  in Theorem  3.2 . Alternatively, we may combine only two of those types (that is,  [I]   + +   [II]  or  [I]   + +   [III] ) to obtain different bounds. Here, we briefly record these new results for potential applications in future works. 
 
 
 Recall that we collected ( 4.46 ), ( 4.47 ), and ( 4.48 ) to obtain the exact bound in Lemma  4.5 . If we bound  α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j {\alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}}  from above by  α max ​ ( 1 + log ⁡ k ) {\alpha_{\rm max}\left(1+\log k\right)}  rather than by  1 1 , we obtain an alternative bound 
 

 
 (4.99) 
 
 ∇ K 2 f ​ ( σ m , ℓ − m x ​ y , x , y ) ≤ 3 ​ e α max ​ ( 1 + log ⁡ k ) ​ m ⁡ ( α y + ℓ − m ) ​ t c min ​ α min ​ α ratio ​ ( ∑ { ζ , ζ ′ } ∈ Ω ∇ 2 f ​ ( ζ , ζ ′ ) ) ​ , {\nabla_{K}^{2}f(\sigma_{m,\ell-m}^{xy};x,y)}\leq{3\,e^{\alpha_{\rm max}(1+\log k)}}\,\frac{{m\left(\alpha_{y}+\ell-m\right)t}}{c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}}\left(\sum_{\{\zeta,\zeta^{\prime}\}{\in}\Omega}\nabla^{2}f(\zeta,\zeta^{\prime})\right)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which does not require ( 4.41 ). Combining this inequality with Lemma  4.3  and applying the same logic explained in that subsection, we get 
 

 
 (4.100) 
 
 ℰ K ≤ C ​ e α max ​ ( 1 + log ⁡ k ) ​ k ​ ( α max + k − 1 ) ​ | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​  2 α max α min ​ c min ​ α ratio ​ ℰ ​ , \mathcal{E}_{K}\leq\frac{C\,e^{\alpha_{\rm max}(1+\log k)}\,k\left(\alpha_{\rm max}+k-1\right)|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)\,2^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}{\alpha_{\rm min}\,c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}}\,\mathcal{E}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  C > 0 C>0  is a global constant.
For simplicity, if we take  α = ε ​ α ^ \alpha=\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}  as in ( 2.11 ), take  ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 , and toss the dependence on  G G  and  α ^ \hat{\alpha}  to the constants, this reduces to 
 

 
 (4.101) 
 
 ℰ K ≤ C G , α ^ ​ e C G , α ^ ​ ε ​ log ⁡ k ​ k 2 ε ​ ℰ as ​ ε → 0 ​ , \mathcal{E}_{K}\leq\frac{C_{G,\hat{\alpha}}\,e^{C_{G,\hat{\alpha}}\,\varepsilon\log k}\,k^{2}}{\varepsilon}\,\mathcal{E}\qquad\text{as}\ \varepsilon\to 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  C G , α ^ > 0 C_{G,\hat{\alpha}}>0  now depends on  G , α ^ G,\hat{\alpha}  but not on  k , ε k,\varepsilon .
Similarly, if we do not lower bound  α x ​ ∑ j = 1 m − 1 1 j \alpha_{x}\sum_{j=1}^{m-1}\frac{1}{j}  in ( 4.73 ) by  1 1  and repeat the same logic as above, we obtain a different bound 
 

 
 (4.102) 
 
 ℰ K ≤ C ​ k ​ ( α max + k − 1 ) ​ | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) ​  6 α max α min 2 ​ c min ​ α ratio ​ log ⁡ k ​ ℰ ​ , \mathcal{E}_{K}\leq\frac{C\,k\left(\alpha_{\rm max}+k-1\right)|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)\,6^{\alpha_{\rm max}}}{\alpha_{\rm min}^{2}\,c_{\rm min}\,\alpha_{\rm ratio}\log k}\,\mathcal{E}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which then implies, in the asymptotic regime ( 2.11 ), 
 

 
 (4.103) 
 
 ℰ K ≤ C G , α ^ ​ k 2 ε 2 ​ log ⁡ k ​ ℰ as ​ ε → 0 ​ , \mathcal{E}_{K}\leq\frac{C_{G,\hat{\alpha}}\,k^{2}}{\varepsilon^{2}\log k}\,\mathcal{E}\qquad\text{as}\ \varepsilon\to 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 with different constants  C > 0 C>0  (global) and  C G , α ^ > 0 C_{G,\hat{\alpha}}>0  (depends only on  G , α ^ G,\hat{\alpha} ). 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.  Proofs of Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 

 
 In this section, we prove Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 , following the outline of Section  2.4.2 . The basic starting idea is to split the infinitesimal generator  L G , α , k L_{G,\alpha,k}  into two parts: a slow part and a fast part. In view of this decomposition, standard limit theorems for slow–fast systems ensure that, in the small-diffusivity limit,  SIP \rm SIP  is well approximated by the slow dynamics, after a suitable projection/thermalization according to the fast one. Understanding how and when this projection affects  SIP {\rm SIP} ’s spectral gap is the main non-trivial task of this section. 
 
 
 Recall from ( 2.11 ) that we assume  α = ε ​ α ^ \alpha=\varepsilon\hat{\alpha} , where  α ^ = ( α ^ x ) x ∈ V \hat{\alpha}=(\hat{\alpha}_{x})_{x\in V}  are fixed site weights and  ε \varepsilon  tends to  0 0 . Finally, since the graph  G G  is fixed all throughout the section, we often drop it from the notation, and simply write, e.g.,  SIP k ​ ( α ) = SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(\alpha)={\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha)  and  L α , k = L G , α , k L_{\alpha,k}=L_{G,\alpha,k} . 
 
 
 5.1.  Slow and fast dynamics 

 
 Fix  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , and consider  SIP k ​ ( ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  with time sped up by a factor  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} . Recalling ( 2.2 ), the corresponding
generator is then simply given by  ε − 1 ​ L α , k = ε − 1 ​ L ε ​ α ^ , k \varepsilon^{-1}L_{\alpha,k}=\varepsilon^{-1}L_{\varepsilon\hat{\alpha},k} .
By separating the  ε \varepsilon -dependent terms from the rest, we obtain 
 

 
 (5.1) 
 
 ε − 1 ​ L ε ​ α ^ , k = A α ^ , k + ε − 1 ​ B k ​ , \varepsilon^{-1}L_{\varepsilon\hat{\alpha},k}=A_{\hat{\alpha},k}+\varepsilon^{-1}B_{k}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where, for all  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  and  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k} , 
 

 
 (5.2) 
 
 A α ^ , k ​ f ​ ( η ) \displaystyle A_{\hat{\alpha},k}f(\eta) 
 ≔ ∑ x , y ∈ V c x ​ y ​ η x ​ α ^ y ​ ( f ⁡ ( η − δ x + δ y ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) ​ , \displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\hat{\alpha}_{y}\left({f(\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})-f(\eta)}\right)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 (5.3) 
 
 B k ​ f ​ ( η ) \displaystyle B_{k}f(\eta) 
 ≔ ∑ x , y ∈ V c x ​ y ​ η x ​ η y ​ ( f ⁡ ( η − δ x + δ y ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) ​ . \displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\eta_{y}\left(f(\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})-f(\eta)\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Let  ( 𝒜 α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{A}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  and  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  denote the Markov chains in  Ξ k \Xi_{k}  generated by  A α ^ , k A_{\hat{\alpha},k}  and  B k B_{k} , respectively. More in detail,  ( 𝒜 α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{A}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  describes  k k  independent particles on  G G , while  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  is a particle system with absorbing set 
 

 
 (5.4) 
 
 Ω k ≔ { η ∈ Ξ k : c x ​ y η x η y = 0 for all x , y ∈ V } , \Omega_{k}\coloneqq\left\{\eta\in\Xi_{k}:c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\eta_{y}=0\ \text{for all}\ x,y\in V\right\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and transient set  Δ k ≔ Ξ k ∖ Ω k \Delta_{k}\coloneqq\Xi_{k}\setminus\Omega_{k} , i.e., 
 

 
 (5.5) 
 
 Δ k ≔ { η ∈ Ξ k : c x ​ y η x η y > 0 for some x , y ∈ V } . \Delta_{k}\coloneqq\left\{\eta\in\Xi_{k}:c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\eta_{y}>0\ \text{for some}\ x,y\in V\right\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Let  Π k : ℝ Ξ k → ℝ Ξ k \Pi_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  denote the  B k B_{k} -harmonic projection operator, i.e., 
 

 
 (5.6) 
 
 Π k ​ f ≔ lim t → ∞ e t ​ B k ​ f ​ , f ∈ ℝ Ξ k ​ . \Pi_{k}f\coloneqq\lim_{t\to\infty}e^{tB_{k}}f\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Remark that the range of  Π k \Pi_{k} , written as  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) ⊆ ℝ Ξ k \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} , is a  | Ω k | |\Omega_{k}| -dimensional subspace. More precisely, since  Ω k \Omega_{k}  is the absorbing set of  B k B_{k} , one has, for all  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} ,  Π k ​ f = f \Pi_{k}f=f  on  Ω k \Omega_{k} , and the values of  Π k ​ f \Pi_{k}f  on  Δ k \Delta_{k}  are fully determined by the absorption probabilities on  Ω k \Omega_{k} . Thus, restriction to  Ω k \Omega_{k}  gives a canonical linear isomorphism  ℝ Ξ k ⊇ ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) ≅ ℝ Ω k \mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\supseteq\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\cong\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k}} , and  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})  consists of  B k B_{k} -harmonic extensions to  ℝ Ξ k \mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  of functions in  ℝ Ω k \mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k}} . 
 
 
 The following result builds on a powerful theorem  [ Kur73 , Theorem 2.1]  (see also  [ EK86 , §1, Theorem 7.6] ) on limit theorems for Markovian slow–fast systems. 
 
 
 Proposition 5.1 . 
 
 The operator  𝒢 α ^ , k ≔ Π k ​ A α ^ , k | ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) : ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) → ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) {\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}\coloneqq\Pi_{k}A_{\hat{\alpha},k}|_{\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})}:\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\to\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})  is an infinitesimal generator on  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) ⊆ ℝ Ξ k \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} . Moreover, we have the following convergence: 
 

 
 (5.7) 
 
 lim ε → 0 max η ∈ Ξ k ⁡ | e t ⁡ ( A α ^ , k + ε − 1 ​ B k ) ​ f ​ ( η ) − e t ​ 𝒢 α ^ , k ​ Π k ​ f ​ ( η ) | = 0 ​ , t > 0 ​ , ​ f ∈ ℝ Ξ k ​ . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\max_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\left|e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha},k}+\varepsilon^{-1}B_{k})}f(\eta)-e^{t{{\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}}}\Pi_{k}f(\eta)\right|=0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad t>0\;\text{,}\;\;f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 In this proof, we write  P t ε = e t ⁡ ( A α ^ , k + ε − 1 ​ B k ) P_{t}^{\varepsilon}=e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha},k}+\varepsilon^{-1}B_{k})} . The first claim and a convergence as in ( 5.7 ) with  P t ε ​ f ​ ( η ) P_{t}^{\varepsilon}f(\eta)  replaced by  P t ε ​ Π k ​ f ​ ( η ) P_{t}^{\varepsilon}\Pi_{k}f(\eta)  are the content of  [ EK86 , §1, Theorem 7.6] . In order to obtain the desired claim (i.e., remove the projection operator  Π k \Pi_{k} ), it suffices to prove that 
 

 
 (5.8) 
 
 lim ε → 0 max η ∈ Ξ k ⁡ | P t ε ​ Π k ​ f ​ ( η ) − P t ε ​ f ​ ( η ) | = 0 ​ , t > 0 ​ . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\max_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\left\lvert P_{t}^{\varepsilon}\Pi_{k}f(\eta)-P_{t}^{\varepsilon}f(\eta)\right\rvert=0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad t>0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 For this purpose, fix  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} , and observe that 
 

 
 (5.9) 
 
 Π k ​ f ​ ( η ) = f ⁡ ( η ) ​ , η ∈ Ω k ​ , \Pi_{k}f(\eta)=f(\eta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega_{k}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 because  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  has  Ω k \Omega_{k}  as absorbing states. Hence, since  Π k \Pi_{k}  is a contraction, we get 
 

 
 (5.10) 
 
 | P t ε ​ Π k ​ f ​ ( η ) − P t ε ​ f ​ ( η ) | ≤ ∑ ξ ∈ Δ k p t ε ​ ( η , ξ ) ​ | Π k ​ f ​ ( ξ ) − f ⁡ ( ξ ) | ≤ ( 2 ​ max ξ ∈ Ξ k ​ | f ⁡ ( ξ ) | ) ​ p t ε ​ ( η , Δ k ) ​ , \left\lvert P_{t}^{\varepsilon}\Pi_{k}f(\eta)-P_{t}^{\varepsilon}f(\eta)\right\rvert\leq\sum_{\xi\in\Delta_{k}}p_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\eta,\xi)\left\lvert\Pi_{k}f(\xi)-f(\xi)\right\rvert\leq\big(2\max_{\xi\in\Xi_{k}}\left\lvert f(\xi)\right\rvert\big)\,p_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\eta,\Delta_{k})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  p t ε ​ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) p_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)  denotes the transition kernel of the  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} -sped up  SIP k ​ ( ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  at time  t t .
Since 
 

 
 (5.11) 
 
 lim ε → 0 p t ε ​ ( η , Δ k ) = 0 ​ , η ∈ Ξ k ​ , ​ t > 0 ​ , \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}p_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\eta,\Delta_{k})=0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Xi_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;t>0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 (see Proposition  A.1  below for the full statement and proof of this fact), the proof of the proposition is concluded.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Fix  t > 0 t>0 , and recall that  e t ⁡ ( A α ^ , k + ε − 1 ​ B k ) e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha},k}+\varepsilon^{-1}B_{k})}  has all real eigenvalues, just one equal to  1 1 , while all the others lying in the interval  ( 0 , 1 ) (0,1) . Further, Proposition  5.1  ensures that all eigenvalues (with multiplicity) of  e t ⁡ ( A α ^ , k + ε − 1 ​ B k ) e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha},k}+\varepsilon^{-1}B_{k})}  converge to those of  e t ​ 𝒢 α ^ , k ​ Π k e^{t{{\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}}}{\Pi_{k}} . Here,  𝒢 α ^ , k {\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}  acts on  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k}) , while  e t ​ 𝒢 α ^ , k ​ Π k e^{t{\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}}\Pi_{k}  is regarded as an operator on  ℝ Ξ k \mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} . On  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k}) , this operator coincides with  e t ​ 𝒢 α ^ , k e^{t{\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}} , whereas on  Ker ⁡ ( Π k ) {\rm Ker}(\Pi_{k})  it is identically zero. In particular, the spectrum of  e t ​ 𝒢 α ^ , k ​ Π k e^{t{{\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}}}{\Pi_{k}}  entirely lies in  [ 0 , 1 ] [0,1] : one eigenvalue is  1 1 ,  | Ω k | − 1 |\Omega_{k}|-1  eigenvalues lie in  ( 0 , 1 ) (0,1) , while the eigenvalue  0 0  has multiplicity  dim ⁡ ( Ker ⁡ ( Π k ) ) = | Ξ k | − | Ω k | = | Δ k | {\rm dim}({\rm Ker}(\Pi_{k}))=|\Xi_{k}|-|\Omega_{k}|=|\Delta_{k}| . 
 
 
 As a consequence, the second-largest eigenvalue of  e t ⁡ ( A α ^ , k + ε − 1 ​ B k ) e^{t(A_{\hat{\alpha},k}+\varepsilon^{-1}B_{k})}  converges to the second-largest eigenvalue of  e t ​ 𝒢 α ^ , k ​ Π k e^{t{{\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}}}{\Pi_{k}}  on  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) ⊂ ℝ Ξ k \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\subset\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} , which is given by  e − t ​ w k ​ ( α ^ ) e^{-tw_{k}(\hat{\alpha})} , where 
 

 
 (5.12) 
 
 w k ​ ( α ^ ) ≔ the second-smallest eigenvalue of − 𝒢 α ^ , k : ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) → ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) ​ . w_{k}(\hat{\alpha})\coloneqq\ \text{the second-smallest eigenvalue of}\ {-{\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}}:\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\to\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, we proved the following claim. (Recall that  gap k ​ ( ε ​ α ^ ) = gap k ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm gap}_{k}(\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})={\rm gap}_{k}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}) .) 
 
 
 Corollary 5.2 . 
 
 For all integers  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , we have 
 

 
 (5.13) 
 
 lim ε → 0 gap k ​ ( ε ​ α ^ ) ε = w k ​ ( α ^ ) ​ . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{{\rm gap}_{k}(\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})}{\varepsilon}=w_{k}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 In the next subsection, we analyze more in detail the limiting generator  𝒢 α ^ , k = Π k ​ A α ^ , k | ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) {\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}=\Pi_{k}A_{\hat{\alpha},k}|_{\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})}  and its block-triangular structure. 
 
 
 
 5.2.  Recurrent and transient states 

 
 As already proved in Proposition  5.1 ,
 𝒢 α ^ , k = Π k ​ A α ^ , k | ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) : ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) → ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) {\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}=\Pi_{k}A_{\hat{\alpha},k}|_{\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})}:\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\to\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})  may be interpreted as the infinitesimal generator of a Markov chain on  Ω k \Omega_{k} , hereafter referred to as  ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0} .
For notational simplicity, instead of analyzing directly the operator
 𝒢 α ^ , k {\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}  on the  | Ω k | |\Omega_{k}| -dimensional subspace  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})  of  ℝ Ξ k \mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} ,
we identify  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})  with  ℝ Ω k \mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k}}  by restriction to  Ω k \Omega_{k} ,
and analyze the transformed rate matrix — defined as  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  in ( 5.16 ) or ( 5.37 ) below — on  ℝ Ω k \mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k}} . Let us now describe this rate matrix in detail. 
 
 
 Recall the chains  ( 𝒜 α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{A}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  and  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  introduced at the beginning of Section  5.1 .
Let  𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 \mathbf{r}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}  be the transition-rate function of the process
 ( 𝒜 α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{A}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0} . Moreover, let
 𝐏 ζ ℬ \mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{B}}_{\zeta}  denote the law of the process
 ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  started from  ζ ∈ Ξ k \zeta\in\Xi_{k} , and define 
 

 
 (5.14) 
 
 τ ξ ≔ inf { t ≥ 0 : ℬ k ​ ( t ) = ξ } ​ , τ Ω k ≔ min ξ ∈ Ω k ⁡ τ ξ ​ . \tau_{\xi}\coloneqq\inf\{t\geq 0:\mathcal{B}_{k}(t)=\xi\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\tau_{\Omega_{k}}\coloneqq\min_{\xi\in\Omega_{k}}\tau_{\xi}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 The effective process on  Ω k \Omega_{k} , denoted by
 ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0} , has non-diagonal transition rates 
 

 
 (5.15) 
 
 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ( η , ξ ) ≔ ∑ ζ ∈ Ξ k 𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ( η , ζ ) 𝐏 ζ ℬ [ τ ξ = τ Ω k ] , η , ξ ∈ Ω k , η ≠ ξ . \mathbf{r}^{\mathcal{M}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta,\xi)\coloneqq\sum_{\zeta\in\Xi_{k}}\mathbf{r}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta,\zeta)\,\mathbf{P}^{\mathcal{B}}_{\zeta}\left[\tau_{\xi}=\tau_{\Omega_{k}}\right]\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta,\xi\in\Omega_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\eta\neq\xi\;\text{.} 
 
 
 The diagonal entries are chosen so that row sums vanish. We write 
 

 
 (5.16) 
 
 M α ^ , k := ( 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η , ξ ) ) η , ξ ∈ Ω k ∈ ℝ Ω x × Ω k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}:=\left(\mathbf{r}^{\mathcal{M}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta,\xi)\right)_{\eta,\xi\in\Omega_{k}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{x}\times\Omega_{k}} 
 
 
 for the corresponding square rate matrix of size  | Ω k | |\Omega_{k}| . 
 
 
 From the form of these rates, we immediately derive a classification of recurrent and transient states for this Markov chain; this will lead to a further characterization of the spectral gap  w k ​ ( α ^ ) w_{k}(\hat{\alpha})  of  𝒢 α ^ , k {\mathscr{G}}_{\hat{\alpha},k} . We collect this classification in Proposition  5.4  below; its direct consequence on the spectral gap is the content of the subsequent corollary. Before that, let us introduce, for every integer  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 , 
 

 
 (5.17) 
 
 Ω k , m ≔ { η ∈ Ω k : | { x ∈ V : η x > 0 } | = m } ​ , \Omega_{k,m}\coloneqq\left\{\eta\in\Omega_{k}:|\{x\in V:\eta_{x}>0\}|=m\right\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 that is, the set of configurations in  Ω k \Omega_{k}  with  m m  separated stacks of particles (cf. ( 5.4 )).
Clearly,  Ω k , m = ∅ \Omega_{k,m}=\varnothing  implies  Ω k , m + ℓ = ∅ \Omega_{k,m+\ell}=\varnothing  for all  ℓ ≥ 1 \ell\geq 1 . Further, we have  Ω k , 1 ≠ ∅ \Omega_{k,1}\neq\varnothing ,  Ω k , k + 1 = ∅ \Omega_{k,k+1}=\varnothing , and, thus,  Ω k = ⊔ m = 1 ∞ Ω k , m = Ω k , 1 ⊔ Ω k , 2 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ Ω k , k \Omega_{k}=\sqcup_{m=1}^{\infty}\Omega_{k,m}=\Omega_{k,1}\sqcup\Omega_{k,2}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\Omega_{k,k} . 
 
 
 To make the limiting dynamics more transparent, we first discuss a simple example. 
 
 
 Example 5.3   (Three-site path: block structure) . 
 
 Let  G G  be the path  { 1 , 2 , 3 } \{1,2,3\} , with conductances  c 12 , c 23 > 0 c_{12},c_{23}>0  and  c 13 = 0 c_{13}=0 , and consider the
limiting chain on  Ω k \Omega_{k}  generated by  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k} . This example illustrates
the block structure described above. 
 
 
 For  k = 2 k=2 , the absorbing set of the fast dynamics is 
 

 
 (5.18) 
 
 Ω 2 = Ω 2 , 1 ⊔ Ω 2 , 2 ​ , Ω 2 , 1 = { 2 ​ δ 1 , 2 ​ δ 2 , 2 ​ δ 3 } ​ , Ω 2 , 2 = { δ 1 + δ 3 } ​ . \Omega_{2}=\Omega_{2,1}\sqcup\Omega_{2,2}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\Omega_{2,1}=\{2\delta_{1},2\delta_{2},2\delta_{3}\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\Omega_{2,2}=\{\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 The block  Ω 2 , 1 \Omega_{2,1}  is recurrent: it describes a single stack of two particles moving
effectively as one random walk on  G G . The block  Ω 2 , 2 \Omega_{2,2}  is transient: from
 δ 1 + δ 3 \delta_{1}+\delta_{3} , a slow jump of either particle to the middle site creates a configuration
in  Δ 2 \Delta_{2} , after which the fast dynamics immediately thermalizes into the recurrent
block  Ω 2 , 1 \Omega_{2,1} , never returning to  Ω 2 , 2 \Omega_{2,2} . 
 
 
 For  k = 3 k=3 , one similarly has 
 

 
 (5.19) 
 
 Ω 3 = Ω 3 , 1 ⊔ Ω 3 , 2 ​ , Ω 3 , 1 = { 3 ​ δ 1 , 3 ​ δ 2 , 3 ​ δ 3 } ​ , Ω 3 , 2 = { 2 ​ δ 1 + δ 3 , δ 1 + 2 ​ δ 3 } ​ , \Omega_{3}=\Omega_{3,1}\sqcup\Omega_{3,2}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\Omega_{3,1}=\{3\delta_{1},3\delta_{2},3\delta_{3}\}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\Omega_{3,2}=\{2\delta_{1}+\delta_{3},\ \delta_{1}+2\delta_{3}\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 while  Ω 3 , m = ∅ \Omega_{3,m}=\varnothing  for all  m ≥ 3 m\geq 3 .
Again,  Ω 3 , 1 \Omega_{3,1}  is recurrent, while  Ω 3 , 2 \Omega_{3,2}  is transient. In contrast with the case  k = 2 k=2 , however, a slow jump from a state in  Ω 3 , 2 \Omega_{3,2}  to  Δ 3 \Delta_{3}  need not be followed by a fast relaxation directly into  Ω 3 , 1 \Omega_{3,1} : the fast dynamics may also reach the other state of  Ω 3 , 2 \Omega_{3,2} . Indeed, starting, e.g., from  2 ​ δ 1 + δ 3 ∈ Ω 3 , 2 2\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}\in\Omega_{3,2} , a slow jump of one of the two particles sitting at site  1 1  to site  2 2  yields  δ 1 + δ 2 + δ 3 ∈ Δ 3 \delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}\in\Delta_{3} . The subsequent fast thermalization may either merge all particles into a single stack, hence reaching  Ω 3 , 1 \Omega_{3,1} , or merge the particle at site  2 2  with the one at site  3 3 , thereby reaching  δ 1 + 2 ​ δ 3 ∈ Ω 3 , 2 \delta_{1}+2\delta_{3}\in\Omega_{3,2} . In conclusion, for  k = 3 k=3 , the transient block is already a genuine two-state sub-Markov chain. 
 
 
 
 Proposition 5.4 . 
 
 For the chain  ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0} , the following two claims hold true: 
 
 (1)  
 
 For every  m , m ′ ≥ 1 m,m^{\prime}\geq 1  with  Ω k , m ≠ ∅ ≠ Ω k , m ′ \Omega_{k,m}\neq\varnothing\neq\Omega_{k,m^{\prime}}  and  η ∈ Ω k , m \eta\in\Omega_{k,m} , we have 
 

 
 (5.20) 
 
 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η , ξ ) = 0 ​ , for all ​ ξ ∈ Ω k , m ′ , m ′ > m ​ , {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta,\xi)=0\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{for all}\ \xi\in\Omega_{k,m^{\prime}},\ m^{\prime}>m\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 while, when  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , there exist  η 1 , … , η ℓ ∈ Ω k , m \eta^{1},\ldots,\eta^{\ell}\in\Omega_{k,m}  satisfying 
 

 
 (5.21) 
 
 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η , η 1 ) ​ ( ∏ h = 1 ℓ − 1 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η h , η h + 1 ) ) ​ 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η ℓ , ξ ) > 0 ​ , for some ​ ξ ∈ Ω k , m ′ , m ′ < m ​ . {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta,\eta^{1})\left(\prod_{h=1}^{\ell-1}{\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta^{h},\eta^{h+1})\right){\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta^{\ell},\xi)>0\;\text{,}\;\;\quad\text{for some}\ \xi\in\Omega_{k,m^{\prime}},\ m^{\prime}<m\;\text{.} 
 
 
 As a consequence,  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m} ,  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , consists of transient states only. 
 
 (2)  
 
 The chain  ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  is irreducible on  Ω k , 1 ≠ ∅ \Omega_{k,1}\neq\varnothing , and the position of the single stack evolves as  RW ⁡ ( α ^ ) {\rm RW}(\hat{\alpha}) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 1  The desired claim follows from the connectedness of the graph  G G , and the observation that the rates  𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)  in ( 5.15 ) describe the following two subsequent mechanisms: a step of the chain  ( 𝒜 α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{A}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  increases the number of stacks at most by one, creating, in this case, at least two neighboring stacks; the chain  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  forces all neighboring particles to eventually merge into isolated (i.e., non-neighboring) stacks. 
 
 
 2  Observe that, for all  x , y , z ∈ V x,y,z\in V  with  c x ​ z > 0 c_{xz}>0 , we have 
 

 
 (5.22) 
 
 𝐏 ( k − 1 ) ​ δ x + δ z ℬ [ τ k ​ δ y = τ Ω k , 1 ] = 𝟙 y = z k + ( k − 1 ) ​  1 y = x k , {\bf P}^{\mathcal{B}}_{(k-1)\delta_{x}+\delta_{z}}\,[\tau_{k\delta_{y}}=\tau_{\Omega_{k,1}}]=\frac{\mathds{1}_{y=z}}{k}+\frac{(k-1)\,\mathds{1}_{y=x}}{k}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 because the trajectories of  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  started from  ( k − 1 ) ​ δ x + δ z ∈ Δ k (k-1)\delta_{x}+\delta_{z}\in\Delta_{k}  coincide with those of the symmetric random walk on  ⟦ 0 , k ⟧ \llbracket 0,k\rrbracket  started from  k − 1 k-1 . Plugging this expression into ( 5.15 ), we obtain, for  x ≠ y x\neq y , 
 

 
 (5.23) 
 
 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( k ​ δ x , k ​ δ y ) = ∑ z ∈ V 𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ( k δ x , ( k − 1 ) δ x + δ z ) 𝐏 ( k − 1 ) ​ δ x + δ z ℬ [ τ k ​ δ y = τ Ω k , 1 ] = k c x ​ y α ^ y 1 k = c x ​ y α ^ y , {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(k\delta_{x},k\delta_{y})\\
=\sum_{z\in V}{\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{A}}(k\delta_{x},(k-1)\delta_{x}+\delta_{z})\,{\bf P}_{(k-1)\delta_{x}+\delta_{z}}^{\mathcal{B}}\,[\tau_{k\delta_{y}}=\tau_{\Omega_{k,1}}]=k\,c_{xy}\,\hat{\alpha}_{y}\,\frac{1}{k}=c_{xy}\,\hat{\alpha}_{y}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 namely, the transition rates of  RW ⁡ ( α ^ ) {\rm RW}(\hat{\alpha}) . Irreducibility is then an obvious consequence of that of  RW ⁡ ( α ^ ) {\rm RW}(\hat{\alpha}) .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.5 . 
 
 The claim in Proposition  5.4 2  is the finite-particle analogue of the metastable picture of  SIP {\rm SIP} , fully detailed in  [ GRV13 ,  BDG17 ] . 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.6 . 
 
 For  k ≥ m ≥ 2 k\geq m\geq 2 , the dynamics on  Ω k , m ≠ ∅ \Omega_{k,m}\neq\varnothing  encoded by  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  goes as follows. All stacks evolve as independent  RW ⁡ ( α ^ ) {\rm RW}(\hat{\alpha})  as long as their mutual distances are at least three. When two or more stacks reach mutual distance two, then there is a positive rate for them to merge. These rates — as well as the new positions of the stacks and their new compositions — depend on  G G  and  α ^ \hat{\alpha} , but also non-trivially on  k ≥ m k\geq m  and, when  k > m k>m , on the stacks’ composition. For more details, see Section  5.3.2  below. 
 
 
 
 The state-classification in Proposition  5.4  may be equivalently restated as follows: after a suitable conjugation with a permutation matrix,  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  is turned into a block lower triangular matrix with  M α ^ , k , 1 , … , M α ^ , k , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k,1},\ldots,M_{\hat{\alpha},k,k}  as diagonal blocks, where,
for all  m ∈ ⟦ 1 , k ⟧ m\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket , 
 

 
 (5.24) 
 
 M α ^ , k , m ∈ ℝ | Ω k , m | × | Ω k , m | M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\Omega_{k,m}|\times|\Omega_{k,m}|} 
 
 
 is the submatrix of  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  obtained from the restriction on configurations in  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  (see Figure  5.2 ). Since the eigenvalues of block triangular matrices are the union of those of the diagonal blocks, it suffices to focus on the spectrum of the blocks  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m} ,  m ∈ ⟦ 1 , k ⟧ m\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket .
As already showed in the previous section, the eigenvalues of  − M α ^ , k , m -M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m} ,  m ∈ ⟦ 1 , k ⟧ m\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket , are all real and non-negative. Remark that: 
 
 •  
 
 for  m = 1 m=1 , the spectrum of  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}  coincides with that of the generator of  RW ⁡ ( α ^ ) {\rm RW}(\hat{\alpha}) ; 
 
 •  
 
 for  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2  and  Ω k , m ≠ ∅ \Omega_{k,m}\neq\varnothing ,  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}  is the rate matrix of a sub-Markovian chain killed upon exiting  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m} ; thus, all its eigenvalues are strictly negative. 
 
 
 In view of these simple observations, we register the following useful characterization of the spectral gap  w k ​ ( α ^ ) w_{k}(\hat{\alpha})  defined in ( 5.12 ). 
 
 
 Corollary 5.7 . 
 
 For all  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2  such that  Ω k , m ≠ ∅ \Omega_{k,m}\neq\varnothing , define 
 

 
 (5.25) 
 
 λ k , m ​ ( α ^ ) ≔ smallest eigenvalue of the matrix − M α ^ , k , m ∈ ℝ | Ω k , m | × | Ω k , m | ​ , \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})\coloneqq\ \text{smallest eigenvalue of the matrix}\ -M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}\in\mathbb{R}^{|\Omega_{k,m}|\times|\Omega_{k,m}|}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 whereas  λ k , m ​ ( α ^ ) ≔ + ∞ \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})\coloneqq+\infty  if  Ω k , m = ∅ \Omega_{k,m}=\varnothing . Then, we have 
 

 
 (5.26) 
 
 w k ( α ^ ) = gap RW ( α ^ ) ∧ min m ∈ ⟦ 2 , k ⟧ λ k , m ( α ^ ) . w_{k}(\hat{\alpha})={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(\hat{\alpha})\wedge\min_{m\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket}\lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Example 5.8   (Three-site path: eigenvalues) . 
 
 We keep the notation of Example  5.3 . 
 
 
 For  k = 2 k=2 , the only transient state is  δ 1 + δ 3 \delta_{1}+\delta_{3} . It exits the transient
block as soon as one of the two particles jumps to site  2 2 , which happens at rate 
 

 
 (5.27) 
 
 p ≔ ( c 12 + c 23 ) ​ α ^ 2 ​ . p\coloneqq\left(c_{12}+c_{23}\right)\hat{\alpha}_{2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Therefore,
 λ 2 , 2 ​ ( α ^ ) = p \lambda_{2,2}(\hat{\alpha})=p . 
 
 
 For  k = 3 k=3 , the transient block is
 Ω 3 , 2 = { 2 ​ δ 1 + δ 3 , δ 1 + 2 ​ δ 3 } \Omega_{3,2}=\{2\delta_{1}+\delta_{3},\delta_{1}+2\delta_{3}\} .
Starting from  2 ​ δ 1 + δ 3 2\delta_{1}+\delta_{3} , a slow jump of one of the two particles at site  1 1 
to site  2 2  occurs at rate  2 ​ c 12 ​ α ^ 2 2c_{12}\hat{\alpha}_{2}  and produces
 δ 1 + δ 2 + δ 3 \delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3} . From this configuration, the fast dynamics reaches
 δ 1 + 2 ​ δ 3 \delta_{1}+2\delta_{3}  with probability  c 23 / ( 2 ​ ( c 12 + c 23 ) ) c_{23}/(2(c_{12}+c_{23})) . Hence, the effective jump rate from
 2 ​ δ 1 + δ 3 2\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}  to  δ 1 + 2 ​ δ 3 \delta_{1}+2\delta_{3}  is 
 

 
 (5.28) 
 
 q ≔ 2 ​ c 12 ​ α ^ 2 ​ c 23 2 ​ ( c 12 + c 23 ) = c 12 ​ c 23 c 12 + c 23 ​ α ^ 2 ​ . q\coloneqq 2c_{12}\hat{\alpha}_{2}\frac{c_{23}}{2(c_{12}+c_{23})}=\frac{c_{12}c_{23}}{c_{12}+c_{23}}\hat{\alpha}_{2}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By symmetry, the effective jump rate in the opposite direction is also  q q . The effective killing rate from either transient state to the recurrent block is  p p .
Indeed, starting again from  2 ​ δ 1 + δ 3 2\delta_{1}+\delta_{3} , the slow jump of the particle at site
 3 3  to site  2 2  contributes  c 23 ​ α ^ 2 c_{23}\hat{\alpha}_{2}  to this killing rate. The slow jump of one of
the two particles at site  1 1  to site  2 2  contributes instead
 2 ​ c 12 ​ α ^ 2 ⋅ 1 2 = c 12 ​ α ^ 2 2c_{12}\hat{\alpha}_{2}\cdot\frac{1}{2}=c_{12}\hat{\alpha}_{2} , since from
 δ 1 + δ 2 + δ 3 \delta_{1}+\delta_{2}+\delta_{3}  the fast dynamics reaches  Ω 3 , 1 \Omega_{3,1}  with probability
 1 / 2 1/2 . Thus, the total killing rate is
 c 23 ​ α ^ 2 + c 12 ​ α ^ 2 = p c_{23}\hat{\alpha}_{2}+c_{12}\hat{\alpha}_{2}=p .
Therefore, in the ordered basis
 ( 2 ​ δ 1 + δ 3 , δ 1 + 2 ​ δ 3 ) \left(2\delta_{1}+\delta_{3},\delta_{1}+2\delta_{3}\right) , 
 

 
 (5.29) 
 
 − M α ^ , 3 , 2 = ( p + q − q − q p + q ) ​ . -M_{\hat{\alpha},3,2}=\begin{pmatrix}p+q&-q\\
-q&p+q\end{pmatrix}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Its smallest eigenvalue is
 λ 3 , 2 ​ ( α ^ ) = p \lambda_{3,2}(\hat{\alpha})=p ,
the other eigenvalue being  p + 2 ​ q > p p+2q>p . Thus, in this example, the same quantity  p p 
gives the transient contribution to the limiting gap for both  k = 2 k=2  and  k = 3 k=3 . 
 
 
 
 The natural next step is to estimate the eigenvalue  λ k , m ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})  for each  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  and  m ∈ ⟦ 2 , k ⟧ m\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket , by bounding it from below by  λ 2 , 2 ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{2,2}(\hat{\alpha}) . This task is carried out in the following two subsections. In the first one, we prove  λ k , m ​ ( α ^ ) = λ m , m ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})=\lambda_{m,m}(\hat{\alpha}) ; in the second one, we show  λ m , m ​ ( α ^ ) ≥ λ 2 , 2 ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{m,m}(\hat{\alpha})\geq\lambda_{2,2}(\hat{\alpha}) . 
 
 
 We conclude this subsection by observing that each submatrix  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}  in  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  is self-adjoint with respect to a positive measure  ς α ^ , k , m \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}  given in ( 5.30 ) below. On the one hand, this property is interesting and may become handy in future use, e.g., for building an  L 2 L^{2} -theory for the corresponding continuum model. On the other hand, in our storyline, neither this property nor the precise form of the symmetrizing measure are of primary importance. Thus, we only state the full statement here, and postpone its proof to Appendix  B . 
 
 
 Lemma 5.9 . 
 
 For all  m ≤ k m\leq k ,  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}  is self-adjoint as an operator on  L 2 ​ ( ς α ^ , k , m ) L^{2}(\varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}) , where 
 

 
 (5.30) 
 
 ς α ^ , k , m ​ ( η ) ≔ ∏ x ∈ V η x > 0 α ^ x η x ​ , η ∈ Ω k , m ​ . \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}(\eta)\coloneqq\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in V\\
\eta_{x}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{x}}{\eta_{x}}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega_{k,m}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 5.3.  Comparing  λ k , m ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})  with  λ m , m ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{m,m}(\hat{\alpha}) 

 
 The main goal of this subsection is to prove the following result. 
 
 
 Proposition 5.10 . 
 
 For all integers  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2  with  Ω m , m ≠ ∅ \Omega_{m,m}\neq\varnothing , we have 
 

 
 (5.31) 
 
 λ k , m ​ ( α ^ ) = λ m , m ​ ( α ^ ) ​ , k ≥ m ​ . \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})=\lambda_{m,m}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq m\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 We divide the proof of this identity into a few steps, and fix  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2  so as to satisfy  Ω m , m ≠ ∅ \Omega_{m,m}\neq\varnothing  all throughout. 
 
 
 5.3.1.  Consistency 

 
 Recall the consistency property ( 3.1 ) satisfied by  SIP {\rm SIP} , as well as the corresponding annihilation operator  𝔞 k : ℝ Ξ k − 1 → ℝ Ξ k \mathfrak{a}_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k-1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  in ( 3.2 ). Since  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  arises as a small-diffusivity limit of  SIP {\rm SIP} , it is natural to expect that an analogous property should hold also for  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k} . This is the content of the following proposition. 
 
 
 Proposition 5.11 . 
 
 Define, for all integers  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , the following “restricted” annihilation operator  𝔞 ^ k : ℝ Ω k − 1 → ℝ Ω k \hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k-1}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k}}  as 
 

 
 (5.32) 
 
 𝔞 ^ k ​ g ​ ( η ) ≔ ∑ x ∈ V η x ​ g ​ ( η − δ x ) ​ , g ∈ ℝ Ω k − 1 ​ , ​ η ∈ Ω k ​ . \hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}g(\eta)\coloneqq\sum_{x\in V}\eta_{x}\,g(\eta-\delta_{x})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad g\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k-1}}\;\text{,}\;\;\eta\in\Omega_{k}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Then, we have 
 

 
 (5.33) 
 
 M α ^ , k ​ 𝔞 ^ k = 𝔞 ^ k ​ M α ^ , k − 1 ​ . M_{\hat{\alpha},k}\,\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}=\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}\,M_{\hat{\alpha},k-1}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 It is well known that a system of  k k  independent random walks is consistent, i.e.,  A α ^ , k ​ 𝔞 k = 𝔞 k ​ A α ^ , k − 1 A_{\hat{\alpha},k}\,\mathfrak{a}_{k}=\mathfrak{a}_{k}\,A_{\hat{\alpha},k-1} . Moreover, by rewriting the consistency property of  SIP ⁡ ( ε ​ α ^ ) {\rm SIP}(\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  in ( 3.1 ) with the notation in ( 5.1 ), we have  ( A α ^ , k + ε − 1 ​ B k ) ​ 𝔞 k = 𝔞 k ​ ( A α ^ , k − 1 + ε − 1 ​ B k − 1 ) (A_{\hat{\alpha},k}+\varepsilon^{-1}B_{k})\,\mathfrak{a}_{k}=\mathfrak{a}_{k}\,(A_{\hat{\alpha},k-1}+\varepsilon^{-1}B_{k-1}) . Thus, by linearity, we must also have  B k ​ 𝔞 k = 𝔞 k ​ B k − 1 B_{k}\,\mathfrak{a}_{k}=\mathfrak{a}_{k}\,B_{k-1} , and a similar relation for the harmonic projection  Π k \Pi_{k}  of  B k B_{k} . By combining these observations, we obtain 
 

 
 (5.34) 
 
 Π k ​ 𝔞 k = 𝔞 k ​ Π k − 1 as maps from ​ ℝ Ξ k − 1 ​ to ​ ℝ Ξ k ​ , \Pi_{k}\,\mathfrak{a}_{k}=\mathfrak{a}_{k}\,\Pi_{k-1}\quad\text{as maps from}\ \mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k-1}}\ \text{to}\ \mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and 
 

 
 (5.35) 
 
 Π k ​ A α ^ , k ​ 𝔞 k = 𝔞 k ​ Π k − 1 ​ A α ^ , k − 1 as maps from ​ ℝ Ξ k − 1 ​ to ​ ℝ Ξ k ​ . \Pi_{k}A_{\hat{\alpha},k}\,\mathfrak{a}_{k}=\mathfrak{a}_{k}\,\Pi_{k-1}A_{\hat{\alpha},k-1}\quad\text{as maps from}\ \mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k-1}}\ \text{to}\ \mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular, ( 5.34 ) ensures that  𝔞 k \mathfrak{a}_{k}  maps  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k − 1 ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k-1})  into  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k}) . 
 
 
 For all  j ≥ 1 j\geq 1 , let
 ρ j : ℛ ⁡ ( Π j ) → ℝ Ω j \rho_{j}:\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{j})\to\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{j}} 
be the restriction map to  Ω j \Omega_{j} , and let
 ι j : ℝ Ω j → ℛ ⁡ ( Π j ) \iota_{j}:\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{j}}\to\mathcal{R}(\Pi_{j}) 
be its inverse given by  B j B_{j} -harmonic extension. By definition of the

 (5.36)

 Moreover, since  𝔞 k \mathfrak{a}_{k}  maps  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k − 1 ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k-1})  into  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k}) , we have 
 

 
 (5.37) 
 
 ι k ​ 𝔞 ^ k = 𝔞 k ​ ι k − 1 as maps from ​ ℝ Ω k − 1 ​ to ​ ℛ ​ ( Π k ) ​ . \iota_{k}\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}=\mathfrak{a}_{k}\iota_{k-1}\quad\text{as maps from}\ \mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k-1}}\ \text{to}\ \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, the right-hand side belongs to  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k}) . Moreover, for all
 g ∈ ℝ Ω k − 1 g\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k-1}}  and  η ∈ Ω k \eta\in\Omega_{k} , 
 

 
 (5.38) 
 
 𝔞 k ​ ι k − 1 ​ g ​ ( η ) \displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{k}\iota_{k-1}g(\eta) 
 = ∑ x ∈ V η x ​ ι k − 1 ​ g ​ ( η − δ x ) = ∑ x ∈ V η x ​ g ​ ( η − δ x ) = 𝔞 ^ k ​ g ​ ( η ) ​ , \displaystyle=\sum_{x\in V}\eta_{x}\,\iota_{k-1}g(\eta-\delta_{x})=\sum_{x\in V}\eta_{x}\,g(\eta-\delta_{x})=\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}g(\eta)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where we used that  η − δ x ∈ Ω k − 1 \eta-\delta_{x}\in\Omega_{k-1}  whenever  η ∈ Ω k \eta\in\Omega_{k}  and
