 In the fourth line, we used that
 Π k − 1 ​ A α ^ , k − 1 ​ ι k − 1 \Pi_{k-1}A_{\hat{\alpha},k-1}\iota_{k-1}  has  ℛ ⁡ ( Π k − 1 ) \mathcal{R}(\Pi_{k-1})  as an image, so that
 ι k − 1 ​ ρ k − 1 \iota_{k-1}\rho_{k-1}  acts as the identity on it. This concludes the proof of the proposition
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.3.2.  Irreducible decompositions 

 
 By an  irreducible component of  ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  on
 Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  we mean a communicating class for the restriction of the
transition graph to  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m} : two configurations  η , ξ ∈ Ω k , m \eta,\xi\in\Omega_{k,m}  belong to the same component if there exist paths
 η = η 0 , η 1 , … , η ℓ = ξ \eta=\eta_{0},\eta_{1},\ldots,\eta_{\ell}=\xi  and
 ξ = ξ 0 , ξ 1 , … , ξ r = η \xi=\xi_{0},\xi_{1},\ldots,\xi_{r}=\eta , all contained in  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m} , such that 
 

 
 (5.40) 
 
 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η h − 1 , η h ) > 0 ​ , 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( ξ h − 1 , ξ h ) > 0 ​ , \mathbf{r}^{\mathcal{M}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta_{h-1},\eta_{h})>0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\mathbf{r}^{\mathcal{M}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi_{h-1},\xi_{h})>0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 for all admissible indices. An irreducible decomposition is the
partition of  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  into such irreducible components. 
 
 
 The chain  ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  is not necessarily irreducible on  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m} . For instance, when  G = 𝕋 2 ​ m G=\mathbb{T}_{2m}  is the one-dimensional discrete torus of size  2 ​ m 2m , 
 

 
 
 Ω m , m = { δ 1 + δ 3 + … + δ 2 ​ m − 1 , δ 0 + δ 2 + … + δ 2 ​ m − 2 } ≕ { η m , odd , η m , even } {\Omega_{m,m}=\{\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}+\ldots+\delta_{2m-1},\delta_{0}+\delta_{2}+\ldots+\delta_{2m-2}\}\eqqcolon\{\eta_{m,\rm odd},\eta_{m,\rm even}\}} 
 
 
 consists of exactly two configurations (one with particles/stacks on the odd numbers, the other one on the even numbers), and  𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η m , odd , η m , even ) = 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η m , even , η m , odd ) = 0 {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta_{m,\rm odd},\eta_{m,\rm even})={\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta_{m,\rm even},\eta_{m,\rm odd})=0 .
Moreover, the structure of the irreducible components of the chain  ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  on  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  is always  finer  than the corresponding structure on  Ω m , m \Omega_{m,m} , in the sense of the following proposition. 
 
 
 Proposition 5.12 . 
 
 Decompose  Ω m , m \Omega_{m,m}  into irreducible components for the chain  ( ℳ α ^ , m ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},m}(t))_{t\geq 0} : for some integer  𝔫 m ≥ 1 \mathfrak{n}_{m}\geq 1  (depending only on  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2  and  G G ), 
 

 
 (5.41) 
 
 Ω m , m = Ω m , m 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ Ω m , m 𝔫 m ​ . \Omega_{m,m}=\Omega_{m,m}^{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\Omega_{m,m}^{\mathfrak{n}_{m}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 For all  k > m k>m  and  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔫 m ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{n}_{m}\rrbracket , define, iteratively, 
 

 
 (5.42) 
 
 Ω k , m i = { η + δ x ∈ Ω k , m : η ∈ Ω k − 1 , m i , x ∈ V such that η x ≥ 1 } . \Omega_{k,m}^{i}=\{\eta+\delta_{x}\in\Omega_{k,m}:\eta\in\Omega_{k-1,m}^{i},\ x\in V\ \text{such that}\ \eta_{x}\geq 1\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Then, there exist partitions  Ω k , m i = Ω k , m i , 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ Ω k , m i , 𝔑 i k \Omega_{k,m}^{i}=\Omega_{k,m}^{i,1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\Omega_{k,m}^{i,\mathfrak{N}^{k}_{i}}  for all  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔫 m ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{n}_{m}\rrbracket  such that 
 

 
 (5.43) 
 
 Ω k , m = ⨆ i = 1 𝔫 m ⨆ j = 1 𝔑 i k Ω k , m i , j \Omega_{k,m}=\bigsqcup_{i=1}^{\mathfrak{n}_{m}}\bigsqcup_{j=1}^{\mathfrak{N}_{i}^{k}}\Omega_{k,m}^{i,j} 
 
 
 is an  irreducible  decomposition of  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  for the chain  ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0} . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First, the fact that  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m} , for each  k ≥ m ≥ 2 k\geq m\geq 2 , can be decomposed into irreducible components follows at once from Lemma  5.9 . Thus, to prove the validity of Proposition  5.12 , it suffices to check the following two properties: 
 
 (1)  
 
 Ω k , m = Ω k , m 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ Ω k , m 𝔫 m \Omega_{k,m}=\Omega_{k,m}^{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\Omega_{k,m}^{\mathfrak{n}_{m}} ; 
 
 (2)  
 
 if  i ≠ i ′ i\neq i^{\prime} ,  η ∈ Ω k , m i \eta\in\Omega_{k,m}^{i} , and  ξ ∈ Ω k , m i ′ \xi\in\Omega_{k,m}^{i^{\prime}} , then  η \eta  and  η ′ \eta^{\prime}  belong to different irreducible components of  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  for  ( ℳ α ^ , k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{M}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(t))_{t\geq 0} . 
 
 
 To verify item  1 , take  η ∈ Ω k , m \eta\in\Omega_{k,m}  and define a new configuration  η ∗ ∈ Ω m , m \eta^{*}\in\Omega_{m,m}  as 
 

 
 (5.44) 
 
 η ∗ ≔ ∑ x ∈ V η x ≥ 1 δ x ​ . \eta^{*}\coloneqq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in V\\
\eta_{x}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\delta_{x}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  η \eta  has  m m  separated stacks of particles,  η ∗ \eta^{*}  belongs to  Ω m , m \Omega_{m,m} . Then, according to ( 5.41 ),  η ∗ ∈ Ω m , m i \eta^{*}\in\Omega_{m,m}^{i}  for some  i i , thus  η ∈ Ω k , m i \eta\in\Omega_{k,m}^{i}  by ( 5.42 ). This proves that  Ω k , m = Ω k , m 1 ⊔ ⋯ ⊔ Ω k , m 𝔫 m \Omega_{k,m}=\Omega_{k,m}^{1}\sqcup\cdots\sqcup\Omega_{k,m}^{\mathfrak{n}_{m}} . 
 
 
 We move on to item  2 . It suffices to prove that, for any  η , ξ ∈ Ω k , m \eta,\xi\in\Omega_{k,m}  with  𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ​ ( η , ξ ) > 0 {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta,\xi)>0 , the corresponding configurations  η ∗ , ξ ∗ ∈ Ω m , m \eta^{*},\xi^{*}\in\Omega_{m,m}  (cf. ( 5.44 )) satisfy either  η ∗ = ξ ∗ \eta^{*}=\xi^{*}  or  𝐫 α ^ , m ℳ ​ ( η ∗ , ξ ∗ ) > 0 {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},m}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta^{*},\xi^{*})>0 . 
 
 
 In view of ( 5.15 ), there exists  ζ ∈ Ξ k \zeta\in\Xi_{k}  such that  𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ​ ( η , ζ ) > 0 {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},k}^{\mathcal{A}}(\eta,\zeta)>0  and  𝐏 ζ ℬ [ τ ξ = τ Ω k ] > 0 {\bf P}_{\zeta}^{\mathcal{B}}\,[\tau_{\xi}=\tau_{\Omega_{k}}]>0 . In turn, there exist  x , y ∈ V x,y\in V  with  c x ​ y > 0 c_{xy}>0  such that  ζ = η − δ x + δ y \zeta=\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y} . First, suppose that  ζ ∈ Ω k \zeta\in\Omega_{k} , i.e.,  ζ = ξ \zeta=\xi . Then, it is straightforward that  ζ ∗ = ξ ∗ ∈ Ω m \zeta^{*}=\xi^{*}\in\Omega_{m}  and  𝐫 α ^ , m 𝒜 ​ ( η ∗ , ζ ∗ ) > 0 {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},m}^{\mathcal{A}}(\eta^{*},\zeta^{*})>0 , thus we have  𝐫 α ^ , m ℳ ​ ( η ∗ , ξ ∗ ) > 0 {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},m}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta^{*},\xi^{*})>0  by ( 5.15 ). On the contrary, suppose that  ζ ∈ Δ k \zeta\in\Delta_{k} . This means that  y y  has at least one neighboring site (possibly  x x ) on which  ζ \zeta  has a particle. We divide into several cases. 
 
 
 
 1.  
 
 If  ζ x ≥ 1 \zeta_{x}\geq 1  and  ζ z = 0 \zeta_{z}=0  for all  z ≠ x z\neq x  with  c y ​ z > 0 c_{yz}>0 , then the dynamics  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  can only move particles between sites  x x  and  y y . Thus, by  𝐏 ζ ℬ [ τ ξ = τ Ω k ] > 0 {\bf P}_{\zeta}^{\mathcal{B}}\,[\tau_{\xi}=\tau_{\Omega_{k}}]>0  it necessarily holds that  ξ = η − η x ​ δ x + η x ​ δ y ∈ Ω k \xi=\eta-\eta_{x}\delta_{x}+\eta_{x}\delta_{y}\in\Omega_{k} . This implies that  ξ ∗ = η ∗ − δ x + δ y ∈ Ω m \xi^{*}=\eta^{*}-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y}\in\Omega_{m} , thus  𝐫 α ^ , m ℳ ​ ( η ∗ , ξ ∗ ) > 0 {\bf r}_{\hat{\alpha},m}^{\mathcal{M}}(\eta^{*},\xi^{*})>0 . 
 
 2.  
 
 Suppose that  ζ x ≥ 1 \zeta_{x}\geq 1  and there exists  z ≠ x z\neq x  with  c y ​ z > 0 c_{yz}>0  such that  ζ z ≥ 1 \zeta_{z}\geq 1 . Then, the dynamics  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  gets absorbed exactly when  y y  becomes empty. For  ξ \xi  to have the same number of stacks,  m m , as  η \eta , it follows that  ξ ∗ = η ∗ \xi^{*}=\eta^{*} . 
 
 3.  
 
 Suppose that  ζ x = 0 \zeta_{x}=0 . Then, since the absorption of  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0}  triggers at least one additional loss of a stack of particles, the absorbed configuration  ξ \xi  has strictly less number of stacks than  η \eta , which contradicts the assumption that  η , ξ ∈ Ω k , m \eta,\xi\in\Omega_{k,m} . 
 
 
 
 
 The above three cases conclude the proof of Proposition  5.12 .
∎ 
 
 
 
 Remark 5.13 . 
 
 The irreducible structure in ( 5.43 ) may indeed get  strictly  finer than the one in ( 5.41 ). For example, consider the following  H-shape  graph  G G  with  V = ⟦ 1 , 6 ⟧ V=\llbracket 1,6\rrbracket ,  c 12 = c 23 = c 25 = c 45 = c 56 = 1 c_{12}=c_{23}=c_{25}=c_{45}=c_{56}=1 , and  c x ​ y = 0 c_{xy}=0  otherwise (see Figure  5.1 ). Then,  Ω 4 , 4 \Omega_{4,4}  is a singleton set with  Ω 4 , 4 1 = { δ 1 + δ 3 + δ 4 + δ 6 } \Omega_{4,4}^{1}=\{\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}+\delta_{6}\} , whereas  Ω 5 , 4 \Omega_{5,4}  has two irreducible components  Ω 5 , 4 1 , 1 \Omega_{5,4}^{1,1}  and  Ω 5 , 4 1 , 2 \Omega_{5,4}^{1,2} , given as 
 

 
 (5.45) 
 
 Ω 5 , 4 1 , 1 \displaystyle\Omega_{5,4}^{1,1} 
 = { 2 ​ δ 1 + δ 3 + δ 4 + δ 6 , δ 1 + 2 ​ δ 3 + δ 4 + δ 6 } \displaystyle=\{2\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}+\delta_{6},\delta_{1}+2\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}+\delta_{6}\} 
 
 
 (5.46) 
 
 Ω 5 , 4 1 , 2 \displaystyle\Omega_{5,4}^{1,2} 
 = { δ 1 + δ 3 + 2 ​ δ 4 + δ 6 , δ 1 + δ 3 + δ 4 + 2 ​ δ 6 } ​ . \displaystyle=\{\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}+2\delta_{4}+\delta_{6},\delta_{1}+\delta_{3}+\delta_{4}+2\delta_{6}\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Ω 4 , 4 1 \Omega_{4,4}^{1} 1 2 3 4 5 6 Ω 5 , 4 1 , 1 \Omega_{5,4}^{1,1} Ω 5 , 4 1 , 2 \Omega_{5,4}^{1,2} 
 Figure 5.1.  The H-shape graph  G G  and the corresponding absorbing spaces  Ω 4 , 4 = Ω 4 , 4 1 \Omega_{4,4}=\Omega_{4,4}^{1}  and  Ω 5 , 4 = Ω 5 , 4 1 , 1 ⊔ Ω 5 , 4 1 , 2 \Omega_{5,4}=\Omega_{5,4}^{1,1}\sqcup\Omega_{5,4}^{1,2}  explained in Remark  5.13 . 
 
 
 
 5.3.3.  Conclusion of the proof of Proposition  5.10 

 
 We are now ready to prove ( 5.31 ). 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition  5.10 . 
 
 Recall the definition of the matrix  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  and its submatrices  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}  from ( 5.16 ) and ( 5.24 ), respectively. 
 
 
 First, we deal with the case  k = m k=m . We split  M α ^ , m , m M_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}  into  𝔫 m \mathfrak{n}_{m}  diagonal blocks 
 

 
 
 M α ^ , m , m 1 , … , M α ^ , m , m 𝔫 m ​ , M_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{1},\ldots,M_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{\mathfrak{n}_{m}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 each corresponding to an irreducible sub-Markovian dynamics. Adopting an analogous notation, we further write the eigenvalue  λ m , m ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{m,m}(\hat{\alpha})  in ( 5.25 ) as 
 

 
 (5.47) 
 
 λ m , m ​ ( α ^ ) = λ m , m 1 ​ ( α ^ ) ∧ ⋯ ∧ λ m , m 𝔫 m ​ ( α ^ ) ​ , \lambda_{m,m}(\hat{\alpha})=\lambda_{m,m}^{1}(\hat{\alpha})\wedge\cdots\wedge\lambda_{m,m}^{\mathfrak{n}_{m}}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  λ m , m i ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{m,m}^{i}(\hat{\alpha})  stands for the smallest eigenvalue of  − M α ^ , m , m i -M_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i} . By the Perron-Frobenius theorem, in view of sub-Markovianity, this eigenvalue is strictly positive. Furthermore, because of irreducibility, this is the only eigenvalue admitting a non-negative (actually, strictly positive) eigenfunction, which we refer to as 
 

 
 (5.48) 
 
 ψ α ^ , m , m i ∈ ℝ Ω m , m i ​ . \psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{m,m}^{i}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Let, for all  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔫 m ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{n}_{m}\rrbracket , 
 

 
 (5.49) 
 
 Ψ α ^ , m , m i ​ ( η ) ≔ { ψ α ^ , m , m i ​ ( η ) if  ​ η ∈ Ω m , m i 0 if  ​ η ∈ Ω m ∖ Ω m , m i ​ , \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}(\eta)\coloneqq\begin{cases}\psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}(\eta)&\text{if }\ \eta\in\Omega_{m,m}^{i}\\
0&\text{if }\ \eta\in\Omega_{m}\setminus\Omega_{m,m}^{i}\;\text{,}\end{cases} 
 
 
 be the extension of the eigenfunction  ψ α ^ , m , m i ∈ ℝ Ω m , m i \psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{m,m}^{i}}  to  ℝ Ω m \mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{m}} . In particular,  Ψ α ^ , m , m i \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}  is nonnegative on  Ω m \Omega_{m}  and strictly positive on  Ω m , m i \Omega_{m,m}^{i} . Then, due to the block lower triangular structure of  M α ^ , m M_{\hat{\alpha},m} , we have 
 

 
 (5.50) 
 
 M α ^ , m ​ Ψ α ^ , m , m i = − λ m , m i ​ ( α ^ ) ​ Ψ α ^ , m , m i ​ . M_{\hat{\alpha},m}\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}=-\lambda_{m,m}^{i}(\hat{\alpha})\,\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Since  ℝ Ω m ∋ Ψ α ^ , m , m i ≠ 0 \mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{m}}\ni\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}\neq 0 , this shows that a straightforward extension of  ψ α ^ , m , m i \psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}  produces an eigenfunction for  M α ^ , m M_{\hat{\alpha},m} . 
 
 
 This canonical extension works well only for the case  k = m k=m , while it does not for  k > m k>m  in view of the block lower triangular structure of  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k} . 
 
 
 For  k > m k>m , we follow a different route. In view of Proposition  5.12 , as done above for  k = m k=m , we split  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}  into  𝔑 1 k + ⋯ + 𝔑 𝔫 m k \mathfrak{N}^{k}_{1}+\cdots+\mathfrak{N}^{k}_{\mathfrak{n}_{m}}  diagonal blocks, say,  M α ^ , k , m i , j M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j}  for  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔫 m ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{n}_{m}\rrbracket  and  j ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔑 i k ⟧ j\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{N}_{i}^{k}\rrbracket , each corresponding to the irreducible sub-Markovian dynamics on  Ω k , m i , j \Omega_{k,m}^{i,j} . We refer to Figure  5.2  for the overall structure of the matrix  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k} . Then, we write the eigenvalue  λ k , m ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})  in ( 5.25 ) as 
 

 
 (5.51) 
 
 λ k , m ( α ^ ) = min i ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔫 m ⟧ min j ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔑 i k ⟧ λ k , m i , j ( α ^ ) , \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})=\min_{i\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{n}_{m}\rrbracket}\min_{j\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{N}_{i}^{k}\rrbracket}\lambda_{k,m}^{i,j}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  λ k , m i , j ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{k,m}^{i,j}(\hat{\alpha})  is the smallest eigenvalue of  − M α ^ , k , m i , j -M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j} , which admits a strictly positive eigenfunction  ψ α ^ , k , m i , j ∈ ℝ Ω k , m i , j \psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k,m}^{i,j}} , again by the Perron-Frobenius theorem. 
 
 
 Now, instead of the canonical extension as in ( 5.49 ), we define  Ψ α ^ , k , m i ∈ ℝ Ω k \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k}}  as the “lifting” of  Ψ α ^ , m , m i \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}  through the annihilation operators in ( 5.32 ): 
 

 
 (5.52) 
 
 Ψ α ^ , k , m i ≔ ( 𝔞 ^ k ∘ ⋯ ∘ 𝔞 ^ m + 1 ) Ψ α ^ , m , m i . \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\coloneqq(\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}\circ\cdots\circ\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{m+1})\,\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 In view of the definition of the annihilation operators in ( 5.32 ) and  Ψ α ^ , m , m i ≥ 0 \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}\geq 0  (see ( 5.49 )), one proves, by induction on  k > m k>m , 
 

 
 (5.53) 
 
 Ψ α ^ , k , m i ​ ( η + ξ ) ≥ Ψ α ^ , m , m i ​ ( η ) ​ , η ∈ Ω m ​ , ​ η + ξ ∈ Ω k ​ . \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}(\eta+\xi)\geq\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}(\eta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega_{m}\;\text{,}\;\;\eta+\xi\in\Omega_{k}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Further,  Ψ α ^ , m , m i > 0 \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}>0  on  Ω m , m i \Omega_{m,m}^{i}  implies 
 

 
 (5.54) 
 
 Ψ α ^ , k , m i ≠ 0 ​ . \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\neq 0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By the intertwining relation ( 5.33 ) and the eigenvalue equation ( 5.50 ), we obtain 
 

 
 (5.55) 
 
 M α ^ , k ​ Ψ α ^ , k , m i = M α ^ , k 𝔞 ^ k ⋯ 𝔞 ^ m + 1 Ψ α ^ , m , m i = 𝔞 ^ k ⋯ 𝔞 ^ m + 1 M α ^ , m Ψ α ^ , m , m i = − λ m , m i ( α ^ ) Ψ α ^ , k , m i . \displaystyle\begin{aligned} M_{\hat{\alpha},k}\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}&=M_{\hat{\alpha},k}\,\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}\cdots\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{m+1}\,\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}\\
&=\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{k}\cdots\hat{\mathfrak{a}}_{m+1}\,M_{\hat{\alpha},m}\,\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}=-\lambda_{m,m}^{i}(\hat{\alpha})\,\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\;\text{.}\end{aligned} 
 
 
 The above identity and ( 5.54 )
ensure that  Ψ α ^ , k , m i ∈ ℝ Ω k \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k}}  is an eigenfunction for  − M α ^ , k -M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  associated to the eigenvalue  λ m , m i ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{m,m}^{i}(\hat{\alpha}) . Moreover, after recalling ( 5.52 ) and ( 5.32 ), we have 
 

 
 (5.56) 
 
 Ψ α ^ , k , m i ( η ) = ∑ ξ ∈ Ω m : ξ ≤ η b k , m ( η , ξ ) Ψ α ^ , m , m i ( ξ ) = 0 , η ∈ Ω k , m ′ i , m ′ < m , \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}(\eta)=\sum_{\xi\in\Omega_{m}\,:\,\xi\leq\eta}b_{k,m}(\eta,\xi)\,\Psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}(\xi)=0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Omega_{k,m^{\prime}}^{i},\ m^{\prime}<m\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the coefficients  b k , m ​ ( η , ξ ) b_{k,m}(\eta,\xi)  are nonnegative and supported on 
 

 
 (5.57) 
 
 Ω m ​ ( η ) ≔ { ξ ∈ Ω m : ξ x ≤ η x ​ for all ​ x ∈ V } ​ , \Omega_{m}(\eta)\coloneqq\{\xi\in\Omega_{m}:\xi_{x}\leq\eta_{x}\ \text{for all}\ x\in V\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and the last identity used ( 5.49 ),  m ′ < m m^{\prime}<m , and that 
 

 
 (5.58) 
 
 η ∈ Ω k , m ′ ​ and ​ ξ ∈ Ω m ​ ( η ) ⟹ ξ ∈ Ω m , m ′′ ​ for some ​ m ′′ ≤ m ′ ​ . {\eta\in\Omega_{k,m^{\prime}}\ \text{and}\ \xi\in\Omega_{m}(\eta)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\xi\in\Omega_{m,m^{\prime\prime}}\ \text{for some}\ m^{\prime\prime}\leq m^{\prime}\;\text{.}} 
 
 
 Because of the block lower triangular structure of  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  and the irreducible decomposition ( 5.43 ), this ensures that the restriction 
 

 
 (5.59) 
 
 Ψ α ^ , k , m i | Ω k , m i , j ∈ ℝ Ω k , m i , j \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\big|_{\Omega_{k,m}^{i,j}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k,m}^{i,j}} 
 
 
 of  Ψ α ^ , k , m i ∈ ℝ Ω k \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k}}  to  ℝ Ω k , m i , j \mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k,m}^{i,j}}  is an eigenfunction for  − M α ^ , k , m i , j -M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j}  with corresponding eigenvalue  λ m , m i ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{m,m}^{i}(\hat{\alpha}) . Finally, by the aforementioned positivity of  ψ α ^ , m , m i ∈ ℝ Ω m , m i \psi_{\hat{\alpha},m,m}^{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{m,m}^{i}} , we have 
 

 
 (5.60) 
 
 Ψ α ^ , k , m i ​ ( η ) > 0 ​ , for all ​ η ∈ Ω k , m i ​ . \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}(\eta)>0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{for all}\ \eta\in\Omega_{k,m}^{i}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, for  η ∈ Ω k , m i \eta\in\Omega_{k,m}^{i} , the configuration
 η ∗ := ∑ x : η x ≥ 1 δ x \eta^{\ast}:=\sum_{x:\eta_{x}\geq 1}\delta_{x}  belongs to  Ω m , m i \Omega_{m,m}^{i} , and
( 5.53 ) applies with  ξ = η − η ∗ \xi=\eta-\eta^{\ast}  (see Figure  5.2  for a visual proof).
Since  M α ^ , k , m i , j M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j}  admits  ψ α ^ , k , m i , j ∈ ℝ Ω k , m i \psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{\Omega_{k,m}^{i}}  as the only positive eigenfunction (up to constant multiples), by the Perron-Frobenius theorem, we have 
 

 
 (5.61) 
 
 Ψ α ^ , k , m i | Ω k , m i , j = c ​ ψ α ^ , k , m i , j for some  ​ c > 0 ​ , \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\big|_{\Omega_{k,m}^{i,j}}=c\,\psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j}\qquad\text{for some }c>0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and, thus,  λ m , m i ​ ( α ^ ) = λ k , m i , j ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{m,m}^{i}(\hat{\alpha})=\lambda_{k,m}^{i,j}(\hat{\alpha}) . By ( 5.51 ), the desired claim in ( 5.31 ) follows.
∎

On the left-hand side, we show the  | Ω k | × | Ω k | |\Omega_{k}|\times|\Omega_{k}|  matrix  M α ^ , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k}  in its block lower triangular form. The diagonal blocks in bold are the  | Ω k , m | × | Ω k , m | |\Omega_{k,m}|\times|\Omega_{k,m}| -matrices  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m} ,  m ∈ ⟦ 1 , k ⟧ m\in\llbracket 1,k\rrbracket , defined in ( 5.24 ). Furthermore, each  M α ^ , k , m M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m} ,  m ≥ 2 m\geq 2 , is further decomposed into block diagonal matrices  M α ^ , k , m i , j M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j} ,  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔫 m ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{n}_{m}\rrbracket ,  j ∈ ⟦ 1 , 𝔑 i k ⟧ j\in\llbracket 1,\mathfrak{N}_{i}^{k}\rrbracket . Focusing on the red region  Ω k , m i , j \Omega_{k,m}^{i,j} , we readily verify that  Ψ α ^ , k , m i | Ω k , m i , j \Psi_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i}\big|_{\Omega_{k,m}^{i,j}}  is a strictly positive eigenfunction of  M α ^ , k , m i , j M_{\hat{\alpha},k,m}^{i,j}  associated to  − λ m , m i ​ ( α ^ ) -\lambda_{m,m}^{i}(\hat{\alpha}) . 
 
 
 
 
 5.4.  λ k , k ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{k,k}(\hat{\alpha})  and  k k  coalescing particles 

 
 This subsection is devoted to the proof of a monotonicity in  k k  of  λ k , k ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{k,k}(\hat{\alpha}) , defined in ( 5.25 ) when  Ω k , k ≠ ∅ \Omega_{k,k}\neq\varnothing , and set equal to  + ∞ +\infty  when  Ω k , k = ∅ \Omega_{k,k}=\varnothing  (see Corollary  5.7 ). 
 
 
 Proposition 5.14 . 
 
 For all integers  k ≥ 3 k\geq 3 , we have 
 

 
 (5.62) 
 
 λ k , k ​ ( α ^ ) ≥ λ k − 1 , k − 1 ​ ( α ^ ) ​ . \lambda_{k,k}(\hat{\alpha})\geq\lambda_{k-1,k-1}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Consequently, 
 

 
 (5.63) 
 
 λ k , k ​ ( α ^ ) ≥ λ 2 , 2 ​ ( α ^ ) ​ , k ≥ 2 ​ . \lambda_{k,k}(\hat{\alpha})\geq\lambda_{2,2}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 2\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Fix  k ≥ 3 k\geq 3 .
As  Ω k − 1 , k − 1 = ∅ \Omega_{k-1,k-1}=\varnothing  implies  Ω k , k = ∅ \Omega_{k,k}=\varnothing , we may assume that  Ω k , k ≠ ∅ \Omega_{k,k}\neq\varnothing . 
 
 
 On  Ω k , k \Omega_{k,k} , the dynamics described by  M α ^ , k , k M_{\hat{\alpha},k,k}  coincides with  k k  independent copies of  RW ⁡ ( α ^ ) = RW ⁡ ( G , α ^ ) {\rm RW}(\hat{\alpha})={\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha})  killed upon exiting  Ω k , k \Omega_{k,k} .
For  ξ ∈ Ω k , k \xi\in\Omega_{k,k} , write  𝖯 ξ \mathsf{P}_{\xi}  for the law of this process started from  ξ \xi , and define  τ k ≥ 0 \tau_{k}\geq 0  as its killing time.
Since the killed chain is finite-state, 
 

 
 (5.64) 
 
 λ k , k ( α ^ ) = − lim t → ∞ 1 t log max ξ ∈ Ω k , k 𝖯 ξ [ τ k > t ] . \lambda_{k,k}(\hat{\alpha})=-\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\max_{\xi\in\Omega_{k,k}}\mathsf{P}_{\xi}[\tau_{k}>t]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Fix  ξ ∈ Ω k , k \xi\in\Omega_{k,k} , and choose  z ∈ V z\in V  such that  ξ z = 1 \xi_{z}=1 . Set
 ξ − ≔ ξ − δ z ∈ Ω k − 1 , k − 1 \xi^{-}\coloneqq\xi-\delta_{z}\in\Omega_{k-1,k-1} .
We couple the  ( k − 1 ) (k-1) -particle process started from  ξ − \xi^{-}  with the process obtained from the  k k -particle dynamics started from  ξ \xi  by ignoring the particle initially at  z z . Under this coupling, 
 

 
 (5.65) 
 
 τ k ≤ τ k − 1 ​ . \tau_{k}\leq\tau_{k-1}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Indeed, any contact among the retained  k − 1 k-1  particles kills both systems, while contacts involving the ignored particle kill only the  k k -particle system. Hence, for all  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , 
 

 
 (5.66) 
 
 𝖯 ξ [ τ k > t ] ≤ 𝖯 ξ − [ τ k − 1 > t ] ≤ max ξ ′ ∈ Ω k − 1 , k − 1 𝖯 ξ ′ [ τ k − 1 > t ] . \mathsf{P}_{\xi}[\tau_{k}>t]\leq\mathsf{P}_{\xi^{-}}[\tau_{k-1}>t]\leq\max_{\xi^{\prime}\in\Omega_{k-1,k-1}}\mathsf{P}_{\xi^{\prime}}[\tau_{k-1}>t]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By taking the maximum over  ξ ∈ Ω k , k \xi\in\Omega_{k,k} , the above bound and ( 5.64 ) give ( 5.62 ).
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 5.5.  Proofs of Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 

 
 We are ready to prove Theorems  2.3  and  2.4 . 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  2.3 . 
 
 By Corollary  5.2  and ( 5.26 ) (we omit  G G  from the notation), 
 

 
 (5.67) 
 
 lim ε → 0 gap k ​ ( ε ​ α ^ ) ε = gap RW ( α ^ ) ∧ min m ∈ ⟦ 2 , k ⟧ λ k , m ( α ^ ) , \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{{\rm gap}_{k}(\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})}{\varepsilon}={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(\hat{\alpha})\wedge\min_{m\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket}\lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 whereas by ( 5.31 ) and ( 5.63 ), 
 

 
 (5.68) 
 
 min m ∈ ⟦ 2 , k ⟧ λ k , m ( α ^ ) = λ 2 , 2 ( α ^ ) . \min_{m\in\llbracket 2,k\rrbracket}\lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})=\lambda_{2,2}(\hat{\alpha})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, since the limit in ( 5.67 ) is strictly positive, Theorem  2.3  holds true.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  2.4 . 
 
 Recall that, for all graphs  G G  and site weights  α ^ \hat{\alpha} , we have  gap 1 ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) = ε ​ gap RW ​ ( G , α ^ ) {{\rm gap}_{1}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})}=\varepsilon\,{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha}) ,  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 ; moreover, by ( 5.67 ) for  k = 2 k=2 , we have 
 

 
 (5.69) 
 
 lim ε → 0 gap 2 ​ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) ε = gap RW ​ ( G , α ^ ) ∧ λ 2 , 2 ​ ( G , α ^ ) ​ . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{{\rm gap}_{2}(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})}{\varepsilon}={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha})\wedge\lambda_{2,2}(G,\hat{\alpha})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, it suffices to verify the existence of  G G  and  α ^ \hat{\alpha}  such that 
 

 
 (5.70) 
 
 λ 2 , 2 ​ ( G , α ^ ) < gap RW ​ ( G , α ^ ) ​ . \lambda_{2,2}(G,\hat{\alpha})<{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha})\;\text{.} 
 
 
 In particular, let us recall from the discussion in Section  5.4 , that  λ 2 , 2 \lambda_{2,2}  is the smallest eigenvalue of (the negative of) the generator describing two independent  RW ⁡ ( G , α ^ ) {\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha}) , having  α ^ = ( α ^ x ) x ∈ V \hat{\alpha}=(\hat{\alpha}_{x})_{x\in V}  as a (non-normalized) reversible measure, and which get instantaneously killed when attempting to get to a mutual graph-distance strictly smaller than two. 
 
 
 In view of this, it is not surprising that the standard discrete torus  𝕋 N d \mathbb{T}_{N}^{d} , with dimension  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , size  N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N} , and homogeneous site weights, satisfies ( 5.70 ), provided that  N N  is large enough. From now on, we fix  α ^ ≡ 1 \hat{\alpha}\equiv 1 ,  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 ,  N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N} , and 
 

 
 (5.71) 
 
 G = 𝕋 N d ​ , ​ with ​ c x ​ y ≡ 1 ​ for all neighboring sites ​ x , y ∈ 𝕋 N d ​ , G=\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}\;\text{,}\;\;\text{with}\ c_{xy}\equiv 1\ \text{for all neighboring sites}\ x,y\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and drop them from the notation, simply writing, e.g.,  λ 2 , 2 \lambda_{2,2}  and  gap RW {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}  instead of  λ 2 , 2 ​ ( G , α ^ ) \lambda_{2,2}(G,\hat{\alpha})  and  RW ⁡ ( G , α ^ ) {\rm RW}(G,\hat{\alpha}) . Later on, we will take the limit  N → ∞ N\to\infty . 
 
 
 Consider the positions  ( X t , Y t ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t},Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}  of two independent particles on  𝕋 N d \mathbb{T}_{N}^{d} , each one jumping with unit rate to any of its neighbors.
Observe that  ( X t , Y t ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t},Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}  is reversible with respect to the uniform measure  κ = κ N , d \kappa=\kappa_{N,d}  on  𝕋 N d × 𝕋 N d \mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d} . In what follows, let  𝖯 ( x , y ) \mathsf{P}_{(x,y)}  and  𝖤 ( x , y ) \mathsf{E}_{(x,y)}  denote the law and corresponding expectation with respect to the law of  ( X t , Y t ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t},Y_{t})_{t\geq 0}  when starting from  ( x , y ) ∈ 𝕋 N d × 𝕋 N d (x,y)\in\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}\times\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d} . It is well known (see, e.g.,  [ LP17 , Sections 12.3.1 & 12.4] ) that, for all  N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N}  large enough and all  d ≥ 1 d\geq 1 , we have 
 

 
 (5.72) 
 
 gap RW = 2 ​ ( 1 − cos ⁡ 2 ​ π N ) ≥ π 2 N 2 ​ . {{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}=2\left(1-\cos\frac{2\pi}{N}\right)\geq\frac{\pi^{2}}{N^{2}}\;\text{.}} 
 
 
 Letting 
 

 
 (5.73) 
 
 𝒲 ≔ { ( x , y ) ∈ 𝕋 N d : dist 𝕋 N d ​ ( x , y ) ≥ 2 } ​ , \mathcal{W}\coloneqq\{(x,y)\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}:{\rm dist}_{\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}(x,y)\geq 2\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 by the second inequality in  [ Her23 , Eq. (1.7)] , we have 
 

 
 (5.74) 
 
 λ 2 , 2 ≤ 1 max 𝒰 ⊆ 𝒲 𝖤 κ ( ⋅ | 𝒰 ) [ τ 𝒰 c ] ≤ 1 𝖤 κ ( ⋅ | 𝒲 ) [ τ 𝒲 c ] ​ , \lambda_{2,2}\leq\frac{1}{\max_{\mathcal{U}\subseteq\mathcal{W}}\mathsf{E}_{\kappa(\,\cdot\,|\mathcal{U})}[\tau_{\mathcal{U}^{c}}]}\leq\frac{1}{\mathsf{E}_{\kappa(\,\cdot\,|\mathcal{W})}[\tau_{\mathcal{W}^{c}}]}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where in the second step we substituted  𝒰 = 𝒲 \mathcal{U}=\mathcal{W} .
The last denominator in ( 5.74 ) is almost the mean meeting time of two independent random walks, both being uniformly distributed on  𝕋 N d \mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}  (and independently) at time  t = 0 t=0 . This latter quantity is known  [ Cox89 , Theorem 4]  to asymptotically diverge, as  N → ∞ N\to\infty , like  s d ​ ( N ) s_{d}(N)  given in ( 2.9 ).
In the remainder of this proof, we show that the denominator in ( 5.74 ) follows the same asymptotic behavior. 
 
 
 Since  κ ⁡ ( 𝒲 ) = 1 − 2 ​ d + 1 N d \kappa(\mathcal{W})=1-\frac{2d+1}{N^{d}} , we calculate 
 

 
 (5.75) 
 
 𝖤 κ ( ⋅ ∣ Ω ) [ τ 𝒲 c ] = ( 1 − 2 ​ d + 1 N d ) − 1 1 N 2 ​ d ∑ ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒲 𝖤 ( x , y ) [ τ 𝒲 c ] . \mathsf{E}_{\kappa(\,\cdot\,\mid\Omega)}[\tau_{\mathcal{W}^{c}}]=\left(1-\frac{2d+1}{N^{d}}\right)^{-1}\frac{1}{N^{2d}}\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{W}}\mathsf{E}_{(x,y)}[\tau_{\mathcal{W}^{c}}]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Moreover, since  Z t ≔ X t − Y t ∈ 𝕋 N d Z_{t}\coloneqq X_{t}-Y_{t}\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}  is a simple random walk (jumping to a nearest neighbor at rate  2 2 ), we have 
 

 
 (5.76) 
 
 𝖤 ( x , y ) ​ [ τ 𝒲 c ] = 𝖤 ^ x − y ​ [ τ ^ 1 ] ​ , ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒲 ​ , \mathsf{E}_{(x,y)}[\tau_{\mathcal{W}^{c}}]=\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{x-y}[\hat{\tau}_{1}]\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad(x,y)\in\mathcal{W}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where we use the hat-notation to refer to quantities related to  Z t Z_{t} , while  τ ^ ℓ \hat{\tau}_{\ell} ,  ℓ ∈ ⟦ 0 , ⌊ N / 2 ⌋ ⟧ \ell\in\llbracket 0,\lfloor N/2\rfloor\rrbracket , denotes the first hitting time of the set 
 

 
 (5.77) 
 
 { z ∈ 𝕋 N d : dist 𝕋 N d ​ ( z , 0 ) = ℓ } ​ . \{z\in\mathbb{T}^{d}_{N}:{\rm dist}_{\mathbb{T}_{N}^{d}}(z,0)=\ell\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By the strong Markov property and translation invariance of the dynamics, we have 
 

 
 (5.78) 
 
 𝖤 ^ x − y ​ [ τ ^ 0 ] = 𝖤 ^ x − y ​ [ τ ^ 1 ] + 𝖤 ^ e 1 ​ [ τ ^ 0 ] = 𝖤 ^ x − y ​ [ τ ^ 1 ] + 𝖤 ^ 0 ​ [ τ ^ 0 + ] − 𝖤 ^ 0 ​ [ τ ^ 1 ] ​ , \widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{x-y}[\hat{\tau}_{0}]=\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{x-y}[\hat{\tau}_{1}]+\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{e_{1}}[\hat{\tau}_{0}]=\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{x-y}[\hat{\tau}_{1}]+\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{0}[\hat{\tau}_{0}^{+}]-\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{0}[\hat{\tau}_{1}]\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  τ ^ 0 + \hat{\tau}_{0}^{+}  denotes the first return time to  0 0  for the walk  Z t Z_{t}  started in  0 0 , i.e., 
 

 
 (5.79) 
 
 τ ^ 0 + ≔ inf { t > τ ^ 1 : Z t = 0 } ​ . \hat{\tau}_{0}^{+}\coloneqq\inf\{t>\hat{\tau}_{1}:Z_{t}=0\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Clearly,  𝖤 ^ 0 ​ [ τ ^ 1 ] = 1 4 ​ d \widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{0}\,[\hat{\tau}_{1}]=\frac{1}{4d} , while, by Kac’s formula for return times (see, e.g.,  [ AF02 , p. 34, Lemma 2.25] ), we obtain 
 

 
 (5.80) 
 
 𝖤 ^ 0 ​ [ τ ^ 0 + ] = N d 4 ​ d ​ . \widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{0}[\hat{\tau}_{0}^{+}]=\frac{N^{d}}{4d}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, we get 
 
 
 
 (5.81) 
 
 1 N 2 ​ d ​ ∑ ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒲 𝖤 ^ x − y ​ [ τ ^ 1 ] \displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2d}}\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{W}}\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{x-y}[\hat{\tau}_{1}] 
 = 1 N 2 ​ d ​ ∑ ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒲 ( 𝖤 ^ x − y ​ [ τ ^ 0 ] − 𝖤 ^ 0 ​ [ τ ^ 0 + ] + 𝖤 ^ 0 ​ [ τ ^ 1 ] ) \displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2d}}\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{W}}\left(\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{x-y}[\hat{\tau}_{0}]-\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{0}[\hat{\tau}_{0}^{+}]+\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{0}[\hat{\tau}_{1}]\right) 
 
 
 
 
 ≥ { 1 N 2 ​ d ​ ∑ ( x , y ) ∈ 𝒲 𝖤 ^ x − y ​ [ τ ^ 0 ] } − N d 4 ​ d ​ . \displaystyle\geq\left\{\frac{1}{N^{2d}}\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{W}}\widehat{\mathsf{E}}_{x-y}[\hat{\tau}_{0}]\right\}-\frac{N^{d}}{4d}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By  [ Cox89 , Theorem 4]  (see also  [ Dur24 , Lemma 7.3.2]  for a more recent textbook version), the expression between curly brackets is, for all  N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N}  large enough, bounded below by  𝔤 d ​ s d ​ ( N ) \mathfrak{g}_{d}\,s_{d}(N) , for some positive constant  𝔤 d \mathfrak{g}_{d}  (which, for  d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 , is strictly larger than  1 4 ​ d \frac{1}{4d} , cf.  [ Cox89 , Eq. (1.2)] ). Hence, collecting ( 5.75 ), ( 5.76 ) and ( 5.81 ), we obtain, for some  C > 0 C>0  (possibly depending only on  d ≥ 2 d\geq 2 , but not on  N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N} ) and for all  N ∈ ℕ N\in\mathbb{N}  large enough, 
 

 
 (5.82) 
 
 𝖤 κ ( ⋅ | 𝒲 ) [ τ 𝒲 c ] ≥ C s d ( N ) , {\mathsf{E}}_{\kappa(\,\cdot\,|\mathcal{W})}[\tau_{\mathcal{W}^{c}}]\geq Cs_{d}(N)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which, along with ( 5.74 ), implies 
 

 
 (5.83) 
 
 λ 2 , 2 ≤ 1 C ​ s d ​ ( N ) ​ . \lambda_{2,2}\leq\frac{1}{Cs_{d}(N)}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Comparing this inequality with ( 5.72 ) (cf. ( 2.9 )) completes the proof of the theorem.
∎ 
 
 
 
 We conclude this section with a remark on the role of the directional limits in ( 2.11 ) in proving Theorem  2.3 . 
 
 
 Remark 5.15   (Directional  vs.  general limits) . 
 
 Theorem  2.3  is stated for directional limits
 α = ε ​ α ^ \alpha=\varepsilon\hat{\alpha} , with  α ^ ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) V \hat{\alpha}\in(0,\infty)^{V}  fixed and
 ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 ; see ( 2.11 ). Let us briefly explain what would be needed in order to pass from ( 2.11 ) to a completely general limit  α → 0 \alpha\to 0 . 
 
 
 A natural target would be the lower bound (we omit  G G  from the notation) 
 

 
 (5.84) 
 
 lim inf α → 0 gap k ​ ( α ) gap 2 ​ ( α ) ≥ 1 ​ , k ≥ 3 ​ . \liminf_{\alpha\to 0}\frac{{\rm gap}_{k}(\alpha)}{{\rm gap}_{2}(\alpha)}\geq 1\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad k\geq 3\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Since, by ( 2.10 ), one has
 gap k ​ ( α ) ≤ gap 2 ​ ( α ) {\rm gap}_{k}(\alpha)\leq{\rm gap}_{2}(\alpha)  for all  k ≥ 3 k\geq 3 ,
( 5.84 ) would imply the full limit 
 

 
 (5.85) 
 
 lim α → 0 gap k ​ ( α ) gap 2 ​ ( α ) = 1 ​ . \lim_{\alpha\to 0}\frac{{\rm gap}_{k}(\alpha)}{{\rm gap}_{2}(\alpha)}=1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By the sequential characterization of the liminf, it suffices to consider arbitrary
sequences  α n → 0 \alpha_{n}\to 0 , as  n → ∞ n\to\infty , in  ( 0 , ∞ ) V (0,\infty)^{V} . By setting 
 

 
 (5.86) 
 
 ε n ≔ ( α n ) max = ‖ α n ‖ ∞ ​ , β n ≔ ε n − 1 ​ α n ​ , \varepsilon_{n}\coloneqq(\alpha_{n})_{\rm max}=\|\alpha_{n}\|_{\infty}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\beta_{n}\coloneqq\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}\alpha_{n}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 we have  ε n → 0 \varepsilon_{n}\to 0  and, up to extracting a subsequence,  β n → α ^ \beta_{n}\to\hat{\alpha} ,
for some  α ^ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] V \hat{\alpha}\in[0,1]^{V}  with  max x ∈ V ⁡ α ^ x = 1 \max_{x\in V}\hat{\alpha}_{x}=1 . Along such a
subsequence, the analogue of ( 5.1 ) reads as 
 

 
 (5.87) 
 
 ε n − 1 ​ L α n , k = A β n , k + ε n − 1 ​ B k ​ , \varepsilon_{n}^{-1}L_{\alpha_{n},k}=A_{\beta_{n},k}+\varepsilon_{n}^{-1}B_{k}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  A β n , k A_{\beta_{n},k}  is the slow part and  B k B_{k}  is the fast part of the generator, see ( 5.2 ) and ( 5.3 ). Since
 A β n , k → A α ^ , k A_{\beta_{n},k}\to A_{\hat{\alpha},k}  as finite matrices, the slow–fast reduction used in
Proposition  5.1  applies, with minor notational changes, also to this
 n n -dependent situation. The limiting effective generator  𝒢 α ^ , k \mathscr{G}_{\hat{\alpha},k}  is given by the same formula there. 
 
 
 If  α ^ ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) V \hat{\alpha}\in(0,\infty)^{V} , then the rest of the proof of Theorem
 2.3  goes through without changes: the limiting quantities
 w k ​ ( α ^ ) w_{k}(\hat{\alpha})  and  λ k , m ​ ( α ^ ) \lambda_{k,m}(\hat{\alpha})  appearing in ( 5.12 ) and
( 5.25 ) are exactly those analyzed in Sections
 5.2 – 5.4 . Thus, strictly positive subsequential directions do not create
any additional difficulty. 
 
 
 The only genuinely new case is when  α ^ x = 0 \hat{\alpha}_{x}=0  for some  x ∈ V x\in V . Then, the
limiting effective generator may acquire additional zero eigenvalues: sites with
 α ^ x = 0 \hat{\alpha}_{x}=0  may generate further transient states and, depending on the graph,
may also disconnect irreducible components of the limiting effective dynamics.
In particular, the effective gap at scale  ε n = ( α n ) max \varepsilon_{n}=(\alpha_{n})_{\rm max}  may vanish,
so that the relevant gaps may be of smaller order than  ε n \varepsilon_{n} . In such a
case, the first-order slow–fast limit does not determine the ratio
 gap k ​ ( α n ) / gap 2 ​ ( α n ) {\rm gap}_{k}(\alpha_{n})/{\rm gap}_{2}(\alpha_{n}) ; one would have to analyze the next
non-vanishing scales of the coordinates of  α n \alpha_{n} . Although one expects the same
two-particle mechanism to arise also through this further multi-scale analysis, we do
not pursue this extension here, and prefer to state Theorem  2.3 
in the clean directional regime with  α ^ ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) V \hat{\alpha}\in(0,\infty)^{V}  fixed. 
 
 
 
 
 
 6.  Non-conservative SIP. Setting and main results 

 
 In this section, we attach particle reservoirs to some sites of the graph. We describe this by introducing two sets of non-negative site parameters,  ω = ( ω x ) x ∈ V \omega=(\omega_{x})_{x\in V}  and  θ = ( θ x ) x ∈ V \theta=(\theta_{x})_{x\in V} . The former one represents the rates of interaction between a site and its reservoir. The latter one prescribes the reservoirs’ particle densities. Next to the inclusion particles’ motion considered so far, we now let particles be created and annihilated with rates resembling those of  SIP {\rm SIP} . 
 
 
 6.1.  Model 

 
 Let  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})  and  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V}  be as in Section  2 , and introduce some non-negative site weights  ω = ( ω x ) x ∈ V \omega=(\omega_{x})_{x\in V}  and  θ = ( θ x ) x ∈ V \theta=(\theta_{x})_{x\in V} . Then, we write  SIP ⁡ ( G , α , ω , θ ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\theta)  for the Markov process on 2 2 
               2 
              
              
              
             Ξ 0 ≔ { ∅ } \Xi_{0}\coloneqq\{\emptyset\}  consists of the empty configuration  ∅ \emptyset  with no particles.   Ξ ≔ ∪ k = 0 ∞ Ξ k \Xi\coloneqq\cup_{k=0}^{\infty}\,\Xi_{k}  with infinitesimal generator  L G , α , ω , θ L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}  given, for a bounded function  f ∈ ℝ Ξ f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi}  and a configuration  η ∈ Ξ \eta\in\Xi , by 
 
 
 
 (6.1) 
 
 
 L G , α , ω , θ ​ f ​ ( η ) = ∑ x , y ∈ V c x ​ y ​ η x ​ ( α y + η y ) ​ ( f ⁡ ( η − δ x + δ y ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ) \displaystyle L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}f(\eta)=\sum_{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\left(\alpha_{y}+\eta_{y}\right)\left(f(\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})-f(\eta)\right) 
 
 
 
 
 + ∑ x ∈ V ω x { η x ( 1 + θ x ) ( f ( η − δ x ) − f ( η ) ) + θ x ( α x + η x ) ( f ( η + δ x ) − f ( η ) ) } . \displaystyle+\sum_{x\in V}\omega_{x}\,\big\{\eta_{x}\left(1+\theta_{x}\right)\left(f(\eta-\delta_{x})-f(\eta)\right)+\theta_{x}\left(\alpha_{x}+\eta_{x}\right)\left(f(\eta+\delta_{x})-f(\eta)\right)\big\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here, the first summation on the right-hand side corresponds with the conservative dynamics described by  L G , α , k L_{G,\alpha,k}  in ( 2.2 ), and is referred to as bulk dynamics. The second summation concerns the particle creation-annihilation mechanism, and may be interpreted as a bulk-boundary dynamics. More in detail, particles at  x ∈ V x\in V  get killed independently, each at rate  ω x ​ ( 1 + θ x ) \omega_{x}\left(1+\theta_{x}\right) , while a new particle is created at  x ∈ V x\in V  with a configuration-dependent rate  ω x ​ θ x ​ ( α x + η x ) \omega_{x}\,\theta_{x}\left(\alpha_{x}+\eta_{x}\right) .
Furthermore, albeit the configuration space  Ξ \Xi  is countably infinite,  SIP ⁡ ( G , α , ω , θ ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\theta)  does not explode in finite time. Indeed, the total number of particles is stochastically dominated by a pure birth process with linearly growing rates (see, e.g.,  [ FGS22 , Proposition 2.1] ). 
 
 
 The long-time behavior of  SIP ⁡ ( G , α , ω , θ ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\theta)  clearly depends on the values of  ω \omega  and  θ \theta . To start with, we mention that  ω x = 0 \omega_{x}=0  means that site  x ∈ V x\in V  does not directly exchange particles with any reservoirs, whereas
 θ x = 0 \theta_{x}=0  corresponds to having a purely absorbing reservoir at  x ∈ V x\in V . As a consequence, if  ω ≡ 0 \omega\equiv 0 , we recover the conservative case having, for any total number of particles  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} ,  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  in ( 2.3 ) as a unique reversible measure. If  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0  and  ω ≢ 0 \omega\not\equiv 0 , the system eventually empties out. In other words, if  θ \theta  vanishes on the support of  ω \omega , that is, 
 

 
 (6.2) 
 
 supp ⁡ ( ω ) ≔ { x ∈ V : ω x > 0 } ​ , {\rm supp}(\omega)\coloneqq\{x\in V:\omega_{x}>0\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 then  SIP ⁡ ( G , α , ω , θ ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\theta)  has the empty configuration  ∅ ∈ Ξ 0 \emptyset\in\Xi_{0}  as its unique stationary state, which, in turn, is absorbing.
More generally, for  ω ≢ 0 \omega\not\equiv 0 , there exists a unique stationary measure for  SIP ⁡ ( G , α , ω , θ ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\theta) , referred to as  μ G , α , ω , θ \mu_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta} .
In this situation, one typically distinguishes between two scenarios, commonly referred to as equilibrium and non-equilibrium, respectively. 
 
 
 More in detail,  equilibrium  occurs if  θ \theta  is constant on
 supp ⁡ ( ω ) {\rm supp}(\omega) . In this case,  μ G , α , ω , θ \mu_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}  is reversible, in product form, and explicit (see, e.g.,  [ CGGR13 ,  FRS22 ] ): assuming that  θ ≡ ϱ > 0 \theta\equiv\varrho>0 , then  μ G , α , ω , θ = ν α , ϱ \mu_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}=\nu_{\alpha,\varrho} , where, for all  η ∈ Ξ \eta\in\Xi , 
 

 
 (6.3) 
 
 ν α , ϱ ​ ( η ) ≔ 1 𝒵 α , ϱ ​ ( ϱ 1 + ϱ ) | η | ​ ∏ x ∈ V Γ ⁡ ( α x + η x ) Γ ⁡ ( α x ) ​ η x ! ​ , with ​ 𝒵 α , ϱ ≔ ( 1 + ϱ ) | α | ​ . \nu_{\alpha,\varrho}(\eta)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\mathcal{Z}_{\alpha,\varrho}}\left(\frac{\varrho}{1+\varrho}\right)^{|\eta|}\prod_{x\in V}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+\eta_{x})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x})\,\eta_{x}!}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{with}\ \mathcal{Z}_{\alpha,\varrho}\coloneqq(1+\varrho)^{|\alpha|}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Note that  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  in ( 2.3 ) is the canonical measure associated to the grand-canonical  ν α , ϱ \nu_{\alpha,\varrho} .
One speaks of  non-equilibrium  if  θ \theta  is non-constant on  supp ⁡ ( ω ) {\rm supp}(\omega) . Then,  μ G , α , ω , θ \mu_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}  is, in general, neither reversible, nor in product form, nor explicit. We will not need to know more on the structure of this steady state; for further details, see, e.g.,  [ FRS22 ]  and references therein. 
 
 
 
 6.2.  Spectral gap identity when  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0 

 
 We start with the analysis of the purely absorbing  SIP {\rm SIP} . As already anticipated in Section  1.4 , we shall see that understanding the case  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0  is one of the key ingredients for the spectral analysis of the setting with general  θ \theta . 
 
 
 Observe that substituting  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0  in ( 6.1 ) gives rise to a system in which all particles are never created, and get annihilated one at the time, until the system eventually gets empty. In particular,  L G , α , ω , 0 L_{G,\alpha,\omega,0} , interpreted as an infinite matrix, is similar to a block lower triangular one. Moreover, the blocks on the diagonal — each for any total number of particles  k ∈ ℕ 0 k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}  in the bulk of the system — are of finite size and describe the following finite-state Markov chains.
The case  k = 1 k=1  corresponds to a process with just one particle in the bulk, which moves as  RW ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm RW}(G,\alpha)  defined in Section  2 , additionally killed at rate  ω x ≥ 0 \omega_{x}\geq 0  when sitting on  x ∈ V x\in V . We refer to this process as  RW ⁡ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega) . The case  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  is similar, with  k k  interacting particles evolving in the bulk as  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha) , each independently killed with rate  ω x ≥ 0 \omega_{x}\geq 0  when sitting at  x ∈ V x\in V . Each of these Markov chains, say  SIP k ​ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha,\omega) , is sub-stochastic. Moreover, since the conservative  SIP k ​ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha)  admits  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  in ( 2.3 ) as its unique reversible measure, all eigenvalues of (the negative of) the generator of  SIP k ​ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha,\omega)  (see ( 7.1 )) are real; as the conservative system is irreducible,  supp ⁡ ( ω ) ≠ ∅ {\rm supp}(\omega)\neq\varnothing  further ensures that such eigenvalues are strictly positive.
Letting  gap k ​ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha,\omega) ,  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , be the lowest of such eigenvalues, we write (cf. ( 2.5 )) 
 

 
 (6.4) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α , ω ) = inf k ≥ 1 gap k ​ ( G , α , ω ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega)=\inf_{k\geq 1}{\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha,\omega)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Similarly, we introduce the spectral gap of  RW ⁡ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)  as 
 

 
 (6.5) 
 
 gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) = gap 1 ​ ( G , α , ω ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)={\rm gap}_{1}(G,\alpha,\omega)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Our main result of this section establishes that, as soon as the killing rate  ω \omega  is not identically zero, the spectral gaps in ( 6.4 ) and ( 6.5 ) coincide, regardless of the underlying geometry and of the site weights — even when  α min ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) \alpha_{\rm min}\in(0,1) . This spectral gap identity stands in stark contrast to the behavior observed in the conservative setting, where restrictions are imposed on the value of  α min \alpha_{\rm min} . 
 
 
 Theorem 6.1   ( θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0 ) . 
 
 For all graphs  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V}) , and site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} ,  ω = ( ω x ) x ∈ V \omega=(\omega_{x})_{x\in V}  with  ω ≠ 0 \omega\neq 0 , we have 
 

 
 (6.6) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α , ω ) = gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega)={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remarkably, the proof of the above identity (deferred to Section  7.1 ) is quite elementary. 
 
 
 
 6.3.  General setting 

 
 When dealing with the general case  θ ≠ 0 \theta\neq 0 , we must specify the infinite-dimensional function analytic setting that we work in. For this purpose, we define a class of functions on  Ξ \Xi  (Definition  6.2 ), written in terms of orthogonal “Meixner” polynomial dualities introduced in  [ FRS22 , Section 4] , which take the following form: for all  ϱ ≥ 0 \varrho\geq 0 , 
 

 
 (6.7) 
 
 D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , η ) = ∏ x ∈ V d α x , ϱ ​ ( ξ x , η x ) ​ , η ∈ Ξ ​ , ​ ξ ∈ Ξ k ​ , ​ k ≥ 0 ​ , D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\eta)=\prod_{x\in V}d_{\alpha_{x},\varrho}(\xi_{x},\eta_{x})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Xi\;\text{,}\;\;\xi\in\Xi_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;k\geq 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where, for  ϱ = 0 \varrho=0 , 
 

 
 (6.8) 
 
 d α x , 0 ( ξ x , η x ) = η x ! ( η x − ξ x ) ! Γ ⁡ ( α x ) Γ ⁡ ( α x + ξ x ) 𝟙 { ξ x ≤ η x } , \displaystyle d_{\alpha_{x},0}(\xi_{x},\eta_{x})=\frac{\eta_{x}!}{\left(\eta_{x}-\xi_{x}\right)!}\frac{\Gamma(\alpha_{x})}{\Gamma(\alpha_{x}+\xi_{x})}\mathds{1}_{\left\{\xi_{x}\leq\eta_{x}\right\}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 whereas, for  ϱ > 0 \varrho>0 , 
 

 
 (6.9) 
 
 d α x , ϱ ​ ( ξ x , η x ) = ∑ ℓ x = 0 ξ x ( ξ x ℓ x ) ​ d α x , 0 ​ ( ℓ x , η x ) ​ ( − ϱ ) ξ x − ℓ x = ( − ϱ ) ξ k ​ F 1 2 ​ ( − ξ x , − η x , α x , − ϱ − 1 ) ​ . d_{\alpha_{x},\varrho}(\xi_{x},\eta_{x})=\sum_{\ell_{x}=0}^{\xi_{x}}\binom{\xi_{x}}{\ell_{x}}\,d_{\alpha_{x},0}(\ell_{x},\eta_{x})\left(-\varrho\right)^{\xi_{x}-\ell_{x}}=\left(-\varrho\right)^{\xi_{k}}{{}_{2}F_{1}(-\xi_{x},-\eta_{x};\alpha_{x};-\varrho^{-1})}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This last identity is just a rewriting in terms of the ordinary hypergeometric function. Let us observe that  D α , ϱ ​ ( ∅ , ⋅ ) ≡ 1 D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\emptyset,\,\cdot\,)\equiv 1  and that, for all  k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N}  and  ξ ∈ Ξ k \xi\in\Xi_{k} ,  η ∈ Ξ ⟼ D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , η ) ∈ ℝ \eta\in\Xi\longmapsto D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\eta)\in\mathbb{R}  is a  k k -th order polynomial in  η = ( η x ) x ∈ V \eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in V} . Moreover, as we shall show in Section  7.2  below, 
 

 
 (6.10) 
 
 { D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , ⋅ ) : | ξ | ≤ k } \{D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\,\cdot\,):|\xi|\leq k\} 
 
 
 is a linearly independent set which spans the space of polynomials in  η \eta  of order  ≤ k \leq k . This and the two other properties presented in Proposition  7.2  below turn the functions in ( 6.7 ) into a convenient basis of polynomials on  Ξ \Xi . 
 
 
 Definition 6.2 . 
 
 For all  ϱ > 0 \varrho>0 , integers  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , and functions  ψ ∈ ℝ Ξ k \psi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} , define 
 

 
 (6.11) 
 
 F α , ϱ ψ ​ ( η ) ≔ ∑ ξ ∈ Ξ k μ α , k ​ ( ξ ) ​ ψ ​ ( ξ ) ​ D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , η ) ​ , η ∈ Ξ ​ . F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}(\eta)\coloneqq\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_{k}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\xi)\,\psi(\xi)\,D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\eta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta\in\Xi\;\text{.} 
 
 
 When  k = 0 k=0  and, thus,  ψ ∈ ℝ \psi\in\mathbb{R}  is a scalar, we conventionally set  F α , ϱ ψ ≡ ψ ∈ ℝ F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}\equiv\psi\in\mathbb{R} . 
 
 
 
 Remark that, as long as  ψ ≠ 0 \psi\neq 0 , the function in ( 6.11 ) is a non-vanishing  k k -th order polynomial in the variables  η = ( η x ) x ∈ V \eta=(\eta_{x})_{x\in V} . In particular, expressions like  L G , α , ω , θ ​ F α , ϱ ψ L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}  will always be well defined.
Finally, introduce the operators
 𝔟 α , ω , ϱ , k − 1 θ : ℝ Ξ k → ℝ Ξ k − 1 \mathfrak{b}_{\alpha,\omega,\varrho,k-1}^{\theta}:\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\to\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k-1}} ,  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , acting on functions  ψ ∈ ℝ Ξ k \psi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  as 
 

 
 (6.12) 
 
 𝔟 α , ω , ϱ , k − 1 θ ​ ψ ​ ( ζ ) ≔ k ​ ∑ x ∈ V ω x ​ ( θ x − ϱ ) ​ α x + ζ x | α | + k − 1 ​ ψ ​ ( ζ + δ x ) ​ , ζ ∈ Ξ k − 1 ​ . \mathfrak{b}_{\alpha,\omega,\varrho,k-1}^{\theta}\psi(\zeta)\coloneqq k\sum_{x\in V}\omega_{x}\left(\theta_{x}-\varrho\right)\frac{\alpha_{x}+\zeta_{x}}{|\alpha|+k-1}\,\psi(\zeta+\delta_{x})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\zeta\in\Xi_{k-1}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Clearly, we have 
 

 
 (6.13) 
 
 𝔟 α , ω , ϱ , k − 1 θ = 0 ​ , if ​ θ ≡ ϱ ​ . \mathfrak{b}_{\alpha,\omega,\varrho,k-1}^{\theta}=0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{if}\ \theta\equiv\varrho\;\text{.} 
 
 
 The following theorem, whose proof is postponed to Section  7.2  below, is stated for any graph  G G  and site weights  α \alpha ,  ω \omega , and  θ \theta . 
 
 
 Theorem 6.3 . 
 
 Fix  ϱ > 0 \varrho>0  and recall Definition  6.2 . 
 
 (1)  
 
 Given  k ≥ 0 k\geq 0 , any eigenpair  λ , ψ \lambda,\psi  of the  k k -particle purely-absorbing system solves 
 

 
 (6.14) 
 
 L G , α , ω , θ ​ F α , ϱ ψ = − λ ​ F α , ϱ ψ + F α , ϱ 𝔟 α , ω , ϱ , k − 1 θ ​ ψ ​ . L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}=-\lambda\,F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}+F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha,\omega,\varrho,k-1}^{\theta}\psi}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 (2)  
 
 The functions  F α , ϱ ψ F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}  as in Definition  6.2  may be chosen to form an orthogonal basis of  L 2 ​ ( ν α , ϱ ) L^{2}(\nu_{\alpha,\varrho}) . 
 
 
 
 
 
 Let us comment on two powerful consequences of the above theorem in the reversible case  θ ≡ ϱ \theta\equiv\varrho  (we discuss the non-reversible case in Remark  6.5  below; see also Remark  6.6  for a comparison with the symmetric  exclusion  process).
The first item tells us that any eigenvalue of the purely absorbing system with  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1  particles is one also for the general system and, moreover, it provides a constructive procedure to build eigenfunctions from those of the  k k -particle absorbing system. Indeed, by ( 6.13 ), the identity in ( 6.14 ) becomes the eigenvalue equation 
 

 
 (6.15) 
 
 L G , α , ω , θ ​ F α , ϱ ψ = − λ ​ F α , ϱ ψ ​ , L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}=-\lambda\,F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 because the second term on the right-hand side of ( 6.14 ) vanishes when  θ ≡ ϱ \theta\equiv\varrho .
The second item guarantees the existence of a complete orthonormal system of eigenfunctions of  L G , α , ω , θ L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta} . This, combined with the fact that the generator is self-adjoint in the associated  L 2 L^{2} -space, ensures that  − L G , α , ω , θ -L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}  has a pure point spectrum therein, all contained in  [ 0 , ∞ ) [0,\infty) , with a gap after the zero eigenvalue coinciding with that of the purely absorbing system.
Letting 
 

 
 (6.16) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α , ω , ϱ ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\varrho) 
 
 
 denote this spectral gap when  θ ≡ ϱ > 0 \theta\equiv\varrho>0 , Theorem  6.1  readily implies the following result. 
 
 
 Corollary 6.4   ( θ ≡ ϱ \theta\equiv\varrho ) . 
 
 For all graphs  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V}) , and site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} ,  ω = ( ω x ) x ∈ V \omega=(\omega_{x})_{x\in V}  with  ω ≠ 0 \omega\neq 0 , we have 
 

 
 (6.17) 
 
 gap SIP ​ ( G , α , ω , ϱ ) = gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) ​ , ϱ > 0 ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\varrho)={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\varrho>0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 The above result is stated in the reversible setting  θ ≡ ϱ \theta\equiv\varrho . The next two remarks clarify the corresponding non-reversible picture: for the infinite-state  SIP \mathrm{SIP}  one has to distinguish algebraic eigenfunction lifting from a full spectral statement on a prescribed function space (Remark  6.5 ), whereas in finite-state analogues such as the partial symmetric exclusion process this distinction disappears (Remark  6.6 ). 
 
 
 Remark 6.5   (Non-reversible setting) . 
 
 In view of Theorem  6.3 , it is plausible to expect that Corollary  6.4  should extend, in some sense, to the non-reversible setting as well. Indeed, since the two functions on the right-hand side of ( 6.14 ) are polynomials of order  k k  and  ℓ ≤ k − 1 \ell\leq k-1 , this guarantees that  λ \lambda  therein lies in the point spectrum of  L G , α , ω , θ L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta} , for all  θ = ( θ x ) x ∈ V \theta=(\theta_{x})_{x\in V} . However, in absence of a natural Hilbert space ensuring that such eigenfunctions form a complete orthonormal system when  θ ≢ const . \theta\not\equiv{\rm const.}  on  supp ⁡ ( ω ) {\rm supp}(\omega) , the presence, e.g., of a continuous part of the spectrum (a property which also depends on the choice of the underlying reference function space) is what Theorem  6.3  does not exclude. 
 
 
 
 Remark 6.6   (Symmetric exclusion processes & Co.) . 
 
 The proof of Theorem  6.3 1  — based on orthogonal-polynomial dualities, annihilation and creation operators — carries over to other non-equilibrium systems enjoying similar properties, e.g.,  [ RS18 ,  FRS22 ,  GRT25 ] . As an example, we mention the symmetric (partial) exclusion process ( SEP {\rm SEP} ) in contact with reservoirs (see, e.g.,  [ FRS22 ,  Sal23 ,  Sal24 ] ), whose generator is readily obtained from  L G , α , ω , θ L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}  in ( 6.1 ) by further imposing  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V ⊂ ℕ \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V}\subset\mathbb{N}  and  θ = ( θ x ) x ∈ V ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] \theta=(\theta_{x})_{x\in V}\in[0,1] , and changing the plus signs in  α x + η x \alpha_{x}+\eta_{x}  and  1 + θ x 1+\theta_{x}  therein into minus signs.
In this case, the configuration space is simply
 ∏ x ∈ V { 0 , 1 , … , α x } \prod_{x\in V}\{0,1,\ldots,\alpha_{x}\} , thus finite, and the technical issues explained in
Remark  6.5  do not arise: the spectrum is just the union of finitely many
eigenvalues. 
 
 
 As our proof arguments
used for  SIP {\rm SIP}  adapt to the open  SEP {\rm SEP} , one obtains analogues of Theorems  6.1 
and  6.3 : on the one hand, the eigenvalues of the open  SEP {\rm SEP}  with arbitrary
reservoir densities  θ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] V \theta\in[0,1]^{V}  coincide with those of the purely absorbing case
 θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0 , and are therefore independent of  θ \theta ; on the other hand, importing the notation used
above, one has  gap SEP ​ ( G , α , ω ) = gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SEP}(G,\alpha,\omega)={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega) . These two results further ensure that 
 

 
 (6.18) 
 
 gap SEP ​ ( G , α , ω , θ ) = gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) ​ , θ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] V ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm SEP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\theta)={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\theta\in[0,1]^{V}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This, thus, recovers, in the reversible
case, the spectral-gap identity of  [ Sal23 , Corollary 1] , and, for
general reservoir densities, the corresponding result of  [ Sal24 , Theorem 2] . 
 
 
 
 
 
 7.  Proofs of Theorems  6.1  and  6.3 

 
 The following two sections may be essentially read independently. 
 
 
 7.1.  Proof of Theorem  6.1 

 
 For every  k ≥ 1 k\geq 1 , the  k k -particle purely absorbing system (i.e., when  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0 ) is described by the infinitesimal generator 
 

 
 (7.1) 
 
 L G , α , ω , k ​ f ​ ( η ) ≔ L G , α , k ​ f ​ ( η ) − f ⁡ ( η ) ​ ∑ x ∈ V ω x ​ η x ​ , L_{G,\alpha,\omega,k}f(\eta)\coloneqq L_{G,\alpha,k}f(\eta)-f(\eta)\sum_{x\in V}\omega_{x}\,\eta_{x}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}} ,  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k} , and  L G , α , k L_{G,\alpha,k}  given in ( 2.2 ). Equivalently, if  f ∈ ℝ Ξ k f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  is extended to  Ξ \Xi  by setting  f ≡ 0 f\equiv 0  on  Ξ ∖ Ξ k \Xi\setminus\Xi_{k} , then the restriction to  Ξ k \Xi_{k}  of the generator in ( 6.1 ) with  θ ≡ 0 \theta\equiv 0  is precisely the operator in ( 7.1 ). Note that, when the process is in configuration  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k} , it gets killed with rate  ∑ x ∈ V ω x ​ η x ≥ 0 \sum_{x\in V}\omega_{x}\,\eta_{x}\geq 0 . 
 
 
 Recall the definition of the probability measure  μ α , k \mu_{\alpha,k}  in ( 2.3 ). Since  L G , α , ω , k L_{G,\alpha,\omega,k}  is the sum of a self-adjoint operator on  L 2 ​ ( μ α , k ) L^{2}(\mu_{\alpha,k})  and a multiplicative one, it is self-adjoint on  L 2 ​ ( μ α , k ) L^{2}(\mu_{\alpha,k})  and, thus, all the eigenvalues of  − L G , α , ω , k -L_{G,\alpha,\omega,k}  are real and non-negative. Additionally, since the conservative dynamics is irreducible and the multiplication operator is non-positive, all such eigenvalues are actually strictly positive. In particular, we obtain that  gap k ​ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha,\omega) , the lowest of such eigenvalues, is positive and, by the Perron-Frobenius theorem, characterized as the worst-case exponential killing rate for large times: letting  τ k \tau_{k}  denote the first time at which one of the  k k  particles gets killed, 
 

 
 (7.2) 
 
 gap k ( G , α , ω ) = − lim t → ∞ 1 t log max η ∈ Ξ k ℙ η G , α , ω , k [ τ k > t ] . {\rm gap}_{k}(G,\alpha,\omega)=-\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{1}{t}\log\max_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{G,\alpha,\omega,k}[\tau_{k}>t]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Thanks to this asymptotic identity, the assertion of Theorem  6.1  boils down to show that, for all  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , 
 

 
 (7.3) 
 
 max η ∈ Ξ k ℙ η G , α , ω , k [ τ k > t ] ≤ max x ∈ V ℙ δ x G , α , ω , 1 [ τ 1 > t ] , t ≥ 0 . \max_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{G,\alpha,\omega,k}[\tau_{k}>t]\leq\max_{x\in V}\mathds{P}_{\delta_{x}}^{G,\alpha,\omega,1}[\tau_{1}>t]\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad t\geq 0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 For this purpose, we introduce a lookdown representation of the particle system with killing, extending the one known within the conservative setting  [ KS24 , Appendix A] .
We need to introduce a bit of notation. For every  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , labeled configuration  𝐱 = ( x 1 , … , x k ) ∈ V k \mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\in V^{k} , and permutation  ς ∈ S k \varsigma\in S_{k} , we write  ς ​ 𝐱 = ( x ς ⁡ ( 1 ) , … , x ς ⁡ ( k ) ) ∈ V k \varsigma\mathbf{x}=(x_{\varsigma(1)},\ldots,x_{\varsigma(k)})\in V^{k} . Further,  𝒮 k : ℝ V k → ℝ V k \mathcal{S}_{k}:\mathbb{R}^{V^{k}}\to\mathbb{R}^{V^{k}}  denotes the symmetrization operator, that is, 
 

 
 (7.4) 
 
 𝒮 k ​ φ ​ ( 𝐱 ) = 1 k ! ​ ∑ ς ∈ S k φ ⁡ ( ς ​ 𝐱 ) ​ , φ ∈ ℝ V k ​ , \mathcal{S}_{k}\varphi(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{k!}\sum_{\varsigma\in S_{k}}\varphi(\varsigma\mathbf{x})\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{V^{k}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 whereas  Φ k ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ i = 1 k δ x i ∈ Ξ k \Phi_{k}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\delta_{x_{i}}\in\Xi_{k}  is the operator which removes the particles’ labels. 
 
 
 Proposition 7.1   (Lookdown representation of  SIP {\rm SIP}  with killing) . 
 
 For all  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , we have 
 

 
 (7.5) 
 
 𝒮 k ​ ℒ G , α , ω , k ​ ( f ∘ Φ k ) = ( L G , α , ω , k ​ f ) ∘ Φ k ​ , f ∈ ℝ Ξ k ​ , \mathcal{S}_{k}{\mathscr{L}}_{G,\alpha,\omega,k}(f\circ\Phi_{k})=(L_{G,\alpha,\omega,k}f)\circ\Phi_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  ℒ G , α , ω , k {\mathscr{L}}_{G,\alpha,\omega,k}  denotes the generator of the lookdown  SIP k ​ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm SIP}_{k}(G,\alpha,\omega)  given, for all  φ ∈ ℝ V k \varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{V^{k}}  and  𝐱 ∈ V k \mathbf{x}\in V^{k} , by 
 

 
 (7.6) 
 
 ℒ G , α , ω , k ​ φ ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ℒ G , α , k ​ φ ​ ( 𝐱 ) − φ ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ∑ i = 1 k ω x i ​ . {\mathscr{L}}_{G,\alpha,\omega,k}\varphi(\mathbf{x})={\mathscr{L}}_{G,\alpha,k}\varphi(\mathbf{x})-\varphi(\mathbf{x})\sum_{i=1}^{k}\omega_{x_{i}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  ℒ G , α , k ​ φ ​ ( 𝐱 ) {\mathscr{L}}_{G,\alpha,k}\varphi(\mathbf{x})  reads as 
 

 
 (7.7) 
 
 ℒ G , α , k ​ φ ​ ( 𝐱 ) = ∑ i = 1 k ∑ x , y ∈ V c x ​ y ​ δ x , x i ​ ( α y + 2 ​ ∑ j = 1 i − 1 δ y , x j ) ​ ( φ ⁡ ( 𝐱 i y ) − φ ⁡ ( 𝐱 ) ) ​ , {\mathscr{L}}_{G,\alpha,k}\varphi(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\sum_{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\,\delta_{x,x_{i}}\left(\alpha_{y}+2\sum_{j=1}^{i-1}\delta_{y,x_{j}}\right)\left(\varphi(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{y})-\varphi(\mathbf{x})\right)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and stands for the conservative part of the lookdown dynamics, with  𝐱 i y ∈ V k \mathbf{x}_{i}^{y}\in V^{k}  denoting the configuration obtained from  𝐱 ∈ V k \mathbf{x}\in V^{k}  by setting the  i i -th coordinate equal to  y ∈ V y\in V . 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 By  [ KS24 , Proposition A.4] , an identity which is analogous to that in ( 7.5 ) holds for the conservative dynamics, i.e., 
 

 
 (7.8) 
 
 𝒮 k ​ ℒ G , α , k ​ ( f ∘ Φ k ) = ( L G , α , k ​ f ) ∘ Φ k ​ , f ∈ ℝ Ξ k ​ . \mathcal{S}_{k}{\mathscr{L}}_{G,\alpha,k}(f\circ\Phi_{k})=(L_{G,\alpha,k}f)\circ\Phi_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad f\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Hence, by linearity, it suffices to prove the identity only for the multiplication operators in ( 7.1 ) and ( 7.6 ) describing the killing part. Nevertheless, this identity holds trivially because particles are killed independently (or, equivalently, because  𝐱 = ( x 1 , … , x k ) ↦ ( f ∘ Φ k ) ​ ( 𝐱 ) ​ ∑ i = 1 k ω x i \mathbf{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})\mapsto(f\circ\Phi_{k})(\mathbf{x})\sum_{i=1}^{k}\omega_{x_{i}}  is invariant under permutations).
∎ 
 
 
 
 We now conclude the proof of Theorem  6.1 . 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  6.1 .

 (7.9)

 7.2.  Proof of Theorem  6.3 

 
 Our main ingredient will be the duality property of  SIP {\rm SIP}  in contact with reservoirs (e.g.,  [ GKRV09 ,  CGGR13 ] ), in particular, the orthogonal polynomial duality functions found in  [ FRS22 ] . We shall not assume any prior knowledge on this topic, but rather exploit a few properties taken from  [ KLS10 ,  RS18 ,  FRS22 ] , which, for the reader’s convenience, we collect in the following proposition. For notational convenience, we simply write
 L θ = L G , α , ω , θ L_{\theta}=L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}  and  L k = L G , α , ω , k L_{k}=L_{G,\alpha,\omega,k}  (defined in ( 6.1 ) and ( 7.1 ), respectively). 
 
 
 Proposition 7.2 . 
 
 Fix  ϱ > 0 \varrho>0 . 
 
 (1)  
 
 Orthogonality and completeness.  Let  ⟨ ⋅ | ⋅ ⟩ α , ϱ \left\langle\,\cdot\,\,\middle|\,\,\cdot\,\right\rangle_{\alpha,\varrho}  denote the inner product in  L 2 ​ ( ν α , ϱ ) L^{2}(\nu_{\alpha,\varrho})  (see ( 6.3 )). Then, for all integers  k , ℓ ≥ 1 k,\ell\geq 1  and  ξ ∈ Ξ k \xi\in\Xi_{k} ,  ζ ∈ Ξ ℓ \zeta\in\Xi_{\ell} , 
 

 
 (7.11) 
 
 ⟨ D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , ⋅ ) | D α , ϱ ​ ( ζ , ⋅ ) ⟩ α , ϱ = 𝟙 { ξ = ζ } ϱ k ​ ( 1 + ϱ ) k Z α , k μ α , k ( ξ ) − 1 ​ . \displaystyle\begin{aligned} \left\langle D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\,\cdot\,)\,\middle|\,D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\zeta,\,\cdot\,)\right\rangle_{\alpha,\varrho}&={\mathds{1}_{\{\xi=\zeta\}}}\,\frac{\varrho^{k}\left(1+\varrho\right)^{k}}{Z_{\alpha,k}}\,{\mu_{\alpha,k}(\xi)^{-1}}\end{aligned}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Moreover,  { D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , ⋅ ) } k ≥ 0 , ξ ∈ Ξ k \left\{D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\,\cdot\,)\right\}_{k\geq 0,\,\xi\in\Xi_{k}}  is a complete system of  L 2 ​ ( ν α , ϱ ) L^{2}(\nu_{\alpha,\varrho}) . 
 
 (2)  
 
 Duality.  For all  θ = ( θ x ) x ∈ V \theta=(\theta_{x})_{x\in V}  and  η ∈ Ξ \eta\in\Xi , we have 
 

 
 (7.12) 
 
 L θ ​ D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , ⋅ ) ​ ( η ) = L k ​ D α , ϱ ​ ( ⋅ , η ) ​ ( ξ ) + ∑ x ∈ V ω x ξ x ( θ x − ϱ ) D α , ϱ ( ξ − δ x , η ) . \displaystyle\begin{aligned} L_{\theta}D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\,\cdot\,)(\eta)&=L_{k}D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\,\cdot\,,\eta)(\xi)\\
&+\sum_{x\in V}\omega_{x}\,\xi_{x}\left(\theta_{x}-\varrho\right)D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi-\delta_{x},\eta)\;\text{.}\end{aligned} 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Remark 7.3 . 
 
 Since  { D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , ⋅ ) } ℓ ≤ k , ξ ∈ Ξ ℓ \left\{D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\,\cdot\,)\right\}_{\ell\leq k,\,\xi\in\Xi_{\ell}}  spans all polynomials of order  ≤ k \leq k , by ( 7.12 ),  L θ = L G , α , ω , θ L_{\theta}=L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}  leaves invariant such a subspace of polynomials. 
 
 
 
 Proof of Theorem  6.3 . 
 
 The rest of the proof of Theorem  6.3  is routine. Indeed, Theorem  6.3 1  follows by linearity and the duality relation in ( 7.12 ): recalling  F α , ϱ ψ F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}  from Definition  6.2 , first we obtain 
 

 
 (7.13) 
 
 L θ ​ F α , ϱ ψ ​ ( η ) \displaystyle L_{\theta}F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}(\eta) 
 = ∑ ξ ∈ Ξ k μ α , k ​ ( ξ ) ​ ψ ​ ( ξ ) ​ L θ ​ D α , ϱ ​ ( ξ , ⋅ ) ​ ( η ) \displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_{k}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\xi)\,\psi(\xi)\,L_{\theta}D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi,\,\cdot\,)(\eta) 
 
 
 (7.14) 
 
 
 = ∑ ξ ∈ Ξ k μ α , k ​ ( ξ ) ​ ψ ​ ( ξ ) ​ L k ​ D α , ϱ ​ ( ⋅ , η ) ​ ( ξ ) \displaystyle=\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_{k}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\xi)\,\psi(\xi)\,L_{k}D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\,\cdot\,,\eta)(\xi) 
 
 
 (7.15) 
 
 
 + ∑ ξ ∈ Ξ k μ α , k ( ξ ) ψ ( ξ ) ∑ x ∈ V ω x ξ x ( θ x − ϱ ) D α , ϱ ( ξ − δ x , η ) , \displaystyle+\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_{k}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\xi)\,\psi(\xi)\sum_{x\in V}\omega_{x}\,\xi_{x}\left(\theta_{x}-\varrho\right)D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\xi-\delta_{x},\eta)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which, by ( 3.12 ) and the definition of  𝔟 α , ω , ϱ , k − 1 ϱ \mathfrak{b}_{\alpha,\omega,\varrho,k-1}^{\varrho}  in ( 6.12 ), rearranges as 
 

 
 (7.16) 
 
 
 ∑ ξ ∈ Ξ k μ α , k ​ ( ξ ) ​ ψ ​ ( ξ ) ​ L k ​ D α , ϱ ​ ( ⋅ , η ) ​ ( ξ ) + F α , ϱ 𝔟 α , ω , θ , k − 1 ϱ ​ ψ ​ . \displaystyle\sum_{\xi\in\Xi_{k}}\mu_{\alpha,k}(\xi)\,\psi(\xi)\,L_{k}D_{\alpha,\varrho}(\,\cdot\,,\eta)(\xi)+F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\mathfrak{b}_{\alpha,\omega,\theta,k-1}^{\varrho}\psi}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By the symmetry of  L k L_{k}  in  L 2 ​ ( μ α , k ) L^{2}(\mu_{\alpha,k}) , we finally obtain 
 

 
 (7.17)
