 and the desired claim follows if  L k ​ ψ = − λ ​ ψ L_{k}\psi=-\lambda\,\psi . 
 
 
 The second item in Theorem  6.3  is a direct consequence of the symmetry of  L k L_{k}  in  L 2 ​ ( μ α , k ) L^{2}(\mu_{\alpha,k})  (thus, the orthogonality relation of eigenfunctions  ψ . φ ∈ ℝ Ξ l \psi.\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{l}}  associated to distinct eigenvalues) and the orthogonality relation in ( 7.11 ): for all integers  k , ℓ ≥ 0 k,\ell\geq 0 , all eigenfunctions  ψ ∈ ℝ Ξ k \psi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{k}}  and  φ ∈ ℝ Ξ ℓ \varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{\Xi_{\ell}}  of  L k L_{k}  and  L ℓ L_{\ell} , respectively, we have 
 

 
 (7.18) 
 
 ⟨ F α , ϱ ψ | F α , ϱ φ ⟩ α , ϱ = 𝟙 { k = ℓ } 𝔠 α , ϱ , k ⟨ ψ | φ ⟩ α , k , \displaystyle\big\langle F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\psi}\big|F_{\alpha,\varrho}^{\varphi}\big\rangle_{\alpha,\varrho}=\mathds{1}_{\left\{k=\ell\right\}}\,\mathfrak{c}_{\alpha,\varrho,k}\left\langle\psi\,\middle|\,\varphi\right\rangle_{\alpha,k}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 for some constant  𝔠 α , ϱ , k > 0 \mathfrak{c}_{\alpha,\varrho,k}>0 . 
 
 
 The completeness of the functions in ( 6.10 ) follows from a
one-dimensional density result. Each marginal of  ν α , ϱ \nu_{\alpha,\varrho}  is a
negative-binomial measure, hence has exponential tails and a determinate
Hamburger moment problem. By the classical theorem of M. Riesz  [ Rie24 ]  (see also  [ Akh65 , Chapter 2, Section 3 and Corollary 2.3.3] ,  [ BC81 ,  Ber96 ] , and references therein), determinacy
implies density of algebraic polynomials in the corresponding  L 2 L^{2} -space. Since
 V V  is finite and  ν α , ϱ \nu_{\alpha,\varrho}  is a product measure on  ℕ 0 V \mathbb{N}_{0}^{V} ,
tensor products of these univariate polynomial bases are dense in
 L 2 ​ ( ν α , ϱ ) L^{2}(\nu_{\alpha,\varrho}) , yielding the desired completeness.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 
 8.  Extensions and related models 

 
 In this last section, we extend our results to the Brownian energy process, and briefly discuss their connections with what is known for some closely related models. 
 
 
 8.1.  Brownian energy process 

 
 Given a graph  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})  and site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} , the corresponding Brownian energy process — referred to as  BEP ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm BEP}(G,\alpha)  — is the diffusion on the probability simplex 
 

 
 (8.1) 
 
 Σ ≔ { ζ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ] V : ∑ x ∈ V ζ x = 1 } ​ , \varSigma\coloneqq\left\{\zeta\in[0,1]^{V}:\sum_{x\in V}\zeta_{x}=1\right\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 with infinitesimal generator 
 

 
 (8.2) 
 
 ℒ G , α = 1 2 ∑ x , y ∈ V c x ​ y { − ( α y ζ x − α x ζ y ) ( ∂ ζ x − ∂ ζ y ) + ζ x ζ y ( ∂ ζ x − ∂ ζ y ) 2 } , \mathcal{L}_{G,\alpha}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\left\{-\left(\alpha_{y}\zeta_{x}-\alpha_{x}\zeta_{y}\right)\left(\partial_{\zeta_{x}}-\partial_{\zeta_{y}}\right)+\zeta_{x}\zeta_{y}\left(\partial_{\zeta_{x}}-\partial_{\zeta_{y}}\right)^{2}\right\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and the following Dirichlet distribution on  Σ \varSigma  as its unique reversible measure 
 

 
 (8.3) 
 
 γ α ​ ( d ​ ζ ) = ( 1 B ⁡ ( α ) ​ ∏ x ∈ V ζ x α x − 1 ) ​ d ​ ζ ​ , with ​ B ​ ( α ) ≔ ∏ x ∈ V Γ ⁡ ( α x ) Γ ⁡ ( | α | ) ​ . \gamma_{\alpha}({\rm d}\zeta)=\left(\frac{1}{{\rm B}(\alpha)}\prod_{x\in V}\zeta_{x}^{\alpha_{x}-1}\right){\rm d}\zeta\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\text{with}\ {\rm B}(\alpha)\coloneqq\frac{\prod_{x\in V}\Gamma(\alpha_{x})}{\Gamma(|\alpha|)}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here,  d ​ ζ {\rm d}\zeta  stands for the uniform measure on  Σ \varSigma . We refer to Section  1 ,  [ KS24 ]  and references therein, for more information and history on this model. Here, we just mention that its mean field version (i.e., when  c x ​ y ≡ const . c_{xy}\equiv{\rm const.} ) coincides with the renowned multi-type Wright-Fisher diffusion with parent independent mutations (see, e.g.,  [ Shi77 ,  WZ19 ] ). 
 
 
 As shown in  [ KS24 ] ,  BEP ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm BEP}(G,\alpha)  inherits its eigenstructure from that of  SIP ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha) . More precisely, the Poisson intertwining relation  [ KS24 , Eq. (4.1)]  between the generators  ℒ G , α \mathcal{L}_{G,\alpha}  and  L G , α , k L_{G,\alpha,k}  allows to lift eigenfunctions of  SIP ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)  to eigenfunctions of  BEP ⁡ ( G , α ) {\rm BEP}(G,\alpha) , both corresponding to the same eigenvalue. Using this intertwining relation (and, more specifically, that  ℒ G , α \mathcal{L}_{G,\alpha}  maps the finite-dimensional subspace of polynomials of degree at most  k k  into itself) and the fact  ℒ G , α \mathcal{L}_{G,\alpha}  is a self-adjoint operator on  L 2 ​ ( γ α ) L^{2}(\gamma_{\alpha}) , one concludes that this lifting saturates all the spectral information (i.e., the spectrum of  BEP {\rm BEP}  is pure point and coincides with that of  SIP {\rm SIP} ; see  [ KS24 , Sections 1.2 and 4]  for further details). In particular, the corresponding spectral gaps coincide 
 

 
 (8.4) 
 
 gap BEP ​ ( G , α ) = gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm BEP}(G,\alpha)={\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Combining the findings in  [ KS24 ]  with our Theorems  2.2  and  2.4  on  gap SIP ​ ( G , α ) {\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha) , we readily deduce the following analogues of ( 1.4 ) and ( 1.8 ). 
 
 
 Corollary 8.1 . 
 
 For all graphs  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V})  and site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} , we have 
 

 
 (8.5) 
 
 α min ​ { 1 21 ​ α ratio 6 α min / α ratio ​ c min | V | 2 ​ diam ​ ( G ) } ≤ gap BEP ​ ( G , α ) ≤ gap RW ​ ( G , α ) ​ , \alpha_{\rm min}\left\{\frac{1}{21}\,\frac{\alpha_{\rm ratio}}{6^{\alpha_{\rm min}/\alpha_{\rm ratio}}}\,\frac{c_{\rm min}}{|V|^{2}\,{\rm diam}(G)}\right\}\leq{\rm gap}_{\rm BEP}(G,\alpha)\leq{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and, in some cases, we have 
 

 
 (8.6) 
 
 gap BEP ​ ( G , α ) < gap RW ​ ( G , α ) ​ . {\rm gap}_{\rm BEP}(G,\alpha)<{\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Interestingly, also our results on the spectrum of the non-conservative  SIP {\rm SIP} , via similar arguments, translate to the context of the  BEP {\rm BEP} . Indeed, the aforementioned Poisson intertwining is effective also when one adds the interaction with external reservoirs. As shown, e.g., in  [ GRT25 , Section 4.1] , using this operator, the generator  L G , α , ω , θ L_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}  in ( 6.1 ) of  SIP ⁡ ( G , α , ω , θ ) {\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\theta)  satisfies, for all  ω = ( ω x ) x ∈ V \omega=(\omega_{x})_{x\in V}  and  θ = ( θ x ) x ∈ V \theta=(\theta_{x})_{x\in V} , an analogous intertwining relation with 
 

 
 (8.7) 
 
 ℒ G , α , ω , θ = ℒ G , α + ∑ x ∈ V ω x { ( α x θ x − ζ x ) ∂ ζ x + θ x ζ x ∂ ζ x 2 } , \mathcal{L}_{G,\alpha,\omega,\theta}=\mathcal{L}_{G,\alpha}+\sum_{x\in V}\omega_{x}\left\{\left(\alpha_{x}\theta_{x}-\zeta_{x}\right)\partial_{\zeta_{x}}+\theta_{x}\zeta_{x}\,\partial_{\zeta_{x}}^{2}\right\}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 the generator of what we refer to as  BEP ⁡ ( G , α , ω , θ ) {\rm BEP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\theta) . 
 
 
 When  θ ≡ ϱ > 0 \theta\equiv\varrho>0 , this non-conservative diffusion on  [ 0 , ∞ ) V [0,\infty)^{V}  is well known to have the following product measure  γ α , ϱ = ⊗ x ∈ V Gamma ( α x , ϱ ) \gamma_{\alpha,\varrho}=\otimes_{x\in V}\,{\rm Gamma}(\alpha_{x},\varrho)  as its unique reversible measure. Hence, arguing as above, we derive the corresponding spectral gap identity 
 

 
 (8.8) 
 
 gap BEP ​ ( G , α , ω , ϱ ) = gap SIP ​ ( G , α , ω , ϱ ) ​ , {\rm gap}_{\rm BEP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\varrho)={\rm gap}_{\rm SIP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\varrho)\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 and, as a consequence, the following analogue of Corollary  6.4 . 
 
 
 Corollary 8.2 . 
 
 For all graphs  G = ( V , ( c x ​ y ) x , y ∈ V ) G=(V,(c_{xy})_{x,y\in V}) , and site weights  α = ( α x ) x ∈ V \alpha=(\alpha_{x})_{x\in V} ,  ω = ( ω x ) x ∈ V \omega=(\omega_{x})_{x\in V}  with  ω ≠ 0 \omega\neq 0 , we have 
 

 
 (8.9) 
 
 gap BEP ​ ( G , α , ω , ϱ ) = gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) ​ , ϱ > 0 ​ , {\rm gap}_{\rm BEP}(G,\alpha,\omega,\varrho)={\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\varrho>0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  gap RW ​ ( G , α , ω ) {\rm gap}_{\rm RW}(G,\alpha,\omega)  is defined in ( 6.5 ). 
 
 
 
 
 8.2.  Other models 

 
 As already anticipated in Section  1 ,  SIP {\rm SIP}  and  BEP {\rm BEP}  come with several closely related models, having a different dynamics, but exactly the same steady states. The models we have in mind fall into the class of mass redistribution models. Here, we shall focus only on those models with configuration-independent edge updates (see, e.g.,  [ Sas15 ]  and references therein for configuration-dependent mass redistribution models with Dirichlet-type steady states): after an edge, say  x ​ y xy , is selected with rate  c x ​ y ≥ 0 c_{xy}\geq 0 , particles/energies sitting on  x x  and  y y  get randomly redistributed therein. Among them, let us mention: 
 
 (1)  
 
 the Beta-Binomial splitting process and its continuous version, also known as Beta splitting process or (generalized) KMP model (see, e.g.,  [ PR26 ,  DMFG24 ,  GRT25 ] ); 
 
 (2)  
 
 the discrete and continuous harmonic processes (see, e.g.,  [ FFG23 ,  GRT25 ] ). 
 
 
 All models in (a) and (b) have been studied in their conservative and non-conservative variants (see the aforementioned references for the precise definitions of the redistribution rules), and, just like  SIP {\rm SIP}  and  BEP {\rm BEP} , discrete and corresponding continuous models are isospectral (when reversible), enjoying completely analogous Poisson intertwining relations. 
 
 
 To the best of our knowledge, the only sharp quantitative results on their convergence to equilibrium are confined to a handful of results, see  [ CCL03 ,  Smi14 ,  CLL20 ,  PR26 ]  and references therein. For what concerns the spectral gap, the only sharp results that we are aware of concern the Beta and Beta-Binomial splitting processes in two specific settings, both conservative: 
 
 (1)  
 
 the complete graph with  α ≡ const . \alpha\equiv{\rm const.}   [ CCL03 ]  (see also  [ CLL20 , Remark 4] ); 
 
 (2)  
 
 the segment with  α ≡ const . ≥ 1 \alpha\equiv{\rm const.}\geq 1   [ CLL20 ] . 
 
 
 In particular, in (i), the gap is attained by the system with  k = 2 k=2  particles (which is strictly smaller than  gap 1 {\rm gap}_{1} ), while in (ii) it coincides with that of the corresponding single particle. 
 
 
 In order to obtain spectral gap asymptotics for small diffusivity, the proof ideas of Theorems  2.3  and  2.4  apply also to these models (actually, with considerable simplifications due to the presence of edge updates only, and edges are a.s. never updated simultaneously). For instance, it is not difficult to show that, on graphs like that in Section  5.5  and for  α \alpha  small enough, the spectral gap of the two particles must be strictly smaller than that of the single random walk.
Similarly, the non-conservative (reversible) models satisfy a one-particle spectral gap identity, analogous to the one contained in Corollary  6.4 . Indeed, the key step in that proof (Section  7.1 ) carries over to these other models in contact with reservoirs, because of the analogous intertwining and consistency/duality relations available also for them, see, e.g.,  [ GRT25 ] . We leave the verification of these claims to the interested reader. What remains largely open is the problem of obtaining spectral gap’s sharp lower bounds — and, possibly, identities in terms of simpler processes — for the conservative variants of these mass redistribution models on general geometries. 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix A  Being in  Ω k \Omega_{k}  at each positive time 

 
 In this appendix, we prove a technical result on the probability that the  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} -accelerated  SIP \rm SIP  sits in the absorbing set  Ω k \Omega_{k}  (see ( 5.4 )) at a given positive time. Since  Ω k = Ξ k \Omega_{k}=\Xi_{k}  when  k = 1 k=1 , we fix  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2  all throughout this appendix. 
 
 
 We use the notation employed in Section  5 .
Recall from the proof of Proposition  5.1  that  p t ε ​ ( ⋅ , ⋅ ) p_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\cdot)  denotes the transition kernel of the  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} -sped up  SIP ⁡ ( G , ε ​ α ^ ) \rm SIP(G,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha})  with  k k  particles, i.e., 
 

 
 (A.1) 
 
 p t ε ​ ( η , ξ ) = e t ​ ε − 1 ​ L ε ​ α ^ , k ​ 𝟙 ξ ​ ( η ) ​ , η , ξ ∈ Ξ k ​ . p_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\eta,\xi)=e^{t\varepsilon^{-1}L_{\varepsilon\hat{\alpha},k}}\mathds{1}_{\xi}(\eta)\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad\eta,\xi\in\Xi_{k}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 The following proposition is the main result of this appendix. 
 
 
 Proposition A.1 . 
 
 For any configuration  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k}  and time  t > 0 t>0 , we have 
 

 
 (A.2) 
 
 lim ε → 0 p t ε ​ ( η , Ω k ) = 1 ​ . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}p_{t}^{\varepsilon}(\eta,\Omega_{k})=1\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 To prove Proposition  A.1 , we need a series of technical lemmas. Let  ( η ε ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\eta^{\varepsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0} 
be the  ε − 1 \varepsilon^{-1} -sped up  SIP \rm SIP  with generator  ε − 1 ​ L ε ​ α ^ , k \varepsilon^{-1}L_{\varepsilon\hat{\alpha},k} . Let  ℙ η ε \mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}  and  𝔼 η ε \mathds{E}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}  denote the law and corresponding expectation of  ( η ε ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\eta^{\varepsilon}(t))_{t\geq 0}  starting from  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k} . 
 
 
 The first lemma states that the time to reach the absorbing set  Ω k \Omega_{k}  from the transient collection  Δ k \Delta_{k}  (defined in ( 5.5 )) is uniformly negligible. Given a subset  A A  of  Ξ k \Xi_{k} , let  τ A \tau_{A}  be its (random) first hitting time. Moreover, define 
 

 
 (A.3) 
 
 c min = min x , y ∈ V c x ​ y > 0 ⁡ c x ​ y > 0 ​ , c max = max x , y ∈ V ⁡ c x ​ y ​ . c_{\rm min}=\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}x,y\in V\\
c_{xy}>0\end{subarray}}c_{xy}>0\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad c_{\rm max}=\max_{x,y\in V}c_{xy}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 Lemma A.2 . 
 
 Suppose that  ε ​ | α ^ | ≤ c min 4 ​ c max ​ k \varepsilon|\hat{\alpha}|\leq\frac{c_{\rm min}}{4\,c_{\rm max}\,k} . Then, for some  C 1 = C 1 ​ ( G ) > 0 C_{1}=C_{1}(G)>0  and for all  ε > 0 \varepsilon>0 ,  k ≥ 2 k\geq 2 , 
 

 
 (A.4) 
 
 max ζ ∈ Δ k ⁡ 𝔼 ζ ε ​ [ τ Ω k ] ≤ C 1 ​ ε c min ​ log ⁡ k ​ . \max_{\zeta\in\Delta_{k}}\mathds{E}_{\zeta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{\Omega_{k}}]\leq\frac{{C_{1}}\varepsilon}{c_{\rm min}}\,\log k\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 Define a test function  ϕ : Ξ k → ℝ \phi:\Xi_{k}\to\mathbb{R}  as 
 

 
 (A.5) 
 
 ϕ ( η ) ≔ − ∑ x ∈ V ( 1 + 1 2 + ⋯ + 1 η x ) . \phi(\eta)\coloneqq-\sum_{x\in V}\left(1+\frac{1}{2}+\cdots+\frac{1}{\eta_{x}}\right)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Then, there exists  C 1 = C 1 ​ ( G ) > 0 C_{1}=C_{1}(G)>0  such that 
 

 
 (A.6) 
 
 max η ∈ Ξ k ⁡ ϕ ⁡ ( η ) − min η ∈ Ξ k ⁡ ϕ ⁡ ( η ) ≤ C 1 4 ​ log ⁡ k ​ . \max_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\phi(\eta)-\min_{\eta\in\Xi_{k}}\phi(\eta)\leq\frac{{C_{1}}}{4}\log k\;\text{.} 
 
 
 For each  ζ ∈ Δ k \zeta\in\Delta_{k} , we may express  L ε ​ α ^ , k ​ ϕ ​ ( ζ ) L_{\varepsilon\hat{\alpha},k}\phi(\zeta)  as 
 

 
 (A.7) 
 
 ∑ { x , y } ⊆ V c x ​ y ​ [ ζ x ​ ( ε ​ α ^ y + ζ y ) ​ ( ϕ ⁡ ( ζ − δ x + δ y ) − ϕ ⁡ ( ζ ) ) + ζ y ​ ( ε ​ α ^ x + η x ) ​ ( ϕ ⁡ ( ζ − δ y + δ x ) − ϕ ⁡ ( ζ ) ) ] ​ . \sum_{\{x,y\}\subseteq V}c_{xy}\left[\zeta_{x}\,(\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{y}+\zeta_{y})\,(\phi(\zeta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y})-\phi(\zeta))+\zeta_{y}\,(\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{x}+\eta_{x})\,(\phi(\zeta-\delta_{y}+\delta_{x})-\phi(\zeta))\right]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 If  ζ x , ζ y ≥ 1 \zeta_{x},\zeta_{y}\geq 1 , then the term inside the bracket in ( A.7 ) becomes 
 

 
 (A.8) 
 
 ζ x ζ y + 1 + ζ y ζ x + 1 + ε ​ α ^ y ​ ( 1 − ζ x ζ y + 1 ) + ε ​ α ^ x ​ ( 1 − ζ y ζ x + 1 ) ≥ ( 1 − ε ​ α ^ y ) ​ ζ x ζ y + 1 + ( 1 − ε ​ α ^ x ) ​ ζ y ζ x + 1 ​ . \frac{\zeta_{x}}{\zeta_{y}+1}+\frac{\zeta_{y}}{\zeta_{x}+1}+\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{y}\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{x}}{\zeta_{y}+1}\right)+\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{x}\left(1-\frac{\zeta_{y}}{\zeta_{x}+1}\right)\geq\frac{(1-\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{y})\,\zeta_{x}}{\zeta_{y}+1}+\frac{(1-\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{x})\,\zeta_{y}}{\zeta_{x}+1}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By the assumption, we have  ε ​ α ^ max ≤ ε ​ | α ^ | ≤ c min 4 ​ c max ​ k < 1 2 \varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{\rm max}\leq\varepsilon|\hat{\alpha}|\leq\frac{c_{\rm min}}{4\,c_{\rm max}\,k}<\frac{1}{2} . Thus, the right-hand side is bounded from below by 
 

 
 (A.9) 
 
 1 2 ​ ( ζ x ζ y + 1 + ζ y ζ x + 1 ) ≥ 1 2 ​ , \frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\zeta_{x}}{\zeta_{y}+1}+\frac{\zeta_{y}}{\zeta_{x}+1}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the inequality holds because  ζ x , ζ y ≥ 1 \zeta_{x},\zeta_{y}\geq 1 . Next, if  ζ x ≥ 1 \zeta_{x}\geq 1  and  ζ y = 0 \zeta_{y}=0 , then the term inside the bracket in ( A.7 ) equals 
 

 
 (A.10) 
 
 ζ x ​ ε ​ α ^ y ​ ( 1 ζ x − 1 ) = − ε ​ α ^ y ​ ( ζ x − 1 ) ≥ − ε ​ α ^ y ​ ζ x ​ . \zeta_{x}\,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{y}\left(\frac{1}{\zeta_{x}}-1\right)=-\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{y}\,(\zeta_{x}-1)\geq-\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{y}\,\zeta_{x}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Similarly, if  ζ x = 0 \zeta_{x}=0  and  ζ y ≥ 1 \zeta_{y}\geq 1 , then the term is at least  − ε ​ α ^ x ​ ζ y -\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{x}\,\zeta_{y} . As  ζ ∈ Δ k \zeta\in\Delta_{k} , there exists at least one  { x ∗ , y ∗ } \{x^{*},y^{*}\}  such that  c x ∗ ​ y ∗ > 0 c_{x^{*}y^{*}}>0  and  ζ x ∗ , ζ y ∗ ≥ 1 \zeta_{x^{*}},\zeta_{y^{*}}\geq 1 . Thus, by ( A.3 ), ( A.7 ), ( A.9 ), and ( A.10 ), 
 

 
 (A.11) 
 
 L ε ​ α ^ , k ​ ϕ ​ ( ζ ) ≥ c x ∗ ​ y ∗ 2 − ∑ x ∈ V ζ x ≥ 1 ∑ y ∈ V ζ y = 0 c x ​ y ​ ε ​ α ^ y ​ ζ x ≥ c min 2 − c max ​ k ​ ε ​ | α ^ | ≥ c min 4 ​ , L_{\varepsilon\hat{\alpha},k}\phi(\zeta)\geq\frac{c_{x^{*}y^{*}}}{2}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}x\in V\\
\zeta_{x}\geq 1\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}y\in V\\
\zeta_{y}=0\end{subarray}}c_{xy}\,\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{y}\,\zeta_{x}\geq\frac{c_{\rm min}}{2}-c_{\rm max}\,k\,\varepsilon|\hat{\alpha}|\geq\frac{c_{\rm min}}{4}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where the last inequality holds since  ε ​ | α ^ | ≤ c min 4 ​ c max ​ k \varepsilon|\hat{\alpha}|\leq\frac{c_{\rm min}}{4\,c_{\rm max}\,k} . 
 
 
 Now, it is well known that 
 

 
 (A.12) 
 
 ϕ ⁡ ( η ε ​ ( t ) ) − ϕ ⁡ ( η ε ​ ( 0 ) ) − ∫ 0 t ( ε − 1 ​ L ε ​ α ^ , k ​ ϕ ) ​ ( η ε ​ ( s ) ) ​ 𝑑 s ​ , t ≥ 0 ​ , \phi(\eta^{\varepsilon}(t))-\phi(\eta^{\varepsilon}(0))-\int_{0}^{t}(\varepsilon^{-1}L_{\varepsilon\hat{\alpha},k}\phi)(\eta^{\varepsilon}(s))\,{\rm d}s\;\text{,}\;\;\quad t\geq 0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 is a  ℙ ζ ε \mathds{P}_{\zeta}^{\varepsilon} -martingale. Setting  t = τ Ω k ∧ r t=\tau_{\Omega_{k}}\wedge r , applying ( A.6 ) and ( A.11 ), taking expectations with respect to  𝔼 ζ ε \mathds{E}_{\zeta}^{\varepsilon} , and then sending  r → ∞ r\to\infty , we obtain 
 

 
 (A.13) 
 
 c min ​ ε − 1 4 ​ 𝔼 ζ ε ​ [ τ Ω k ] ≤ 𝔼 ζ ε ​ [ ∫ 0 τ Ω k ( ε − 1 ​ L ε ​ α ^ , k ​ ϕ ) ​ ( η ε ​ ( s ) ) ​ 𝑑 s ] ≤ C 1 4 ​ log ⁡ k ​ . \frac{c_{\rm min}\,\varepsilon^{-1}}{4}\,\mathds{E}_{\zeta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{\Omega_{k}}]\leq\mathds{E}_{\zeta}^{\varepsilon}\left[\int_{0}^{\tau_{\Omega_{k}}}(\varepsilon^{-1}L_{\varepsilon\hat{\alpha},k}\phi)(\eta^{\varepsilon}(s))\,{\rm d}s\right]\leq\frac{{C_{1}}}{4}\log k\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎ 
 
 
 
 For each  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0 , let  T ε ​ ( t ) T_{\varepsilon}(t)  be the local time spent by  ( η ε ​ ( s ) ) s ≥ 0 (\eta^{\varepsilon}(s))_{s\geq 0}  in  Ω k \Omega_{k}  up to time  t t : 
 

 
 (A.14) 
 
 T ε ( t ) ≔ ∫ 0 t 𝟏 { η ε ( s ) ∈ Ω k } d s . T_{\varepsilon}(t)\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}{\bf 1}\{\eta^{\varepsilon}(s)\in\Omega_{k}\}\,{\rm d}s\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Then, let  S ε ​ ( s ) S_{\varepsilon}(s) ,  s ≥ 0 s\geq 0 , be its generalized inverse: 
 

 
 (A.15) 
 
 S ε ​ ( s ) ≔ sup { t ≥ 0 : T ε ​ ( t ) ≤ s } ​ . S_{\varepsilon}(s)\coloneqq\sup\,\{t\geq 0:T_{\varepsilon}(t)\leq s\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By definition,  T ε ​ ( S ε ​ ( s ) ) = s T_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon}(s))=s  and  T ε ​ ( S ε ​ ( s ) + u ) > s T_{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon}(s)+u)>s  for any  s ≥ 0 s\geq 0  and  u > 0 u>0 . In turn, we have 
 

 
 (A.16) 
 
 η ε ​ ( S ε ​ ( s ) ) ∈ Ω k ​ , s ≥ 0 ​ . \eta^{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon}(s))\in\Omega_{k}\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad s\geq 0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Note also that  { S ε ( s ) ≥ s + u } = { T ε ( s + u ) ≤ s } \{S_{\varepsilon}(s)\geq s+u\}=\{T_{\varepsilon}(s+u)\leq s\}  for  s ≥ 0 s\geq 0  and  u > 0 u>0 . 
 
 
 Lemma A.3 . 
 
 For all  η ∈ Ξ k \eta\in\Xi_{k} ,  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0  and  δ > 0 \delta>0 , we have 
 

 
 (A.17) 
 
 lim ε → 0 ℙ η ε [ S ε ( t ) ≥ t + δ ] = 0 . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[S_{\varepsilon}(t)\geq t+\delta]=0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 First, suppose that  η ∈ Δ k \eta\in\Delta_{k} . We may bound 
 

 
 (A.18) 
 
 ℙ η ε [ S ε ( t ) ≥ t + δ ] \displaystyle\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[S_{\varepsilon}(t)\geq t+\delta] 
 = ℙ η ε [ T ε ( t + δ ) ≤ t ] \displaystyle=\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[T_{\varepsilon}(t+\delta)\leq t] 
 
 
 (A.19) 
 
 
 ≤ ℙ η ε [ T ε ( t + δ ) ≤ t , τ Ω k < δ / 2 ] + ℙ η ε [ τ Ω k ≥ δ / 2 ] . \displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[T_{\varepsilon}(t+\delta)\leq t,\ \tau_{\Omega_{k}}<\delta/2]+\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{\Omega_{k}}\geq\delta/2]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By Lemma  A.2  and the Markov inequality, we obtain 
 

 
 (A.20) 
 
 ℙ η ε [ τ Ω k ≥ δ / 2 ] ≤ 2 δ 𝔼 η ε [ τ Ω k ] ≤ 2 δ C 1 ​ ε c min log k → ε → 0 0 . \mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{\Omega_{k}}\geq\delta/2]\leq\frac{2}{\delta}\,\mathds{E}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{\Omega_{k}}]\leq\frac{2}{\delta}\,\frac{{C_{1}}\varepsilon}{c_{\rm min}}\,\log k\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, by the strong Markov property of  ( η ε ​ ( s ) ) s ≥ 0 (\eta^{\varepsilon}(s))_{s\geq 0}  and the fact that  t ↦ T ε ​ ( t ) t\mapsto T_{\varepsilon}(t)  is non-decreasing, we deduce 
 

 
 (A.21) 
 
 lim sup ε → 0 ℙ η ε [ S ε ( t ) ≥ t + δ ] ≤ lim sup ε → 0 sup ξ ∈ Ω k ℙ ξ ε [ T ε ( t + δ / 2 ) ≤ t ] . \limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[S_{\varepsilon}(t)\geq t+\delta]\leq\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\xi\in\Omega_{k}}\mathds{P}_{\xi}^{\varepsilon}\,[T_{\varepsilon}(t+\delta/2)\leq t]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Thus, it suffices to prove Lemma  A.3  for  η ∈ Ω k \eta\in\Omega_{k} . 
 
 
 It remains to prove that, for  η ∈ Ω k \eta\in\Omega_{k} ,  t ≥ 0 t\geq 0  and  δ > 0 \delta>0 , 
 

 
 (A.22) 
 
 lim ε → 0 ℙ η ε [ T ε ( t + δ ) ≤ t ] = 0 . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[T_{\varepsilon}(t+\delta)\leq t]=0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Let  0 < τ 1 < τ 2 < ⋯ 0<\tau_{1}<\tau_{2}<\cdots  be the consecutive jump times from  Ω k \Omega_{k}  to  Δ k \Delta_{k} . Then, for any integer  m ≥ 1 m\geq 1 , we get 
 

 
 (A.23) 
 
 ℙ η ε [ T ε ( t + δ ) ≤ t ] ≤ ℙ η ε [ T ε ( t + δ ) ≤ t , τ m + 1 > t + δ ] + ℙ η ε [ τ m + 1 ≤ t + δ ] . \mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[T_{\varepsilon}(t+\delta)\leq t]\leq\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[T_{\varepsilon}(t+\delta)\leq t,\ \tau_{m+1}>t+\delta]+\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{m+1}\leq t+\delta]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Conditioned on the first event in the right-hand side of ( A.23 ), there occurs at most  m m  jumps from  Ω k \Omega_{k}  to  Δ k \Delta_{k}  up to time  t + δ t+\delta . Let  τ 1 ′ < τ 2 ′ < ⋯ \tau_{1}^{\prime}<\tau_{2}^{\prime}<\cdots  denote the consecutive return times to  Ω k \Omega_{k} , such that we have 
 

 
 (A.24) 
 
 0 < τ 1 < τ 1 ′ < τ 2 < τ 2 ′ < ⋯ . 0<\tau_{1}<\tau_{1}^{\prime}<\tau_{2}<\tau_{2}^{\prime}<\cdots\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Then, by ( A.14 ), we may bound the first probability in the right-hand side of ( A.23 ) by 
 

 
 (A.25) 
 
 ℙ η ε [ ( τ 1 ′ − τ 1 ) + ( τ 2 ′ − τ 2 ) + ⋯ + ( τ m ′ − τ m ) ≥ δ ] . \mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[(\tau_{1}^{\prime}-\tau_{1})+(\tau_{2}^{\prime}-\tau_{2})+\cdots+(\tau_{m}^{\prime}-\tau_{m})\geq\delta]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 We may further bound this by 
 
 
 
 (A.26) 
 
 ∑ i = 1 m ℙ η ε [ τ i ′ − τ i ≥ δ / m ] \displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}[\tau_{i}^{\prime}-\tau_{i}\geq\delta/m] 
 ≤ m sup ζ ∈ Δ k ℙ ζ ε [ τ Ω k ≥ δ / m ] \displaystyle\leq m\sup_{\zeta\in\Delta_{k}}\mathds{P}_{\zeta}^{\varepsilon}[\tau_{\Omega_{k}}\geq\delta/m] 
 
 
 
 
 ≤ m 2 δ ​ sup ζ ∈ Δ k 𝔼 ζ ε ​ [ τ Ω k ] ≤ m 2 ​ C 1 ​ ε ​ log ⁡ k δ ​ c min → ε → 0 0 ​ . \displaystyle\leq\frac{m^{2}}{\delta}\sup_{\zeta\in\Delta_{k}}\mathds{E}_{\zeta}^{\varepsilon}[\tau_{\Omega_{k}}]\leq\frac{m^{2}{C_{1}}\varepsilon\log k}{\delta c_{\rm min}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon\to 0}0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Here, the first inequality is due to the strong Markov property at  τ i \tau_{i}  for each  i ∈ ⟦ 1 , m ⟧ i\in\llbracket 1,m\rrbracket , the second inequality is due to the Markov inequality, and the third inequality is due to Lemma  A.2 .
Further, for  ( η ε ​ ( s ) ) s ≥ 0 (\eta^{\varepsilon}(s))_{s\geq 0} , any direct jump from  Ω k \Omega_{k}  to  Δ k \Delta_{k}  has rate bounded above by 
 

 
 (A.27) 
 
 sup η ∈ Ω k sup x , y ∈ V ε − 1 ​ c x ​ y ​ η x ​ ε ​ α ^ y ≤ c max ​ k ​ α ^ max ​ , \sup_{\eta\in\Omega_{k}}\sup_{x,y\in V}\varepsilon^{-1}\,c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\varepsilon\hat{\alpha}_{y}\leq c_{\rm max}\,k\,\hat{\alpha}_{\rm max}\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  α ^ max ≔ max x ∈ V ⁡ α ^ x \hat{\alpha}_{\rm max}\coloneqq\max_{x\in V}\hat{\alpha}_{x} . Hence, each of the random variables  τ 1 , τ 2 − τ 1 ′ , … , τ m + 1 − τ m ′ \tau_{1},\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\tau_{m+1}-\tau_{m}^{\prime}  stochastically dominates an exponential random variable with rate  c max ​ k ​ α ^ max c_{\rm max}\,k\,\hat{\alpha}_{\rm max} . Thus, letting  τ 1 ∗ , τ 2 ∗ , … \tau_{1}^{*},\tau_{2}^{*},\dots  be some i.i.d. exponentials of rate  c max ​ k ​ α ^ max c_{\rm max}\,k\,\hat{\alpha}_{\rm max} , by Markovianity we get 
 
 
 
 (A.28) 
 
 ℙ η ε [ τ m + 1 ≤ t + δ ] \displaystyle\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{m+1}\leq t+\delta] 
 ≤ ℙ [ τ 1 ∗ + ⋯ + τ m + 1 ∗ ≤ t + δ ] \displaystyle\leq\mathds{P}\,[\tau_{1}^{*}+\cdots+\tau_{m+1}^{*}\leq t+\delta] 
 
 
 
 
 = ℙ [ Gamma ( m + 1 , c max k α ^ max ) ≤ t + δ ] → m → ∞ 0 , \displaystyle=\mathds{P}\,[{\rm Gamma}\,(m+1,c_{\rm max}\,k\,\hat{\alpha}_{\rm max})\leq t+\delta]\xrightarrow{m\to\infty}0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 where  Gamma ⁡ ( u , β ) {\rm Gamma}\,(u,\beta)  denotes the Gamma distribution with shape  u > 0 u>0  and rate  β > 0 \beta>0 . Therefore, by ( A.23 ), ( A.26 ) and ( A.28 ), we conclude that 
 

 
 (A.29) 
 
 lim sup ε → 0 ℙ η ε [ T ε ( t + δ ) ≤ t ] \displaystyle\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[T_{\varepsilon}(t+\delta)\leq t] 
 ≤ lim sup m → ∞ lim sup ε → 0 m 2 ​ C 1 ​ ε ​ log ⁡ k δ ​ c min \displaystyle\leq\limsup_{m\to\infty}\limsup_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{m^{2}\,{C_{1}}\,\varepsilon\,\log k}{\delta\,c_{\rm min}} 
 
 
 (A.30) 
 
 
 + lim sup m → ∞ ℙ [ Gamma ( m + 1 , c max k α ^ max ) ≤ t + δ ] = 0 , \displaystyle+\limsup_{m\to\infty}\mathds{P}\,[{\rm Gamma}\,(m+1,c_{\rm max}\,k\,\hat{\alpha}_{\rm max})\leq t+\delta]=0\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 as desired.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Lemma A.4 . 
 
 We have 
 

 
 (A.31) 
 
 lim δ → 0 lim ε → 0 sup ξ ∈ Ω k ℙ ξ ε [ τ Δ k < 3 δ ] = 0 . \lim_{\delta\to 0}\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\sup_{\xi\in\Omega_{k}}\mathds{P}_{\xi}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{\Delta_{k}}<3\delta]=0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 
 
 
 Proof. 
 
 As explained in the proof of Lemma  A.3 , starting from any  ξ ∈ Ω k \xi\in\Omega_{k} ,  τ Δ k \tau_{\Delta_{k}}  stochastically dominates an exponential random variable of rate  c max ​ k ​ α ^ max c_{\rm max}\,k\,\hat{\alpha}_{\rm max} . Thus, we get 
 

 
 (A.32) 
 
 lim sup δ → 0 sup ε ∈ ( 0 , 1 ) ℙ ξ ε [ τ Δ k < 3 δ ] ≤ lim sup δ → 0 ( 1 − e − c max ​ k ​ α ^ max ​  3 ​ δ ) = 0 . \limsup_{\delta\to 0}\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1)}\mathds{P}_{\xi}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{\Delta_{k}}<3\delta]\leq\limsup_{\delta\to 0}\left(1-e^{-c_{\rm max}\,k\,\hat{\alpha}_{\rm max}\,3\delta}\right)=0\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This concludes the proof.
∎ 
 
 
 
 Now, we are ready to prove Proposition  A.1 . 
 
 
 Proof of Proposition  A.1 . 
 
 We prove 
 

 
 (A.33) 
 
 lim ε → 0 ℙ η ε [ η ε ( t ) ∈ Ω k ] = 1 , t > 0 , η ∈ Ξ k . \lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\eta^{\varepsilon}(t)\in\Omega_{k}]=1\;\text{,}\;\;\qquad t>0\;\text{,}\;\;\eta\in\Xi_{k}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 We follow the proof ideas of  [ LLM18 , Lemma 3.1] . By ( A.16 ), for  δ ∈ ( 0 , t / 3 ) \delta\in(0,t/3) , we estimate 
 

 
 (A.34) 
 
 1 = ℙ η ε [ η ε ( S ε ( t − 3 δ ) ) ∈ Ω k ] ≤ \displaystyle 1=\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\eta^{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon}(t-3\delta))\in\Omega_{k}]\leq 
 ℙ η ε ​ [ η ε ​ ( S ε ​ ( t − 3 ​ δ ) ) ∈ Ω k , S ε ​ ( t − 3 ​ δ ) ∈ [ t − 3 ​ δ , t − 2 ​ δ ] ] \displaystyle\ \mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\left[\eta^{\varepsilon}(S_{\varepsilon}(t-3\delta))\in\Omega_{k},\ S_{\varepsilon}(t-3\delta)\in[t-3\delta,t-2\delta]\right] 
 
 
 (A.35) 
 
 
 + ℙ η ε [ S ε ( t − 3 δ ) > t − 2 δ ] . \displaystyle+\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[S_{\varepsilon}(t-3\delta)>t-2\delta]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 By Lemma  A.3 , the second probability in the right-hand side vanishes as  ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 . Moreover, by ( A.16 ), the first probability in the right-hand side is bounded by 
 

 
 (A.36) 
 
 ℙ η ε [ ∃ s ∈ [ t − 3 δ , t − 2 δ ] , η ε ( s ) ∈ Ω k ] . \mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\,\exists s\in[t-3\delta,t-2\delta],\ \eta^{\varepsilon}(s)\in\Omega_{k}\,]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 We can further divide this into two events and bound the last probability as 
 

 
 (A.37) 
 
 ℙ η ε [ η ε ( t ) ∈ Ω k ] + ℙ η ε [ ∃ s ∈ [ t − 3 δ , t − 2 δ ] , η ε ( s ) ∈ Ω k , η ε ( t ) ∈ Δ k ] . \mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\eta^{\varepsilon}(t)\in\Omega_{k}]+\mathds{P}_{\eta}^{\varepsilon}\,[\,\exists s\in[t-3\delta,t-2\delta],\ \eta^{\varepsilon}(s)\in\Omega_{k},\ \eta^{\varepsilon}(t)\in\Delta_{k}\,]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Then, by the Markov property, the second probability above is bounded by 
 

 
 (A.38) 
 
 sup ξ ∈ Ω k ℙ ξ ε [ τ Δ k < 3 δ ] , \sup_{\xi\in\Omega_{k}}\mathds{P}_{\xi}^{\varepsilon}\,[\tau_{\Delta_{k}}<3\delta]\;\text{,}\;\; 
 
 
 which, by Lemma  A.4 , vanishes by first taking the limit  ε → 0 \varepsilon\to 0 , and then  δ → 0 \delta\to 0 .
Collecting all displayed inequalities, we conclude that ( A.33 ) holds.
∎ 
 
 
 
 
 Appendix B  Reversibility of the limit process 

 
 In this appendix, we prove Lemma  5.9  and simply write  ς α ^ , k = ς α ^ , k , m \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}=\varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k,m} ,  k ≥ m k\geq m . 
 
 
 Proof of Lemma  5.9 . 
 
 We start by introducing an auxiliary continuous-time Markov chain on  Ξ k \Xi_{k}  with rate function (cf. ( 5.1 )–( 5.5 )) 
 

 
 (B.1) 
 
 𝐬 ⁡ ( η , ⋅ ) ≔ { 𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ​ ( η , ⋅ ) if ​ η ∈ Ω k ​ , 𝐫 k ℬ ​ ( η , ⋅ ) if ​ η ∈ Δ k ​ . {\bf s}(\eta,\,\cdot\,)\coloneqq\begin{cases}{\bf r}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta,\,\cdot\,)&\text{if}\ \eta\in\Omega_{k}\;\text{,}\\
{\bf r}^{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\eta,\,\cdot\,)&\text{if}\ \eta\in\Delta_{k}\;\text{.}\end{cases} 
 
 
 In words, the above dynamics follows the rule of  A α ^ , k A_{\hat{\alpha},k}  in  Ω k \Omega_{k} , while that of  B k B_{k}  in  Δ k \Delta_{k} . (Remark that this is not a  SIP {\rm SIP} -dynamics.)
Now, recall the definition of  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  from ( 5.17 ) and, similarly, introduce the set 
 

 
 (B.2) 
 
 Δ k , m ≔ { ζ ∈ Δ k : | { x ∈ V : ζ x > 0 } | = m } ​ . \Delta_{k,m}\coloneqq\left\{\zeta\in\Delta_{k}:|\{x\in V:\zeta_{x}>0\}|=m\right\}\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Observe that this auxiliary chain can jump from  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  to either  Δ k , m \Delta_{k,m}  or  Δ k , m + 1 \Delta_{k,m+1}  (if  m ≤ k − 1 m\leq k-1 ), but a jump from  Ω k , m \Omega_{k,m}  to  Δ k , m \Delta_{k,m}  has rate zero to backtrack. 
 
 
 Now, fix  m ≤ k − 1 m\leq k-1 . We claim that this dynamics, restricted to  Ω k , m ∪ Δ k , m + 1 \Omega_{k,m}\cup\Delta_{k,m+1} , is reversible with respect to  ς α ^ , k \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k} .
For this purpose, it suffices to consider pairs  η , ξ = η − δ x + δ y ∈ Ω k , m ∪ Δ k , m + 1 \eta,\xi=\eta-\delta_{x}+\delta_{y}\in\Omega_{k,m}\cup\Delta_{k,m+1}  (hence,  η x ≥ 1 \eta_{x}\geq 1 ); rates are zero otherwise. If  η ∈ Ω k , m \eta\in\Omega_{k,m}  and  ξ ∈ Ω k , m \xi\in\Omega_{k,m}  (such that  η x = 1 \eta_{x}=1 ), then 
 

 
 (B.3) 
 
 ς α ^ , k ​ ( η ) ​ 𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ​ ( η , ξ ) = c x ​ y ​ η x ​ α ^ y ​ ∏ z ∈ V η z > 0 α ^ z η z = c x ​ y ​ α ^ x ​ ξ y ​ ∏ z ∈ V ξ z > 0 α ^ z ξ z = ς α ^ , k ​ ( ξ ) ​ 𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ​ ( ξ , η ) ​ . \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta)\,{\bf r}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta,\xi)=c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\hat{\alpha}_{y}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in V\\
\eta_{z}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{z}}{\eta_{z}}=c_{xy}\,\hat{\alpha}_{x}\xi_{y}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in V\\
\xi_{z}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{z}}{\xi_{z}}=\varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi)\,{\bf r}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi,\eta)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 If  η ∈ Ω k , m \eta\in\Omega_{k,m}  and  ξ ∈ Δ k , m + 1 \xi\in\Delta_{k,m+1}  (such that  η x ≥ 2 \eta_{x}\geq 2 ), we have 
 

 
 (B.4) 
 
 ς α ^ , k ​ ( η ) ​ 𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ​ ( η , ξ ) = c x ​ y ​ η x ​ α ^ y ​ ∏ z ∈ V η z > 0 α ^ z η z = c x ​ y ​ ξ x ​ ξ y ​ ∏ z ∈ V ξ z > 0 α ^ z ξ z = ς α ^ , k ​ ( ξ ) ​ 𝐫 k ℬ ​ ( ξ , η ) ​ . \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta)\,{\bf r}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta,\xi)=c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\hat{\alpha}_{y}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in V\\
\eta_{z}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{z}}{\eta_{z}}=c_{xy}\,\xi_{x}\xi_{y}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in V\\
\xi_{z}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{z}}{\xi_{z}}=\varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi)\,{\bf r}^{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\xi,\eta)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 If  η , ξ ∈ Δ k , m + 1 \eta,\xi\in\Delta_{k,m+1} , we have 
 

 
 (B.5) 
 
 ς α ^ , k ​ ( η ) ​ 𝐫 k ℬ ​ ( η , ξ ) = c x ​ y ​ η x ​ η y ​ ∏ z ∈ V η z > 0 α ^ z η z = c x ​ y ​ ξ x ​ ξ y ​ ∏ z ∈ V ξ z > 0 α ^ z ξ z = ς α ^ , k ​ ( ξ ) ​ 𝐫 k ℬ ​ ( ξ , η ) ​ . \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta)\,{\bf r}^{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\eta,\xi)=c_{xy}\,\eta_{x}\eta_{y}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in V\\
\eta_{z}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{z}}{\eta_{z}}=c_{xy}\,\xi_{x}\xi_{y}\,\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in V\\
\xi_{z}>0\end{subarray}}\frac{\hat{\alpha}_{z}}{\xi_{z}}=\varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi)\,{\bf r}^{\mathcal{B}}_{k}(\xi,\eta)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This shows that  ς α ^ , k \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}  is reversible for the Markov chain described in ( B.1 ), restricted to  Ω k , m ∪ Δ k , m + 1 \Omega_{k,m}\cup\Delta_{k,m+1} .
Furthermore, by ( 5.15 ), we have, for all distinct  η , ξ ∈ Ω k , m \eta,\xi\in\Omega_{k,m} , 
 

 
 (B.6) 
 
 ς α ^ , k ( η ) 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ( η , ξ ) = ς α ^ , k ( η ) ∑ ζ ∈ Δ k , m + 1 𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ( η , ζ ) 𝐏 ζ ℬ [ τ ξ = τ Ω k ] . \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta)\,{\bf r}^{\mathcal{M}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta,\xi)=\varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta)\sum_{\zeta\in\Delta_{k,m+1}}{\bf r}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\eta,\zeta)\,{\bf P}_{\zeta}^{\mathcal{B}}\,[\tau_{\xi}=\tau_{\Omega_{k}}]\;\text{.} 
 
 
 Following  ( ℬ k ​ ( t ) ) t ≥ 0 (\mathcal{B}_{k}(t))_{t\geq 0} , from  ζ ∈ Δ k , m + 1 \zeta\in\Delta_{k,m+1}  to arrive at  ξ ∈ Ω k , m \xi\in\Omega_{k,m} , the trajectory must stay in  Δ k , m + 1 \Delta_{k,m+1} . Thus, by reversibility, the right-hand side is equal to 
 

 
 (B.7) 
 
 ς α ^ , k ( ξ ) ∑ ζ ∈ Δ k , m + 1 𝐫 α ^ , k 𝒜 ( ξ , ζ ) 𝐏 ζ ℬ [ τ η = τ Ω k ] = ς α ^ , k ( ξ ) 𝐫 α ^ , k ℳ ( ξ , η ) . \varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi)\sum_{\zeta\in\Delta_{k,m+1}}{\bf r}^{\mathcal{A}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi,\zeta)\,{\bf P}_{\zeta}^{\mathcal{B}}\,[\tau_{\eta}=\tau_{\Omega_{k}}]=\varsigma_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi)\,{\bf r}^{\mathcal{M}}_{\hat{\alpha},k}(\xi,\eta)\;\text{.} 
 
 
 This concludes the proof of the lemma.
∎

as a quick reference; all objects are defined in detail at the indicated locations.

     Instructions for reporting errors 
     We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
      support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
      methods listed below: 
     
       Click the "Report Issue"  ( 
             
           )  button, located in the page header. 
     
     Tip:  You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report. 
     Our team has already identified  the following issues . We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
      may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
      should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
      all. 
     Have a free development cycle? Help support accessibility at arXiv! Our collaborators at LaTeXML maintain a  list of packages that need conversion , and welcome  developer contributions .
